Balanced Allocations with the Choice of Noise
Dimitrios Los, Thomas Sauerwald

Abstract
We consider the allocation of m m balls (jobs) into n n bins (servers). In the standard Two-Choice process, at each step t = 1 , 2 , … , m t=1,2,\ldots,m we first sample two randomly chosen bins, compare their two loads and then place a ball in the least loaded bin. It is well-known that for any m ⩾ n m\geqslant n , this results in a gap (difference between the maximum and average load) of log 2 ⁡ log ⁡ n + Θ ⁡ ( 1 ) \log_{2}\log n+\Theta(1) (with high probability). In this work, we consider Two-Choice in different settings with noisy load comparisons. One key setting involves an adaptive adversary whose power is limited by some threshold g ∈ ℕ g\in\mathbb{N} . In each step, such adversary can determine the result of any load comparison between two bins whose loads differ by at most g g , while if the load difference is greater than g g , the comparison is correct. For this adversarial setting, we first prove that for any m ⩾ n m\geqslant n the gap is 𝒪 ⁡ ( g + log ⁡ n ) \mathcal{O}(g+\log n) with high probability. Then through a refined analysis we prove that if g ⩽ log ⁡ n g\leqslant\log n , then for any m ⩾ n m\geqslant n the gap is 𝒪 ⁡ ( g log ⁡ g ⋅ log ⁡ log ​ n ) \mathcal{O}\big(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\big) . For constant values of g g , this generalizes the heavily loaded analysis of [ 16 , 49 ] for the Two-Choice process, and establishes that asymptotically the same gap bound holds even if load comparisons among “similarly loaded” bins are wrong. Finally, we complement these upper bounds with tight lower bounds, which establish an interesting phase transition on how the parameter g g impacts the gap. The analysis also applies to settings with outdated and delayed information. For example, for the setting of [ 15 ] where balls are allocated in consecutive batches of size b = n b=n , we present an improved and tight gap bound of Θ ⁡ ( log ⁡ n log ⁡ log ⁡ n ) \Theta\big(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\big) . This bound also extends for a range of values of b b and applies to a relaxed setting where the reported load of a bin can be any load value from the last b b steps.



1
Introduction

Motivation

In this work we examine balls-and-bins processes where the goal is to allocate
m
balls (jobs or tasks) sequentially into
n
bins (processors or servers). The balls-and-bins framework a.k.a. balanced allocations
[
8
]
is a popular abstraction for various resource allocation and storage problems such as load balancing, scheduling or hashing (see surveys
[
42
,
51
]
). In order to allocate the balls in an efficient and decentralized way, randomized strategies are usually employed which are based on
sampling a number of bins for each ball, and then allocating the ball into one of those bins.

It is well-known that if each ball is placed in a bin chosen independently and uniformly at random (called
One-Choice
), then the maximum load is
\Theta(\log n/\log\log n)
w.h.p.
1
1

1

In general, with high probability refers to probability of at least
1-n^{-c}
for some constant
c>0
.
for
m=n
, and
m/n+\Theta(\sqrt{(m/n)\log n})
w.h.p. for
m\geqslant n\log n
.
Azar, Broder, Karlin and Upfal
[
8
]
(and implicitly Karp, Luby and Meyer auf der Heide
[
31
]
) proved the remarkable result that if each ball is placed in the lesser loaded of
d\geqslant 2
randomly chosen bins, then the maximum load drops to
\log_{d}\log n+\mathcal{O}(1)
w.h.p., if
m=n
. This dramatic improvement from
d=1
(
One-Choice
) to
d=2
(
Two-Choice
) is known as “power-of-two-choices”, and similar effects have been observed in other problems including routing, hashing and randomized rounding
[
42
]
.

Later, Berenbrink, Czumaj, Steger and Vöcking
[
16
]
extended the analysis of
d
-Choice
to the so-called “heavily loaded case”, where
m\geqslant n
can be arbitrarily large. In particular, for
Two-Choice
an upper bound on the gap (the difference between the maximum and average load) of
\log_{2}\log n+\mathcal{O}(1)
w.h.p. was shown. A simpler but slightly weaker analysis was later given by Talwar and Wieder
[
49
]
.

A crucial ability of
Two-Choice
(or
d
-Choice
) is to quickly recover from a bad load vector, thanks to the information and flexibility provided by the additional bin sample(s).
Hence these processes typically reach an equilibrium with load vectors involving only a small number of different load values (around the mean). This can be seen as some self-stabilizing property, and was exploited in other balls-into-bins settings where balls can be removed, e.g.,
[
20
,
17
]
. Also there are many distributed algorithms for voting and consensus that make use of a similar “power-of-two-choices” effect, see e.g.,
[
24
,
21
,
10
,
12
]
. Other applications in distributed computing include population protocols
[
5
]
and distributed data structures
[
45
]
, whose analyses rely on a noisy version of
Two-Choice
.

It should be noted that the theoretical results for
Two-Choice
assume that balls arrive sequentially one by one, so that each bin has perfect knowledge about its load at any point in time. This assumption may not be always met in practice, e.g., in a concurrent setting, bins may not be able to update their load immediately (even if this were possible, in some applications bins might still prefer not to reveal their true load).
These issues had been observed as early as 2001 in a survey on power-of-two-choices by Mitzenmacher, Richa and Sitaraman
[
42
]
, who remarked:

“…the general problem of how to cope with incomplete or inaccurate load information and still achieve good load balancing performance appears to be an area with a great deal of research potential.”

In this work we are trying to improve our understanding of this problem and consider the following questions:

1.

What if the load information of a bin at time
t
is outdated, e.g., it might be as small as the load at an earlier time
t-\tau
for some parameter
\tau
?

2.

What if the load information of a bin at time
t
is subject to some adversarial noise, e.g., the reported load of a bin might be an adversarial perturbation from the exact load within some range
g
?

3.

What if instead of having adversarial noise, there is a Gaussian perturbation with standard deviation
\sigma
on the reported loads?

A closely related setting to the first question called
batching
was studied by Berenbrink, Czumaj, Englert, Friedetzky and Nagel
[
15
]
(and later in
[
17
]
). Here the allocation of the balls proceeds in consecutive batches of size
b
, and the load is only updated at the end of each batch. The authors proved that for
b=n
the gap is
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
w.h.p. Recently, the authors of this work proved some further results in the batched setting for a broader class of processes
[
35
]
. However, applying these to
Two-Choice
only gives a tight gap bound for
b\geqslant n\log n
, which is
\Theta(b/n
).
Mitzenmacher
[
40
]
studied the two-choice-paradigm in queuing systems, where the batched setting is referred to as
periodic update model
. His results include some fixed-point analysis and simulations, but no quantitative bounds on the gap are derived. Along similar lines, Dahlin
[
23
]
investigated several
d
-Choice
-based processes, and demonstrated through experiments that allocation strategies which interpret load information based on its age, can outperform simpler strategies.

In this work we will focus on noise settings related to the three questions above, but below we will also discuss other “robustness” aspects of
Two-Choice
that were studied in previous works.

Further Related Work

Peres, Talwar and Wieder
[
46
]
introduced the
(1+\beta)
-process, in which two choices are available with probability
\beta\in(0,1]
, and otherwise only one. This process mixes
One-Choice
with
Two-Choice
steps and is useful in applications where it is costly to always take two bin samples. Their upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(\log n/\beta+\log(1/\beta)/\beta)
on the gap for any
m\geqslant n
shows that we can achieve a gap that is independent of
m
for
\beta:=\beta(n)
.
A natural generalization of
Two-Choice
studied in the same work
[
46
]
(and earlier in
[
32
]
for
m=n
) is the so-called
graphical allocation setting
. In this setting, bins correspond to vertices of a graph, and for each ball we sample an edge uniformly at random and place the ball in the lesser loaded bin of the two endpoints. The results of
[
46
]
show that for any connected and regular graph, the gap remains independent of
n
. In
[
9
]
, Bansal and Feldheim analyzed a sophisticated algorithm that achieves a poly-logarithmic gap on sparse regular graphs in the graphical allocation setting. On a related problem where averaging is allowed, a tighter bound for the cycle was shown by Alistarh, Nadiradze and Sabour
[
6
]
. Also recently, Greenhill, Mans and Pourmiri studied the graphical allocation setting on dynamic hypergraphs
[
29
]
.

In the
(1+\beta)
-process the decision whether to take a second bin sample does not depend on the first bin sample. In contrast to that, the
Two-Thinning
process allocates in a two-stage procedure: Firstly, sample a random bin
i
. Secondly, based on the load of bin
i
(and additional information based on the history of the process), we can either place the ball into
i
, or place the ball into another randomly chosen bin
j
(without comparing its load with
i
). This process has received a lot of attention lately, and several variations were studied in
[
27
]
for
m=n
and
[
26
,
36
,
38
]
for
m\geqslant n
. Finally, Czumaj and Stemann
[
22
]
investigated so-called
adaptive allocation schemes
for
m=n
. In contrast to
Thinning
, after having taken a certain number of bin samples, the ball is allocated into the least loaded bin among all samples. In another related model recently studied by the authors of this work, the load of a sampled bin can only be approximated through binary queries of the form “Is your load at least
g
?”
[
36
]
. It was shown that by using
1\leqslant k=\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
queries for each of the two samples, for any
m\geqslant n
, the gap is
\mathcal{O}(k\cdot(\log n)^{1/k})
w.h.p.

A setting relaxing the
uniform sampling
assumption was investigated by Wieder
[
50
]
, which shows that for any
d>1
, the
d
-Choice
gap bounds continue to hold as long as the probability by which the
d
bins are sampled is close enough to uniform. A setting with heterogeneous bin capacities was studied in Berenbrink, Brinkmann, Friedetzky and Nagel
[
14
]
, who showed the gap bound of
\log_{d}\log n+\mathcal{O}(1)
for
d
-Choice
continues to hold.

There is also a rich line of work investigating randomized allocation schemes which use fewer random bits. For example, Alon, Gurel-Gurevich and Lubetzky
[
7
]
established a trade-off between the number of bits used for the representation of the load and the number of
d
bin choices. For
d=2
choices, Benjamini and Makarychev
[
13
]
presented some tight results relating the gap of
Two-Choice
to the available memory.

We remark that parallel versions of
Two-Choice
exist that involve only a very small number of rounds of interactions between all balls and bins. Most of the studies, e.g.,
[
2
,
34
]
focus on
m=n
, and only recently the heavily loaded case was addressed in
[
33
]
. In comparison to our settings, the gap bounds are stronger, however, these algorithms require more coordination and do not handle any noisy or outdated load information.

From a higher perspective, investigating the complexity of problems in the presence of noisy data is a popular area in algorithms, machine learning and distributed computing. For example, of similar flavor as our noisy allocation setting are studies on sorting and ranking with unreliable information
[
3
,
18
]
. In the paradigm of algorithms with predictions, different scheduling algorithms under noisy job size estimates have been analyzed
[
44
,
41
,
48
]
.

Our Contribution

Our first contribution is to present a general framework of four different classes of noise settings (a formal description of these settings can be found in
Section

2
). This framework contains some of the previously studied processes and settings (like batching
[
15
,
11
,
35
]
and
g
-Bounded

[
45
]
), but it also leads to new settings, which were not studied before.

Perhaps the most important setting involves
Two-Choice
in the presence of an
adaptive adversary
with parameter
g\in\mathbb{N}
. In each step, if the two bin samples
i_{1},i_{2}
have a load difference of at most
g
, the adversary can manipulate the outcome of the load comparison arbitrarily and thereby decide whether the ball will be placed in
i_{1}
or
i_{2}
. However, if the load difference is more than
g
, the comparison will be correct and
Two-Choice
will place the ball in the less loaded bin. A slightly weaker setting, which we call
myopic setting
, works similarly, but now, in case the load difference is at most
g
, the ball is placed into a
random
bin (among
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
).
Through a combination of different lower and upper bounds, we establish the following phase transition in how the parameter
g
impacts the gap:

1.

If
\log n\leqslant g
, then for all
m\geqslant n
,
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(g)
in the adversarial setting. Further, there is a matching lower bound in the myopic setting.

2.

If
1<g\leqslant\log n
, then for all
m\geqslant n
,
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
in the adversarial setting
2
2

2

For
g=1
, we prove a bound of
\Theta(\log\log n)
and for
g=0
, the process is equivalent to the
Two-Choice
process without noise.
. Further, there is a matching lower bound in the myopic setting.

Equivalently, we could say that
\Gap(m)=\Theta(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n+g)
for any
g>1
. Both upper bounds improve and generalize a bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\cdot\log(ng))
which was shown by Nadiradze
[
45
]
for the so-called
g
-Bounded
process, where an adversary “greedily” reverts all comparisons if the load difference is at most
g
. We believe that our new bounds could be helpful in obtaining a tighter analysis of the
multi-counter data structure
studied in
[
4
,
45
]
. Note that for constant
g
, our upper bound shows that
Two-Choice
maintains a
\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
gap even if bins with constant load difference cannot be compared correctly. While it may seem intuitive that such a result should hold, we are not aware of any simple argument. Even in the most basic case
g=1
, there seems to be no ad-hoc method, e.g., using couplings or majorization, which would extend the upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
for
Two-Choice
from
[
16
,
49
]
to this noise setting – not to mention a more general albeit loose upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\cdot\log\log n)
for any
g>1
.

We then proceed to delay settings with outdated load information. In the
delay setting
, each load information of a bin can be outdated by at most
\tau\geqslant 1
rounds. This is a generalization of the
batched setting

[
15
]
, where the load information of all bins is updated after rounds
0,b,2b,\ldots
. For a range of values for
\tau
and
b
around
n
, we prove tight bounds on the gap in the delay and batched setting. This upper bound is matched by a trivial lower bound for the batched setting, which is based on the gap created by
One-Choice
when allocating the first
b
balls randomly into the
n
bins. Our upper bound demonstrates that the special property of batching to reset all load values to their correct value at the beginning of a batch is not crucial, and it suffices if bin loads are updated asynchronously.
Finally, for the batched setting with
b=n
, our results improve the gap bound of
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
from
[
15
]
to a tight bound of
\Theta\big(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\big)
.

Finally, complementing the adversarial and myopic settings, we also investigate a
probabilistic noise
setting. In this setting, whenever two bins with load difference
\delta
are compared, the comparison will be correct with probability
\rho(\delta)\in[0,1]
. One natural instance of this setting is when the reported bin loads are randomly perturbed by some Gaussian noise with variance
\sigma^{2}
, which essentially leads to
\rho(\delta)=1-\frac{1}{2}\exp(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2})
. We do not present a tight analysis of this setting, but our upper and lower bounds demonstrate that the gap is polynomial in
\sigma
and poly-logarithmic in
n
.

In
Section

2
, we formally define the various processes and settings, and in
Table

2.3
we give a summary of upper and lower bounds proved in this paper.

Our Techniques

Since most of our upper bounds use similar techniques as the upper bound for the adversarial setting, we only outline the proof of the latter. As with most of the previous works, e.g.,
[
46
,
9
,
36
]
, we make extensive use of exponential potential functions in order to prove that the process stabilizes. This technique essentially suffices to prove a weaker gap bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\cdot\log(ng))
for the adversarial setting. However, in order to prove stronger gap bounds, we require that a technical precondition on the load distribution is satisfied in most of the rounds. Roughly, this precondition says that a linear potential function is small (even though the exponential potential function may be very large). Extending
[
38
]
, we establish this precondition by studying the interplay between three potential functions: the linear, quadratic and exponential potential functions.

For sub-logarithmic values of
g
, we prove sub-logarithmic gap bounds by employing a type of layered induction argument over a series of super-exponential potential functions. These potential functions are extensions of those used in
[
36
]
to circumvent rounding issues and prove more fine-grained gap bounds.

Organization

In
Section

2
we present in more detail the different noise settings considered in this work. Then, in
Section

3
we introduce some basic notation and definitions used in the later analysis. In
Section

4
, we present our first bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\cdot\log(ng))
on the gap for the adversarial setting. In
Section

5
, this gap bound is then refined to
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
(
5.14
). In
Section

6
, we outline the use of a layered-induction inspired technique to prove a gap bound of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for
g=o(\log n)
(
9.2
) and in the following three sections we give the proof of this bound. More specifically, in
Section

7
, we strengthen the results of
Section

5
to obtain the base case, in
Section

8
we provide a general analysis of super-exponential potentials and in
Section

9
, we use this to complete the layered induction step. Next, in
Section

10
we derive upper bounds for both the probabilistic noise and the delay settings, essentially through reductions to the adversarial setting.
Section

11
contains our lower bounds for the different settings, including a tight lower bound of
\Omega(g+\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for
g
-Myopic-Comp
(
11.4
).
Section

12
presents some experimental results and in
Section

13
we conclude with a brief summary of the main results and some open questions.

2
Our Settings

All of our settings are based on running the
Two-Choice
process with
m
balls into
n
bins, labeled
[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.

In the normal setting, referred to as “without noise”, the load comparisons between the two bin samples are correct in all steps. Here, we investigate several noise settings where the outcome of the load comparisons may not always be correct. We distinguish between three classes of noise settings: (1) Adversarial Load and Comparison, (2) Adversarial Delay and (3) Probabilistic Load and Comparison.
A schematic figure with the connections between the different settings is shown in
Fig.

2.1
.

-
Adv-Comp
g
-Bounded
(g/2)
-

(1+\beta)
(w.o. noise)

Figure 2.1
:
Overview of settings (rounded rectangles) and processes (rectangles). A directed arrow from setting (process)
A
to setting (process)
B
means that
B
is stronger than
A
(that is,
B
can simulate
A
). For
\tau
-Delay
, a dashed arrow is used for the connection to
g
-Adv-Comp
, as the (strong) connection is slightly more involved.

Two-Choice
Process (without noise):

Iteration:
For each step
t\geqslant 1
,

1.

Sample two bins
i_{1}=i_{1}^{t}
and
i_{2}=i_{2}^{t}
with replacement, independently and uniformly at random.

2.

Let
i^{t}\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
be a bin such that

x_{i^{t}}^{t-1}=\min\big\{x_{i_{1}}^{t-1},x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}\big\},
breaking ties arbitrarily.

3.

Allocate one ball to bin
i^{t}
.

We now present our framework where
Two-Choice
runs in a noisy setting, meaning that step
2
is subject to some noise.
One possibility is to have an adversary that is able to replace the load values
x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}
and
x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}
by estimates
\widetilde{x}_{i_{1}}^{t-1}
and
\widetilde{x}_{i_{2}}^{t-1}
, while the bin
i^{t}
will always be the one with the better load estimate. A more general framework is to have a two-sample process with a constrained decision function that decides in which of the two samples
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
to allocate to. This function may depend on the filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{t-1}
corresponding to the first
t-1
allocations of the process.
3
3

3

In particular, filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{t}
reveals the load vector
x^{t}
.

Two-Choice
Process with Noise:

Noise:
The adversary
A^{t}:\mathfrak{F}^{t-1}\times[n]\times[n]\to[n]

Iteration:
For each step
t\geqslant 1
,

1.

Sample two bins
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
with replacement, independently and uniformly at random.

2.

Let
i^{t}=A^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t-1},i_{1},i_{2})\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
be the bin determined by the adversary.

3.

Allocate one ball to bin
i^{t}
.

This is a very general setting, and in order to prove any meaningful bounds, we will have to restrict the power of the adversary
A^{t}
, giving rise to different settings defined below. Also note that the adversary
A^{t}
is allowed to use coin-flips, meaning that the above framework encompasses settings in which, for instance, load estimates or comparisons are determined probabilistically.

Clearly we can recover
Two-Choice
without noise as a special case, if the adversary uses

A^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t-1},i_{1},i_{2})=\mathrm{arg\,min}_{k\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}\ x_{k}^{t-1},

breaking ties arbitrarily. On the other hand, for the
g
-Bounded
process,

A^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t-1},i_{1},i_{2})=\begin{cases}\mathrm{arg\,max}_{k\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}\ x_{k}^{t-1}&\text{if }|x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}|\leqslant g,\\
\mathrm{arg\,min}_{k\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}}\ x_{k}^{t-1}&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

breaking ties arbitrarily.

Adversarial Load and Comparison

Settings.
We first present a setting with an
adaptive adversary
, who has direct control on the outcome of load comparisons (provided that the load difference is small).
Specifically, in
g
-Adv-Load
for
g\in\mathbb{N}
, at each step
t=1,2,\ldots
the adversary first determines for each bin
k
a load estimate
\widetilde{x}_{k}^{t-1}\in[x_{k}^{t-1}-g,x_{k}^{t-1}+g]
. Then our process samples two bins
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, and allocates into the bin with the smaller load estimate (ties can be broken arbitrarily).
A slightly stronger adversary appears in the setting
g
-Adv-Comp
. Here, at each step
t=1,2,\ldots
the process samples two bins
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
, and then the adversary is able to determine the outcome of the comparison if
|x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}|\leqslant g
, thereby deciding where
Two-Choice
places the ball. Otherwise, if
|x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}|>g
,
Two-Choice
will place the ball in the less loaded bin. For
g=0
, we recover the
Two-Choice
process without noise.

We remark that
g
-Adv-Load
can be simulated by
(2g)
-Adv-Comp
, which is why we will only study the slightly more powerful
Adv-Comp
setting here.

Processes.
A special instance of
g
-Adv-Comp
is one that forces
Two-Choice
to allocate the ball to the heavier of the two bins when
|x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}|\leqslant g
. This process was studied in
[
45
]
under the name
g
-Bounded
and served as an analysis tool for the multi-counter distributed data structure.

Another special instance of the
adversarial
setting is a
myopic
process, where the outcomes of “tight” load comparisons is decided uniformly at random. More precisely, in
g
-Myopic-Comp
, at each step
t=1,2,\ldots
, if
|x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}|\leqslant g
, the ball is allocated to a random bin among
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
, otherwise, the ball is allocated to the less loaded among
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
. By using random coin flips, the adversary of
g
-Adv-Comp
can trivially simulate
g
-Myopic-Comp
.

Adversarial Delay

Setting.
Next we turn to adversarial settings where the load information a bin reports may be
outdated
. The first setting, called
\tau
-
Delay
for
\tau\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
, is similar to
g
-Adv-Comp
, but here the range of load values the adversary can choose from is based on a sliding time interval. That is, after bins
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are sampled in step
t
, an adaptive adversary provides us with load estimates that must satisfy
\widetilde{x}_{i_{1}}^{t-1}\in[x_{i_{1}}^{t-\tau},x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}]
and

\widetilde{x}_{i_{2}}^{t-1}\in[x_{i_{2}}^{t-\tau},x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}]
, and the ball is then allocated to the bin with a smaller load estimate, breaking ties arbitrarily. As an example, this includes an adversary that has the power of delaying load updates to each bin arbitrarily, but any load update (i.e., allocation) that is
\tau
or more steps in the past must be processed.

Processes.
A special case of
\tau
-Delay
is
b
-Batch
for
b\in\mathbb{N}_{\geqslant 1}
, introduced in
[
15
]
, where balls are allocated in consecutive batches of size
b
each. When the load of a bin
i
is queried, the bin
i
will report the load the bin had at the beginning of the batch (i.e., all allocations within the current batch will not be considered). More formally, when two bins
i_{1},i_{2}
are sampled in step
t
, the decision where to allocate is based on comparing
x_{i_{1}}^{\lfloor(t-1)/b\rfloor\cdot b}
and
x_{i_{2}}^{\lfloor(t-1)/b\rfloor\cdot b}
, breaking ties randomly. We recall that in
[
15
]
, only the special case
b=n
was studied, and a gap bound of
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
was shown. Clearly, for
\tau=b
,
\tau
-Delay
can simulate
b
-Batch
and for
\tau=b=1
, both are equivalent to the
Two-Choice
process without noise. Note that
b
-Batch
assumes that load updates are perfectly synchronized, while the
\tau
-Delay
setting relaxes this assumption, therefore covering a wider class of asynchronous update schemes. The reduction of the
\tau
-Delay
(and
b
-Batch
) settings to a relaxed
g
-Adv-Comp
setting will be formalized in
Section

10.2
.

Probabilistic Noise

Setting.
We now turn to non-adversarial settings where comparisons are subject to
probabilistic noise
, with a larger chance of getting the correct comparison the larger the load difference. Specifically, we consider the
Noisy-Comp
setting, where we have an arbitrary non-decreasing function
\rho:\mathbb{N}\rightarrow[0,1]
such that for any
\delta\in\mathbb{N}
,
\rho(\delta)
is the probability that a comparison between two bins with absolute load difference
\delta
will be correct.
As before we assume independence, i.e., for all
t\geqslant 1
, the event of a correct comparison in step
t
only depends on the load difference of the two sampled bins. Thus, for a specific function
\rho
,
\rho
-Noisy-Comp
defines a process.

Processes.
This is a very expressive setting, encompassing, for example the

g
-Bounded
and
g
-Myopic-Comp
processes by choosing
\rho
as step functions with values in
\{0,1/2,1\}
(see
Fig.

2.2
). Similarly,
Two-Choice
(without noise),
One-Choice
and
(1+\beta)
correspond to
\rho
being the constant
1
,
1/2
and
1/2+\beta/2
, respectively.

As a concrete example consider the
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
process. Here, when a bin
i\in\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
is sampled at step
t
, it reports an unbiased load estimate
\widetilde{x}_{i}^{t-1}=x_{i}^{t-1}+Z_{i}^{t-1}
, where
Z_{i}^{t-1}
has a normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
(and all
\{Z_{i}^{t}\}_{i\in[n],t\geqslant 0}
are mutually independent). Then
Two-Choice
allocates a ball to the bin that reports the smallest load estimate. Thus if
x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}=\delta>0
, the probability to allocate to the lighter bin can be computed as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i_{1}}^{t-1}\leqslant\widetilde{x}_{i_{2}}^{t-1}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z_{i_{1}}^{t-1}-Z_{i_{2}}^{t-1}\leqslant\delta\,\right]

\displaystyle=1-\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{N}(0,2\sigma^{2})>\delta\,\right]

\displaystyle=1-\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{N}(0,1)>\delta/(\sqrt{2}\sigma)\,\right]

\displaystyle=1-\Phi(\delta/(\sqrt{2}\sigma)).

Note that
\Phi(z)=1/2
for
z=0
and
\Phi(z)
is increasing in
z
.
As shown in
[
30
, p. 17]
, for
z\geqslant 0
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{z^{2}+4}+z}\cdot e^{-z^{2}/2}\leqslant 1-\Phi(z)\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\cdot\frac{2}{\sqrt{z^{2}+2}+z}\cdot e^{-z^{2}/2}.

By setting
z:=\delta/(\sqrt{2}\sigma)
, ignoring the linear term in
1/z
, and re-scaling
\sigma
, we can define
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
as the process satisfying for all steps
t
and samples
i_{1},i_{2}
with
x_{i_{2}}^{t-1}-x_{i_{1}}^{t-1}=\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i_{1}}^{t-1}\leqslant\widetilde{x}_{i_{2}}^{t-1}\,\right]=\rho(\delta):=1-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\exp\left(-\left(\frac{\delta}{\sigma}\right)^{2}\right),

(2.1)

meaning that the correct comparison probability exhibits a Gaussian tail behavior.

is the load difference among the two sampled bins and
\rho(\delta)
is the probability that the load comparison is correct for the
g
-Bounded
,
g

and
\sigma

processes, respectively.

3
Notation and Preliminaries

We sequentially allocate
m
balls (jobs) to
n
bins (servers) with labels in
[n]:=\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
.
The
load vector
after
t\geqslant 0
steps, i.e.,
t
allocations, is
x^{t}=(x_{1}^{t},x_{2}^{t},\ldots,x_{n}^{t})
and at the beginning,
x_{i}^{0}=0
for any
i\in[n]
. For any
t\geqslant 0
, we also define the normalized load by sorting the entries of
x^{t}
non-increasingly and setting
y_{i}^{t}:=x_{i}^{t}-\frac{t}{n}
; so
y_{1}^{t}\geqslant y_{2}^{t}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant y_{n}^{t}
.
We will analyze the performance of allocation processes via the
gap
, defined for any
t\geqslant 0
as

\Gap(t):=\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}x_{i}^{t}-\frac{t}{n}=y_{1}^{t},

i.e., the difference between maximum and average load after step
t\geqslant 0
. It is well-known that even for
Two-Choice
without noise, the difference between the maximum and minimum load is
\Omega(\log n)
for
m=\Omega(n\log n)
.
Further, for any step
t\geqslant 0
, we define
B_{+}^{t}:=\{i\in[n]:y_{i}^{t}\geqslant 0\}
as the set of
overloaded
bins and
B_{-}^{t}:=\{i\in[n]:y_{i}^{t}<0\}
as the set of
underloaded
bins.

Following
[
46
]
, many allocation processes can be described by a
probability allocation vector

r^{t}=(r_{1}^{t},r_{2}^{t},\ldots,r_{n}^{t})
for step
t
, where
r_{i}^{t}
is the probability for incrementing the load of the
i
-th most loaded bin.

Recall the definition of the
Two-Choice
process from
Section

2
. The
Two-Choice
process without noise has a time-independent probability allocation vector
p
, where the probability to allocate to the
i
-th most loaded bin is given by
p_{i}=\frac{2i-1}{n^{2}},

for
i\in[n]
. Recall that in
One-Choice
, each ball is allocated into a bin sampled independently and uniformly at random. This corresponds to
r
being the uniform distribution. We say that a probability vector
q
majorizes another probability vector
r
if for all
k\in[n]
, it satisfies
\sum_{j=1}^{k}q_{j}\geqslant\sum_{j=1}^{k}r_{j}
. The same definition applies unchanged to sorted load vectors.

For a sequence of random variables
(X^{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
, we define
\Delta X^{t+1}:=X^{t+1}-X^{t}
. We also use the shorthands
u^{+}:=\max\{u,0\}
and
u_{1}\wedge u_{2}:=\min\{u_{1},u_{2}\}
.

Many statements in this work hold only for sufficiently large
n
, and several constants are chosen generously with the intention of making it easier to verify some technical inequalities.

4
Warm-Up: Upper Bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
for
g
-Adv-Comp

In this section we will prove the
\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
gap bound for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting (with
g\geqslant 1
being arbitrary), also recovering the
\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
gap bound for the
g
-Bounded
process proven in
[
45
]
. The proof relies on a version of the exponential potential function
[
46
]
, called
hyperbolic cosine potential

\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
, which is defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}:=\Gamma^{t}(\gamma):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma_{i}^{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[e^{\gamma\cdot y_{i}^{t}}+e^{-\gamma\cdot y_{i}^{t}}\right],

(4.1)

for a smoothing parameter
\gamma\in(0,1)
, to be specified in
4.3
. Recall that
\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}:=\Gamma^{t+1}-\Gamma^{t}
. We make use of the following general lemma from
[
46
]
regarding the expected change of
\Gamma
.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
46
, Lemmas 2.1 & 2.3]
)
.

Consider any allocation process with probability allocation vector
r^{t}
and the potential
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
with any
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\Big(r_{i}^{t}\cdot(\gamma+\gamma^{2})-\Big(\frac{\gamma}{n}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{n^{2}}\Big)\Big)\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}+\Big(r_{i}^{t}\cdot(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})+\Big(\frac{\gamma}{n}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{n^{2}}\Big)\Big)\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{i}^{t}}\right].

For convenience, we rewrite
4.1
by decomposing the upper bound into the components that are independent of the probability allocation vector
r^{t}
and those that are not, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant h(y^{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}^{t}\cdot f(y_{i}^{t}),

(4.2)

where
h(y^{t}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}-\big(\frac{\gamma}{n}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{n^{2}}\big)\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}+\big(\frac{\gamma}{n}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{n^{2}}\big)\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{i}^{t}}
and
f(y_{i}^{t}):=(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}+(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{i}^{t}}
.

We will also make use of the following drop inequality for
Two-Choice
without noise (see also
[
35
, Theorem 3.1]
).

Lemma 4.2

(
implied by
[
46
, Theorem 2.9]
)
.

Consider the
Two-Choice
process without noise with probability allocation vector
p
and the potential
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
with any
\gamma\in(0,\frac{1}{6\cdot 12})
. Then, there exists a constant
c>0
, such that for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant h(y^{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\cdot f(y_{i}^{t})\leqslant-\frac{\gamma}{48n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c.

We will analyze
\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}
for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting by relating it to the change
\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}
for the
Two-Choice
process without noise. To this end, it will be helpful to define all pairs of bins (of unequal load), whose comparison is under the control of the adversary:

\displaystyle R^{t}:=\left\{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]\colon y_{j}^{t}<y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g\right\}.

(4.3)

So for each pair
(i,j)\in R^{t}
, the adversary determines the outcome of the load comparison assuming
\{i,j\}
are the two bin samples in step
t+1
, which happens with probability
2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}=\frac{2}{n^{2}}
. This can be seen as moving a probability of up to
\frac{2}{n^{2}}
from bin
j
to bin
i
, if we relate the probability allocation vector
p
(of
Two-Choice
without noise) to the probability allocation vector
q^{t}=q^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t})
of
Two-Choice
with noise.

x_{i}^{t}
:

Figure 4.1
:
Illustration of the set
R^{t}
and the change in the probability allocation vector from
p
to
q^{t}
, where
n=8
and
g=3
. In the example, each directed arrow moves a probability of
\frac{2}{n^{2}}
(indicated by the blue rectangles) from a bin
j
to a heavier bin
i<j
. Note that in this example, the adversary decides not to reverse some of the comparisons, e.g., between bins
7
and
8
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
, and the potential
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
with
\gamma:=-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})/g<\frac{1}{6\cdot 12}
. Then, there exist constants
c_{1}\geqslant 1,c_{2}>0,c_{3}\geqslant 2
, such that the following three statements hold for all steps
t\geqslant 0
:

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c_{1},

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t}\,\right]\leqslant c_{2}ng,

\displaystyle(iii)

\displaystyle\qquad\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-14}.

Proof.

First statement.
Consider the probability allocation vector
q^{t}
at step
t
in the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting. By
Eq.

4.2
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant h(y^{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}^{t}\cdot f(y_{i}^{t}).

Recall that
p
is the probability allocation vector of
Two-Choice
without noise. Then,

q^{t}:=p+\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\left(\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j}\right)\cdot\gamma_{i,j}^{t}+\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}=y_{j}^{t}}\left(\mathbf{e}_{i}-\mathbf{e}_{j}\right)\cdot\gamma_{i,j}^{t},

where
\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
i
-th unit vector, and
\gamma_{i,j}^{t}
is a number in
\big[0,\frac{2}{n^{2}}\big]
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant h(y^{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\cdot f(y_{i}^{t})+\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\gamma_{i,j}^{t}\cdot\left(f(y_{i}^{t})-f(y_{j}^{t})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}=y_{j}^{t}}\gamma_{i,j}^{t}\cdot\left(f(y_{i}^{t})-f(y_{j}^{t})\right)

\displaystyle=h(y^{t})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}p_{i}\cdot f(y_{i}^{t})+\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\gamma_{i,j}^{t}\cdot\left(f(y_{i}^{t})-f(y_{j}^{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{\gamma}{48n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c+\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\gamma_{i,j}^{t}\cdot\left(f(y_{i}^{t})-f(y_{j}^{t})\right),

(4.4)

using in the last inequality that by
4.2
there exists such a constant
c>0
for the
Two-Choice
process without noise and for the same
\gamma
(since
\gamma<\frac{1}{6\cdot 12}
).

For any pair of indices
(i,j)\in R^{t}
, we define

\displaystyle\xi_{i,j}^{t}:=\

\displaystyle\gamma_{i,j}^{t}\cdot\left(f(y_{i}^{t})-f(y_{j}^{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}+(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{i}^{t}}-(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{j}^{t}}-(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{j}^{t}}\right),

and proceed to upper bound
\xi_{i,j}^{t}
, using the following lemma, which is based on a case distinction and Taylor estimates for
\exp(\cdot)
.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any pair of indices
(i,j)\in R^{t}
, we have
\xi_{i,j}^{t}\leqslant\frac{\gamma}{96n^{2}}\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})+\frac{8}{n^{2}}.

Proof of
4.4
.

Recall that for any
(i,j)\in R^{t}
we have that
y_{j}^{t}<y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g
. So, now we consider the following three disjoint cases:

Case 1 [
y_{i}^{t}>g
]:
In this case, we also have that
y_{j}^{t}>0
, so

\displaystyle\xi_{i,j}^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}-(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{j}^{t}}+1\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}-(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma(y_{i}^{t}-g)}+1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{\gamma y_{i}^{t}}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\Big((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot\Gamma_{i}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+1\Big),

using in
(a)
that
0\leqslant\gamma-\gamma^{2}\leqslant 1
and
e^{-\gamma y_{k}^{t}}\leqslant 1
for any bin
k\in[n]
with
y_{k}^{t}>0
, and in
(b)
that
y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g
.

Case 2 [
y_{j}^{t}<-g
]:
In this case, we also have that
y_{i}^{t}<0
,

\displaystyle\xi_{i,j}^{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{i}^{t}}-(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{j}^{t}}+1\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma(y_{j}^{t}+g)}-(-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{j}^{t}}+1\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\left((-\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot e^{-\gamma y_{j}^{t}}\cdot(e^{-\gamma g}-1)+1\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\Big((\gamma-\gamma^{2})\cdot\Gamma_{j}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+1\Big),

using in
(a)
that
\gamma+\gamma^{2}\leqslant 1
and that
e^{\gamma y_{k}^{t}}\leqslant 1
for any bin
k\in[n]
with
y_{k}^{t}<0
and in
(b)
that
y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g
and
\gamma^{2}\leqslant\gamma
.

Case 3 [
\max\{|y_{i}^{t}|,|y_{j}^{t}|\}\leqslant g
]:
In this case, we have that

\xi_{i,j}^{t}\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot(\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(2\cdot 2\cdot e^{\gamma g}\right)\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot 4,

using that
\gamma+\gamma^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
(since
\gamma<\frac{1}{6\cdot 12}
) and by the definition of
\gamma
, we have
e^{\gamma g}=e^{-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})}<2
.

Combining the upper bounds for the three cases, we have that for any
(i,j)\in R^{t}
,

\displaystyle\xi_{i,j}^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\Big((\gamma+\gamma^{2})\cdot\Gamma_{i}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+(\gamma-\gamma^{2})\cdot\Gamma_{j}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+4\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot\Big(2\gamma\cdot\Gamma_{i}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+2\gamma\cdot\Gamma_{j}^{t}\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+4\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{4\gamma}{n^{2}}\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})\cdot(1-e^{-\gamma g})+\frac{8}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\frac{4\gamma}{n^{2}}\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})\cdot\frac{1}{8\cdot 48}+\frac{8}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{96n^{2}}\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})+\frac{8}{n^{2}},

using in
(a)
that
\gamma^{2}\leqslant\gamma
since
\gamma\leqslant 1
and in
(b)
that
\gamma=-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})/g
.
∎

We continue with the proof of
4.3
.
By
4.4
and
Eq.

4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant-\frac{\gamma}{48n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c+\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\Big(\frac{\gamma}{96n^{2}}\cdot(\Gamma_{i}^{t}+\Gamma_{j}^{t})+\frac{8}{n^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}-\frac{\gamma}{48n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c+\frac{\gamma}{96n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+4

\displaystyle=-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\cdot\Gamma^{t}+c_{1},

(4.5)

for
c_{1}:=c+4\geqslant 1
, where
(a)
holds since if
(i,j)\in R^{t}
then
(j,i)\not\in R^{t}
, so every bin
k\in[n]
appears at most
n
times in
R^{t}
. This concludes the proof of the first statement.

Second statement.
By
A.11

(ii)
(for
a=1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}
and
b=c_{1}
), since
\Gamma^{0}=2n\leqslant\frac{96c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n
(as
c_{1}\geqslant 1
and
\gamma\leqslant 1
) and
Eq.

4.5
hold, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{96c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n=:c_{2}ng.

Third statement.
Using Markov’s inequality, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gamma^{t}\leqslant c_{2}\cdot(ng)^{15}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-14}.

When the event
\big\{\Gamma^{t}\leqslant c_{2}\cdot(ng)^{15}\big\}
holds, we have that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot\left(\log c_{2}+15\log(ng)\right)\leqslant\frac{16\log(ng)}{\gamma}=:c_{3}g\log(ng),

for sufficiently large
n
and for the constant

\displaystyle c_{3}:=\frac{16}{\gamma g}=\frac{16}{-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})}\geqslant 2.

(4.6)

Therefore we conclude that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-14}.\qed

Next we will also state a simple corollary that starting with a “small” gap, in any future step, w.h.p. the gap will be small. This corollary will be used in obtaining the tighter
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
gap bound in
Section

5
. We defer its proof to
Section

B.1
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, for any steps
t_{0}\geqslant 0
and
t_{1}\geqslant t_{0}
, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant 2g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{14}.

5
Upper Bound of
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
for
g
-
Adv-Comp

In this section we give the proof of the
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
gap bound, as stated in the theorem below. For
g=\Omega(\log n)
, this matches the lower bound for the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process in
11.2
up to multiplicative constants.

Theorem
5.14

(
Simplified version, page
5.14
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-9}.

5.1
Proof Outline of Theorem
5.14

The proof of this theorem is considerably more involved than that of
4.3
, making use of an interplay between a variant of the hyperbolic cosine, the absolute value and the quadratic potential (to be defined below).

First, we define the
hyperbolic cosine potential with an offset
, denoted by
\Lambda
, as

\displaystyle\Lambda^{t}:=\Lambda^{t}(\alpha,c_{4}g):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{i}^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}+e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\right],

(5.1)

for smoothing parameter
\alpha:=\frac{1}{18}
and offset
c_{4}g
with
c_{4}:=730
, and recalling that
u^{+}:=\max\{u,0\}
. Compared to
\Gamma
used in
Section

4
, the potential
\Lambda
has a larger, i.e., constant smoothing parameter
\alpha
at the cost of an offset of
\Theta(g)
. This means that if at some step
t\geqslant 0
,
\Lambda^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
holds, then we can deduce a stronger upper bound of
\Gap(t)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
.

However, we are
not able to show that
\Lambda
drops in expectation in every step. More specifically, we only show that
\Lambda^{t}
drops in expectation at step
t
when the
absolute value potential

\Delta^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|
satisfies the condition
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
for
D:=365
. This condition implies that there is at most a constant fraction of bins
i\in[n]
with
\big|y_{i}^{t}\big|\geqslant\frac{3}{2}Dg
and hence there is a bias to place away from bins
j\in[n]
with
\big|y_{j}^{t}\big|\geqslant 2Dg=c_{4}g
, i.e., the ones with load above the offset of
\Lambda
.
More specifically, in such
good
steps
t
we show that
\Lambda^{t}
satisfies a drop inequality, meaning that when large it drops by a multiplicative factor over the next step in expectation.

Lemma 5.1

(
Simplified version, page
5.9
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and let
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{12}
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng\,\right]\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{2\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big)+18\alpha.

In order to show that there are many steps with
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
, we relate the expected change of the
quadratic potential

\Upsilon^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{t})^{2}
to
\Delta^{t}
. More specifically, by upper bounding the contribution of each pair
(i,j)\in R^{t}
(defined in
Eq.

4.3
), similarly to the proof of
4.3
, we show that:

Lemma 5.2

(
Restated, page
5.5
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+2g+1.

This allows us to prove that in any sufficiently long interval, there is a large constant fraction of good steps, i.e., with
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
(
Section

5.3
). Thus using an
adjusted exponential function
based on
\Lambda
(see
Section

5.5
for the definition), we show that w.h.p.
\Lambda^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
for some
s\in[m-\Theta(ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2}),m]
(
recovery phase
) and then w.h.p.
\Lambda^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
once every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps (
stabilization phase
). This implies that w.h.p.
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
.
In more detail:

1.

(
Base case
) For any
t\geqslant 0
, w.h.p.
\Gap(t)=\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
. (This follows from
4.3
)

2.

(
Recovery
) Starting with
\Gap(t_{0})=\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
for
t_{0}=m-\Theta(ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2})
, w.h.p. there exists a step
t_{1}\in[t_{0},m]
such that
\Lambda^{t_{1}}=\mathcal{O}(n)
(
5.11
).

3.

(
Stabilization
) Starting with
\Lambda^{t_{1}}=\mathcal{O}(n)
, w.h.p. there exists a step
s\in(t_{1},t_{1}+\Theta(n\cdot(g+\log n))]
such that
\Lambda^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
(
5.12
).

4.

(
Gap deduction
) Hence w.h.p. there is a step
t\in[m,m+\Theta(n\cdot(g+\log n))]
with
\Gap(t)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
, which by smoothness of the gap, implies
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
(
Section

5.7
).

5.2
Absolute Value and Quadratic Potentials

Recall that the
absolute value potential
is defined as

\displaystyle\Delta^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|,

(5.2)

and the
quadratic potential
is defined as

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}^{t})^{2}.

(5.3)

We will upper bound the expected change
\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]
in the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting by relating it to the change of the quadratic potential for
Two-Choice
, starting with the same load vector
y^{t}
at step
t
.

We will first analyze the expected change of the quadratic potential for the
Two-Choice
process without noise. We will make use of the following general lemma (proven in
B.2
), which provides a formula for the change of the quadratic potential:

Lemma 5.3
.

Consider any allocation process with probability allocation vector
r^{t}
at step
t\geqslant 0
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,
(i)
it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1,

and
(ii)
it holds that

\left|\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\right|\leqslant 4\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|+2.

We now use the general formula in
5.3

(i)
to obtain an expression for the expected change of the quadratic potential for
Two-Choice
without noise.

Lemma 5.4

(
cf.
[
38
, Lemma 6.2]
)
.

Consider the
Two-Choice
process without noise with probability allocation vector
p
. Then, it holds that for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1\leqslant-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+1.

Proof.

Applying
5.3

(i)
to the probability allocation vector
p
yields

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1.

Recall that
B_{+}^{t}:=\{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}\geqslant 0\}
is the set of overloaded bins at step
t
and
B_{-}^{t}:=\{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}<0\}
, the set of underloaded bins. The
Two-Choice
process allocates a ball into the set of overloaded bins with probability
|B_{+}^{t}|^{2}/n^{2}
, and thus the average allocation probability across overloaded bins is
p_{+}^{t}=|B_{+}^{t}|/n^{2}
. Consequently,
Two-Choice
allocates to the set of underloaded bins with probability
1-|B_{+}^{t}|^{2}/n^{2}
, and thus the average allocation probability across underloaded bins is

p_{-}^{t}=\frac{1}{|B_{-}^{t}|}\cdot\left(1-\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|^{2}}{n^{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{n-|B_{+}^{t}|}\cdot\frac{(n+|B_{+}^{t}|)\cdot(n-|B_{+}^{t}|)}{n^{2}}=\frac{1}{n}+\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|}{n^{2}}.

By splitting the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}
into underloaded and overloaded bins, we get

\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}=\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\sum_{i\in B_{-}^{t}}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}.

Since
p_{i}
is non-decreasing, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{j}p_{i}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{j}p_{+}^{t}
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant|B_{+}^{t}|
. Further, since
y_{i}^{t}
is non-increasing over the overloaded bins, by
A.12
we have

\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}\leqslant\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}2\cdot p_{+}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}=2\cdot p_{+}^{t}\cdot\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}y_{i}^{t}=\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|}{n^{2}}\cdot\Delta^{t},

since
\sum_{i\in B_{-}^{t}}y_{i}^{t}=-\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}y_{i}^{t}
and thus
\sum_{i\in B_{+}^{t}}y_{i}^{t}=\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{t}
. Analogously, since
y_{i}^{t}
is non-increasing over the underloaded bins,

\sum_{i\in B_{-}^{t}}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}\leqslant\sum_{i\in B_{-}^{t}}2\cdot p_{-}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}=2\cdot p_{-}^{t}\cdot\sum_{i\in B_{-}^{t}}y_{i}^{t}=\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|}{n^{2}}\right)\cdot\Delta^{t}.

Combining these we get

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1\leqslant\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|}{n^{2}}\cdot\Delta^{t}-\left(\frac{1}{n}+\frac{|B_{+}^{t}|}{n^{2}}\right)\cdot\Delta^{t}+1=-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+1.\qed

Now we relate the change of the quadratic potential for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting to the change of the quadratic potential for
Two-Choice
without noise, using that the adversary can determine (and possibly revert) a load comparison between
y_{i}^{t}
and
y_{j}^{t}
only if
|y_{i}^{t}-y_{j}^{t}|\leqslant g
.

Lemma 5.5
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+2g+1.

Proof.

By
5.3

(i)
, for the
g
-Adv-Comp
probability allocation vector
q^{t}
we have,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot q_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1.

This probability vector
q^{t}
is obtained from the probability allocation vector
p
of
Two-Choice
without noise by moving a probability of up to
\frac{2}{n^{2}}
from any bin
j
to a bin
i
with
y_{j}^{t}<y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g
. Recalling that
R^{t}:=\{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]\colon y_{j}^{t}<y_{i}^{t}\leqslant y_{j}^{t}+g\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1+2\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-y_{j}^{t})

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1+2\cdot\sum_{(i,j)\in R^{t}}\frac{2}{n^{2}}\cdot g

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot p_{i}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1+2g,

using that
|R^{t}|<\frac{1}{2}n^{2}
. Hence, using
5.4
, we conclude that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]\leqslant-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+2g+1.\qed

5.3
Constant Fraction of Good Steps

We define a step
s\geqslant 0
to be a
good step
if
\mathcal{G}^{s}:=\{\Delta^{s}\leqslant Dng\}
holds, for
D:=365
. Further,
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:=G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(D)
denotes the number of good steps in
[t_{0},t_{1}]
. Later, in
Section

5.4
we will show that in a good step, the exponential potential
\Lambda
with any sufficiently small constant
\alpha
drops in expectation.

In the following lemma we show that at least a constant fraction
r
of the steps are good in a sufficiently long interval. We will apply this lemma with two different values for
T
:
(i)
in the recovery phase, to prove that there exists a step
s\in[m-\Theta(ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2}),m]
with
\Lambda^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
and
(ii)
in the stabilization phase, to prove that every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps there exists a step
s
with
\Lambda^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
. In the analysis below, we pick
r:=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{12}
.

Lemma 5.6
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and let
r:=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}
and
D:=365
. Then, for any constant
\hat{c}\geqslant 1
and any
T\in[ng^{2},n^{2}g^{3}/\hat{c}]
, we have for any steps
t_{0}\geqslant 0
and
t_{1}:=t_{0}+\hat{c}\cdot T\cdot g^{-1}-1
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(D)\geqslant r\cdot(t_{1}-t_{0}+1)\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

Proof.

We define the sequence
(Z^{t})_{t\geqslant t_{0}}
with
Z^{t_{0}}:=\Upsilon^{t_{0}}
and for any
t>t_{0}
,

Z^{t}:=\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{t-1}\left(\frac{\Delta^{s}}{n}-2g-1\right).

This sequence forms a super-martingale since by
5.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.Z^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Upsilon^{t+1}+\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{t}\left(\frac{\Delta^{s}}{n}-2g-1\right)~\,\right|\,~\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Upsilon^{t}-\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+2g+1+\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{t}\left(\frac{\Delta^{s}}{n}-2g-1\right)

\displaystyle=\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{t-1}\left(\frac{\Delta^{s}}{n}-2g-1\right)=Z^{t}.

Further, let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant t_{0}\colon\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t}|>2g(\log(ng))^{2}\}
and consider the stopped random variable

\widetilde{Z}^{t}:=Z^{t\wedge\tau},

which is then also a super-martingale. Applying

4.5
and the union bound over steps
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant t_{1}\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant(\hat{c}\cdot T\cdot g^{-1})\cdot(ng)^{-14}\leqslant(ng)^{-12},

(5.4)

using that
T\leqslant n^{2}g^{3}/\hat{c}
. This means that the maximum absolute normalized load does not increase above
2g(\log(ng))^{2}
in any of the steps in
[t_{0},t_{1}]
w.h.p.

To prove concentration of
\widetilde{Z}^{t_{1}+1}
, we will now derive an upper bound on the difference
|\widetilde{Z}^{t+1}-\widetilde{Z}^{t}|
:

Case 1 [
t\geqslant\tau
]:

In this case,
\widetilde{Z}^{t+1}=Z^{(t+1)\wedge\tau}=Z^{\tau}
, and similarly,
\widetilde{Z}^{t}=Z^{t\wedge\tau}=Z^{\tau}
, so
|\widetilde{Z}^{t+1}-\widetilde{Z}^{t}|=0
.

Case 2 [
t<\tau
]:
In this case, we have
\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant 2g(\log(ng))^{2}
and by
5.3

(ii)
, we have that
|\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}|\leqslant 8c_{3}g(\log(ng))^{2}+2
. This implies that

|\widetilde{Z}^{t+1}-\widetilde{Z}^{t}|\leqslant|\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}|+\bigg|\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}-2g-1\bigg|\leqslant 8g(\log(ng))^{2}+2+(2g(\log(ng))^{2}-2g-1)\leqslant 10g(\log(ng))^{2}.

Combining the two cases above, we conclude that for all
t\geqslant t_{0}
,

|\widetilde{Z}^{t+1}-\widetilde{Z}^{t}|\leqslant 10g(\log(ng))^{2}.

Using Azuma’s inequality for super-martingales (
A.1
) for
\lambda=T
and
a_{i}=10g(\log(ng))^{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{Z}^{t_{1}+1}-\widetilde{Z}^{t_{0}}\geqslant T\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}}{2\cdot\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}(10g(\log(ng))^{2})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\left(-\frac{T^{2}}{\hat{c}\cdot T\cdot g^{-1}\cdot 200\cdot g^{2}\cdot(\log(ng))^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\exp\left(-\frac{T}{200\cdot\hat{c}\cdot g\cdot(\log(ng))^{4}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\exp\left(-\frac{ng^{2}}{200\cdot\hat{c}\cdot g\cdot(\log(ng))^{4}}\right)=(ng)^{-\omega(1)},

where in
(a)
we used that
T\geqslant ng^{2}
.
Hence, we conclude that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{Z}^{t_{1}+1}<\widetilde{Z}^{t_{0}}+T\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-\omega(1)}.

Thus by taking the union bound with
Eq.

5.4
we have

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z^{t_{1}+1}<Z^{t_{0}}+T\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

For the sake of a contradiction, assume now that more than an
(1-r)
fraction of the steps
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
satisfy
\Delta^{t}>Dng
. This implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}>Dg\cdot(1-r)\cdot(t_{1}-t_{0}+1)=D\cdot(1-r)\cdot\hat{c}\cdot T,

(5.5)

using that
t_{1}-t_{0}+1=\hat{c}\cdot T\cdot g^{-1}
.
When
\{Z^{t_{1}+1}<Z^{t_{0}}+T\}
and
\{\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T\}
hold, then we have

\Upsilon^{t_{1}+1}+\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}-(2g+1)\cdot(t_{1}-t_{0}+1)<\Upsilon^{t_{0}}+T\leqslant 2T.

By rearranging this leads to a contradiction as

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\Upsilon^{t_{1}+1}

\displaystyle<2T-\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+(2g+1)\cdot(t_{1}-t_{0}+1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}2T-\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+\hat{c}(2g+1)\cdot T\cdot g^{-1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}-\sum_{t=t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\frac{\Delta^{t}}{n}+5\hat{c}\cdot T

\displaystyle\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:g_adv_sum_of_abs_value}})}}{{<}}-D\cdot(1-r)\cdot\hat{c}\cdot T+5\hat{c}\cdot T

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}0

using in
(a)
that
t_{1}-t_{0}+1=\hat{c}\cdot T\cdot g^{-1}
, in
(b)
that
\hat{c}\geqslant 1
and
g\geqslant 1
, and in
(c)
that
D=\frac{5}{1-r}=365
(as
r=\frac{6}{6+1/12}
).

We conclude that when
\{Z^{t_{1}+1}<Z^{t_{0}}+T\}
holds, then at least an
r
fraction of the steps
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
satisfy
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
, and thus,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\geqslant r\cdot(t_{1}-t_{0}+1)\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}.\qed

The following lemma provides two ways of upper bounding the quadratic potential using the exponential potential
\Lambda
. These will be used in the recovery and stabilization lemmas, to obtain the starting point condition (
\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T
) for
5.6
. We defer its proof to
B.4
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Consider the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
for any constant
\alpha\in(0,1)
, any
g\geqslant 1
and any constant
c_{4}>0
. Then
(i)
for any constant
\hat{c}>0
, there exists a constant
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},\hat{c})\geqslant 1
, such that for any step
t\geqslant 0
with
\Lambda^{t}\leqslant\hat{c}\cdot n
,

\Upsilon^{t}\leqslant c_{s}\cdot ng^{2}.

Furthermore,
(ii)
there exists a constant
c_{r}:=c_{r}(\alpha,c_{4})\geqslant 1
, such that for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\Upsilon^{t}\leqslant c_{r}\cdot n\cdot\Big(g^{2}+(\log\Lambda^{t})^{2}\Big).

5.4
Exponential Potential

We now prove bounds on the expected change of the
\Lambda
potential function over one step. Note that these hold for any sufficiently small constant
\alpha>0
.

We start with a relatively weak bound which holds at any step.

Lemma 5.8
.

Consider any allocation process with probability allocation vector
r^{t}
such that
\max_{i\in[n]}r_{i}^{t}\leqslant\frac{2}{n}
. Further, consider the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big]
, any
g\geqslant 1
and any
c_{4}>0
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,
(i)
for every bin
i\in[n]
it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\Big).

Furthermore, by aggregating over all bins,
(ii)
it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\Big).

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary bin
i\in[n]
. We upper bound the change of the overloaded component, i.e.,
e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}
, by placing one ball in bin
i\in[n]
with probability
r_{i}^{t}\leqslant\frac{2}{n}
and ignoring the change of the average load. Also, we upper bound the change for the underloaded component, i.e.,

e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}
,
by considering only the change of the average load by
1/n
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot((1-r_{i}^{t})+r_{i}^{t}\cdot e^{\alpha})+e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot((1-r_{i}^{t})\cdot e^{\alpha/n}+r_{i}^{t}\cdot e^{\alpha/n})

\displaystyle=e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot(1+r_{i}^{t}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1))+e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot e^{\alpha/n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{2}{n}\cdot 1.5\alpha\Big)+e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1+1.5\frac{\alpha}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\Big),

using in
(a)
that
e^{u}\leqslant 1+1.5u
(for any
0\leqslant u\leqslant 0.7
),
\alpha\leqslant 1/2
and
r_{i}^{t}\leqslant\frac{2}{n}
. ∎

Now we improve this bound for any good step
t
, i.e., when
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
holds.

Lemma 5.9
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha\in\left(0,\frac{1}{18}\right]
,
c_{4}:=2D
and
D:=365
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,
(i)
for
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{12}
, it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng\,\right]\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{2\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big)+18\alpha.

Furthermore, this also implies that
(ii)
for
c:=\frac{18}{\varepsilon}
, it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng,\Lambda^{t}>cn\,\right]\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big).

Proof.

First statement.
Consider an arbitrary step
t\geqslant 0
with
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
. We bound the expected change of
\Lambda
over one step, by considering the following cases for each bin
i\in[n]
:

Case 1 [
y_{i}^{t}\in(-c_{4}g-2,c_{4}g+2)
]:
Using
5.8

(i)
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(\frac{\alpha}{6n}+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+(2\cdot e^{2\alpha})\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{6}+3\Big)\cdot\frac{\alpha}{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{18\alpha}{n},

using in
(a)
that
\Lambda_{i}^{t}\leqslant e^{2\alpha}+1\leqslant 2\cdot e^{2\alpha}
by the assumption that
y_{i}^{t}\in(-c_{4}g-2,c_{4}g+2)
and in
(b)
that
(2\cdot e^{2\alpha})\cdot(\frac{1}{6}+3)\leqslant 18
, since
\alpha\leqslant 1/4
.

Case 2 [
y_{i}^{t}\geqslant c_{4}g+2
]:
By the condition
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
, the number of bins
j
with
y_{j}^{t}\geqslant\frac{3}{2}Dg
is at most
\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{t}\cdot\frac{2}{3Dg}\leqslant\frac{Dng}{3Dg}=\frac{n}{3}
(see
Fig.

5.1
). We can allocate to bin
i\in[n]
with
y_{i}^{t}\geqslant c_{4}g+2=2Dg+2\geqslant\frac{3}{2}Dg+g
only if we sample bin
i
and a bin
j
with
y_{j}^{t}\geqslant\frac{3}{2}Dg
. Hence,

q_{i}^{t}\leqslant 2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{3}=\frac{2}{3n}.

\geqslant 2Dg
[\frac{3}{2}Dg,2Dg)
<\frac{3}{2}Dg
\leqslant n/3
\geqslant 2n/3
i
j

Figure 5.1
:
In any step
t
with
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
there are at most
\frac{n}{3}
bins with
y_{i}^{t}\geqslant\frac{3}{2}Dg
. In the
g
-Adv-Comp
, we can distinguish between the red and green bins, so the probability to allocate to a red bin is at most
\frac{2}{3n}
.

By assumption, bin
i
deterministically satisfies
y_{i}^{t+1}\geqslant c_{4}g+2-1/n>c_{4}g
, so

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big((1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot e^{-\alpha/n}+q_{i}^{t}\cdot e^{\alpha(1-1/n)}\Big)+1

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\left((1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}\Big)+q_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1+\alpha\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+\alpha^{2}\Big)\right)+1

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1+\alpha\cdot\Big(q_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+(1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}+q_{i}^{t}\cdot\alpha^{2}\Big)+1

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{3n}+\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{3n}\Big)+1

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+1

\displaystyle\qquad=e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+1\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{\alpha}{6n}

\displaystyle\qquad=\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{\alpha}{6n},

using in
(a)
that
e^{u}\leqslant 1+u+u^{2}
for
u<1.75
,
\alpha\leqslant 1
and
(1-1/n)^{2}\leqslant 1
, in
(b)
that
q_{i}^{t}\leqslant\frac{2}{3n}
and
(1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}\leqslant\frac{2\alpha^{2}}{3n}
for
n\geqslant 2
, and in
(c)
that
\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
, so
\frac{4\alpha^{2}}{3n}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{6n}
.

Case 3 [
y_{i}^{t}\leqslant-c_{4}g-2
]:

The number of bins
j
with
y_{j}^{t}\leqslant-\frac{3}{2}Dg
is at most
\frac{1}{2}\Delta^{t}\cdot\frac{2}{3Dg}\leqslant\frac{Dng}{3Dg}=\frac{n}{3}
and the number of bins
j
with
y_{j}^{t}>-\frac{3}{2}Dg
is at least
\frac{2n}{3}
(see
Fig.

5.2
). Similarly to Case 2, we can allocate to a bin
i\in[n]
with load
y_{i}^{t}\leqslant-c_{4}g-2
only if we sample
i
and a bin
j
with
y_{j}^{t}>-\frac{3}{2}Dg
. Hence,

q_{i}^{t}\geqslant 2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2}{3}=\frac{4}{3n}.

>-\frac{3}{2}Dg
(\text{-}2Dg,\text{-}\frac{3}{2}Dg]
\leqslant-2Dg
\geqslant 2n/3
\leqslant n/3
i
j

Figure 5.2
:
In any step
t
with
\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng
there are at most
n/3
bins with
y_{i}^{t}\leqslant-\frac{3}{2}Dg
and at least
2n/3
bins with
y_{i}^{t}>-\frac{3}{2}Dg
. In the
g
-Adv-Comp
, we can distinguish between the red and green bins, so the probability to allocate to a green bin is at least
\frac{4}{3n}
.

By assumption, bin
i
deterministically satisfies
y_{i}^{t+1}\leqslant-c_{4}g-2+1<-c_{4}g
, so

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=1+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big((1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot e^{\alpha/n}+q_{i}^{t}\cdot e^{-\alpha(1-1/n)}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}1+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\left((1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\Big(1+\frac{\alpha}{n}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}\Big)+q_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\alpha\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+\alpha^{2}\Big)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=1+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1+\alpha\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{n}-q_{i}^{t}\Big)+(1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}+q_{i}^{t}\cdot\alpha^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}1+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{3n}+\frac{5\alpha^{2}}{2n}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leqslant}}1+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad=\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+e^{\alpha(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{\alpha}{6n}

\displaystyle\qquad=\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{\alpha}{6n},

using in
(a)
that
e^{u}\leqslant 1+u+u^{2}
for
u<1.75
,
\alpha\leqslant 1
and
(1-1/n)^{2}\leqslant 1
, in
(b)
that
q_{i}^{t}\in[\frac{4}{3n},\frac{2}{n}]
and
(1-q_{i}^{t})\cdot\frac{\alpha^{2}}{n^{2}}\leqslant\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2n}
for
n\geqslant 2
and in
(c)
that
\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{15}
, so
\frac{5\alpha^{2}}{2n}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{6n}
.

Combining these three cases and letting
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{12}
, we conclude that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Lambda_{i}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha}{6n}\Big)+\frac{18\alpha}{n}\right)=\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{2\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big)+18\alpha.

Second statement.
Letting
c:=\frac{18}{\varepsilon}=18\cdot 12
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Lambda^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\Delta^{t}\leqslant Dng,\Lambda^{t}>cn\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{2\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big)+18\alpha

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big)-\Lambda^{t}\cdot\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}+18\alpha

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\Big).\qed

5.5
Adjusted Exponential Potential

In
5.9
, we proved that in a good step
t
with
\Lambda^{t}>cn
(for
c:=18\cdot 12
), the potential drops in expectation by a multiplicative factor. Our goal will be to show that w.h.p.
\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn
at a single step (
recovery
) and then show that it becomes small at least once every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps (
stabilization
). Since we do not have an expected drop in every step, but only at a constant fraction
r
of the steps, we will define an
adjusted exponential potential function
.
First, for any step
t_{0}
,
and any step
s\geqslant t_{0}
, we define the following event:

\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}:=\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},s]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}>cn\right\}.

Next, we define the sequence
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha,c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
as
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}:=\Lambda^{t_{0}}(\alpha,c_{4}g)
and, for any
s>t_{0}
,

\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s}:=\Lambda^{s}(\alpha,c_{4}g)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigg)\cdot\exp\bigg(+\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigg),

(5.6)

recalling that
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
is the number of good steps in
[t_{0},t_{1}]
, i.e., steps where the event
\mathcal{G}^{s}:=\{\Delta^{s}\leqslant Dng\}
holds, and
B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:=[t_{0},t_{1}]\setminus G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
is the number of bad steps in
[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

By its definition and
5.8
and
5.9
, it follows that the sequence
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}
is a super-martingale. A proof is given in
Section

B.2
.

Lemma 5.10

(
cf.
[
38
, Lemma 9.1]
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the sequence
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha,c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
for any starting step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
, any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{18}]
and
\varepsilon,c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. Then, for any step
s\geqslant t_{0}
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s}.

5.6
Recovery and Stabilization

We are now ready to prove the recovery lemma, that is the
\Lambda
potential becomes small every
\Theta(ng(\log(ng))^{2})
steps.

Recovery phase
Stabilization phase
4.3
cn
2cn
e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}
\Lambda^{t}
t
m-\Delta_{r}
s_{0}
\tau_{1}
s_{1}
\tau_{2}
s_{2}
m

5.11
Each
s_{i}-\tau_{i}\leqslant\Delta_{s}=\Theta(n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\})
by
5.12

Figure 5.3
:
Visualization of the recovery (
5.11
) and stabilization (
5.12
) phases. Note that the red intervals w.h.p. will have length
\leqslant\Delta_{s}
, implying
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
.

Lemma 5.11

(
Recovery
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{18}
, and
c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. Further, let the constants
c,\varepsilon>0
be as defined in
5.9
,
c_{r}:=c_{r}(\alpha,c_{4})\geqslant 1
as in
5.7

(ii)
,
r\in(0,1)
as in
5.6
and
c_{3}\geqslant 2
as in
4.3
. Then, for any step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
,
(i)
for
\Delta_{r}:=\Delta_{r}(g):=\frac{60c_{3}^{2}c_{r}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2}
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\}\;\right|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]\geqslant 1-3\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

Further,
(ii)
it holds that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.

Proof.

First statement.
Consider an arbitrary step
t_{0}
with
\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t_{0}}|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)
. Our aim is to show that w.h.p.
\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn
for some step
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]
. We will do this by first showing that w.h.p. there is a significant number of good steps, i.e.,
G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{r}
, and when this happens w.h.p.
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}=0
, which implies the conclusion.

We start by upper bounding
\Lambda^{t_{0}}
as follows,

\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{\alpha c_{3}g\log(ng)}\leqslant e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}=:\lambda,

since
\alpha\leqslant\frac{1}{18}
and
c_{3}\geqslant 2
. Hence, by
5.7

(ii)
, there exists a constant
c_{r}:=c_{r}(\alpha,c_{4})
, such that

\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant c_{r}\cdot n\cdot(g^{2}+(\log\Lambda^{t_{0}})^{2})\leqslant 2c_{r}\cdot n\cdot(c_{3}g\log(ng))^{2}=:T.

Let
t_{1}:=t_{0}+\Delta_{r}
. Applying
5.6
with
T=2c_{r}\cdot n\cdot(c_{3}g\log(ng))^{2}=o(n^{2}g^{3})
(and
\geqslant ng^{2}
) and
\hat{c}=\frac{\Delta_{r}\cdot g}{T}\geqslant\frac{30}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\geqslant 1
as
\alpha,\varepsilon,r\leqslant 1
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{r}\;\left|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{r}\;\left|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

(5.7)

By
5.10
,
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t})_{t\geqslant t_{0}}
is a super-martingale, so
\mathbf{E}[\,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}}\,]\leqslant\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}=\Lambda^{t_{0}}
. Hence, using Markov’s inequality we get
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}>\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda\,\right]\leqslant(ng)^{-12}
. Thus, by the definition of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
in
Eq.

5.6
, we have

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Lambda^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\right)\,\Bigg|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-12}.

(5.8)

Further, if in addition to the two events
\{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\}
and
\{\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda\}
, also the event
\{G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{r}\}
holds, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\bigg)
