\displaystyle\leqslant e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot(1-r)\cdot\Delta_{r}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot r\cdot\Delta_{r}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Delta_{r}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot r\cdot\Delta_{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle=e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Delta_{r}\bigg)

\displaystyle=e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\frac{60c_{3}^{2}c_{r}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{c_{3}g\log(ng)}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(-30c_{3}g\log(ng)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 1,

where we used in
(a)
that
r=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}
implies
\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot(1-r)=\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{6+\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}
, in
(b)
that
g\geqslant 1
,
c_{r}\geqslant 1
and
c_{3}\geqslant 1
.
By the definition of
\Lambda
, we have that
\Lambda^{t_{1}}\geqslant n
holds deterministically, and so we can deduce from the above inequality that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}=0
, that is,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\neg\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\;\Bigg|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\;\;\;\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12},\;\;\;\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda,\;\;\;G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{r}\,\right]=1.

Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}:=\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}-1]}\{\Lambda^{t}>cn\}
and taking the union bound over
Section

5.6
and
Eq.

5.8
yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\}\;\right|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant\lambda\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}-(ng)^{-12}=1-3\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

(5.9)

Second statement.
Using
4.3

(iii)
, for the constant
c_{3}\geqslant 2
, it holds that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-14}.

Hence, combining with
Eq.

5.9
, we conclude that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\}\,\right]\geqslant\left(1-3\cdot(ng)^{-12}\right)\cdot\left(1-(ng)^{-14}\right)\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.\qed

The derivation of the lemma below is similar to that of
5.11
, with the main difference being the tighter condition that
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn
, which allows us to choose a slightly shorter time window of
\Theta(n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\})
steps (see
Fig.

5.3
). We defer the proof for
B.5
.

Lemma 5.12

(
Stabilization
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{18}
, and
c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. Further, let the constants
c,\varepsilon>0
be as defined in
5.9
,
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},2c)\geqslant 1
as in
5.7

(i)
and
r\in(0,1)
as in
5.6
. Then, for
\Delta_{s}:=\Delta_{s}(g):=\frac{60c_{s}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}
, we have that for any step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{s}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}~\right|~\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.

5.7
Completing the Proof of Theorem
5.14

We will now prove that starting with
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn
implies that for any step
t_{1}\in[t_{0},t_{0}+(ng)^{2}]
, we have w.h.p.
\Gap(t_{1})=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
.

Lemma 5.13
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
, the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{18}
,
c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
and
\Delta_{s}>0
as defined in
5.12
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa\geqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}
such that for any steps
t_{0}\geqslant 0
and
t_{1}\in(t_{0},t_{0}+(ng)^{2}]
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\,\,\Big|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-9}.

Proof.

Consider any step
t_{0}
with
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn
. We define the event

\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}:=\left\{\text{for all }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\text{ there exists }s\in[t,t+\Delta_{s}]\text{ such that }\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn\right\},

that is, if
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds then we have
\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn
at least once every
\Delta_{s}:=\frac{60c_{s}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}
steps.

Assume now that
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds. We will show that

\max_{i\in[n]}\big|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\big|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n).

Choosing
t=t_{1}
, implies that there exists
s\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\Delta_{s}]
such that
\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn
, which in turn implies by definition of
\Lambda
that
\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{s}|\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\log(cn)+c_{4}g<\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\log n+c_{4}g
. Clearly, any
y_{i}^{t}
can decrease by at most
1/n
in each step, and from this it follows that if
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds, then

\max_{i\in[n]}y_{i}^{t_{1}}\leqslant\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{s}\right|+\frac{\Delta_{s}}{n}\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n),

for the constant

\displaystyle\kappa:=\frac{2}{\alpha}+c_{4}+\frac{\Delta_{s}}{n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}}=\frac{2}{\alpha}+c_{4}+\frac{60c_{s}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}>0.

(5.10)

If
t_{1}\geqslant t_{0}+\Delta_{s}
and
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds, then choosing
t=t_{1}-\Delta_{s}
, there exists
s\in[t_{1}-\Delta_{s},t_{1}]
such that
\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn
. (In case
t_{1}<t_{0}+\Delta_{s}
, then we arrive at the same conclusion by choosing
s=t_{0}
and using the precondition
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn
). This in turn implies

\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{s}|\leqslant\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\log n+c_{4}g.

Hence

\min_{i\in[n]}y_{i}^{t_{1}}\geqslant-\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{s}\right|-\frac{\Delta_{s}}{n}\geqslant-\kappa\cdot(g+\log n).

Hence,
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
together with the precondition on
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn
implies that
\max_{i\in[n]}\big|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\big|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)
. It remains to bound
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\neg\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]
.

Note that if for some step
j_{1}
we have that
\Lambda^{j_{1}}\leqslant cn
and for some
j_{2}\geqslant j_{1}
that
\Lambda^{j_{2}}>2cn
, then there must exist
j\in(j_{1},j_{2})
such that
\Lambda^{j}\in(cn,2cn]
, since for every
t\geqslant 0
it holds that
\Lambda^{t+1}\leqslant\Lambda^{t}\cdot e^{\alpha}\leqslant 2\Lambda^{t}
, as
\alpha\leqslant 1/2
.
Let
t_{0}<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots
and
t_{0}=:s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots
be two interlaced sequences defined recursively for
i\geqslant 1
by

\tau_{i}:=\inf\left\{\tau>s_{i-1}:\Lambda^{\tau}\in(cn,2cn]\right\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{i}:=\inf\left\{s>\tau_{i}:\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn\right\}.

Thus we have

t_{0}=s_{0}<\tau_{1}<s_{1}<\tau_{2}<s_{2}<\cdots,

and since
\tau_{i}>\tau_{i-1}
we have
\tau_{t_{1}-t_{0}}\geqslant t_{1}
. Therefore, if the event
\cap_{i=1}^{t_{1}-t_{0}}\{s_{i}-\tau_{i}\leqslant\Delta_{s}\}
holds, then also
\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds.

Recall that by
5.12
we have for any
i=1,2,\ldots,t_{1}-t_{0}
and any
\tau=t_{0}+1,\ldots,t_{1}

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[\tau_{i},\tau_{i}+\Delta_{s}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}~\right|~\mathfrak{F}^{\tau},\;\Lambda^{\tau}\in(cn,2cn],\tau_{i}=\tau\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11},

and by negating and the definition of
s_{i}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,s_{i}-\tau_{i}>\Delta_{s}\,\Big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{\tau},\Lambda^{\tau}\in(cn,2cn],\tau_{i}=\tau\,\right]\leqslant(ng)^{-11}.

Since the above bound holds for any
i\geqslant 1
and
\mathfrak{F}^{\tau}
, with
\tau_{i}=\tau
, it follows by the union bound over all
i=1,2,\ldots,t_{1}-t_{0}
, as
t_{1}-t_{0}\leqslant(ng)^{2}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\neg\mathcal{M}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\leqslant(t_{1}-t_{0})\cdot(ng)^{-11}\leqslant(ng)^{-9}.\qed

Finally, we deduce that for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting, for an arbitrary step
m
w.h.p.
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
.

Theorem 5.14
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
, the constant
\kappa\geqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}
defined in
Eq.

5.10
in
5.13
and
\alpha=\frac{1}{18}
. Then, for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-9}.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary step
m\geqslant 0
and recall that
\Delta_{r}:=\frac{60c_{3}^{2}c_{r}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2}
. If
m<\Delta_{r}
, then the claim follows by
5.13
as
\Lambda^{0}=2n\leqslant cn
and
\Delta_{r}<(ng)^{2}
.

Otherwise, let
t_{0}:=m-\Delta_{r}
. Firstly, by the recovery lemma (
5.11

(ii)
), we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.

(5.11)

Hence for
\tau:=\inf\{s\geqslant t_{0}\colon\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn\}
we have
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant m\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}
, as
t_{0}+\Delta_{r}=m
.

Secondly, using
5.13
, there exists a constant
\kappa:=\kappa(\alpha,\varepsilon)>0
such that for any step
s\in[t_{0},m]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)~\Big|~\mathfrak{F}^{s},\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-9}.

(5.12)

Combining the two inequalities from above, we conclude the proof

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{m}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)~\Big|~\tau=s\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau=s\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{s=t_{0}}^{m}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)~\Big|~\mathfrak{F}^{s},\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau=s\,\right]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:g_adv_stabilisation_minmax_gap}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-(ng)^{-9}\right)\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant m\,\right]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:g_adv_recovery_lambda}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-(ng)^{-9}\right)\cdot\left(1-(ng)^{-11}\right)\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-9}.\qed

6
Upper Bound of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for
g
-
Adv-Comp
with
g\leqslant\log n
: Outline

In this section, we will outline the proof for the upper bound on the gap of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting with
1<g\leqslant\log n
(which also implies for
g\in\{0,1\}
the
\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
gap bound by monotonicity). This matches the lower bound for the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process proven later in
11.3
up to multiplicative constants. The upper bound proof will be completed in
Sections

7
,
8
and
9
.

Theorem
9.2

(
Restated, page
9.2
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\in(1,\log n]
. Then, there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

6.1
Definitions of Super-Exponential Potential Functions

The proof of this theorem employs some kind of layered induction over
k
different, super-exponential potential functions. We will be using the following definition of super-exponential potentials.

The
super-exponential potential function
with smoothing parameter
\phi\geqslant 1
and integer offset
z:=z(n)>0
is defined at any step
t\geqslant 0
as

\displaystyle\Phi^{t}:=\Phi^{t}(\phi,z):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\phi\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-z)^{+}},

(6.1)

where
u^{+}:=\max\{u,0\}
.

These are similar to the potential functions used in
[
36
, Section 6]
, but with two extensions:
(i)
the potential functions are “smoother”, e.g., allowing us to deduce gap bounds between
\sqrt{\log n}
and
\log n
, and
(ii)
the concentration of the base potential in the layered induction follows from strengthening the stabilization theorem in
Section

5
.

Let

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}:=\frac{1}{6\kappa}\leqslant\frac{1}{6\cdot 18},

(6.2)

for
\kappa\geqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}=18>0
the constant in
Eq.

5.10
in
5.13
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}:=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{84}\leqslant\frac{1}{84\cdot 6\cdot 18}.

(6.3)

We define the function

f(k):=(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}=e^{\frac{1}{k}\cdot\log(\alpha_{1}\log n)},

which is monotone decreasing in
k>0
, and for
k=1
,
f(1)=\alpha_{1}\log n
. This implies that for every
1<g<\alpha_{1}\log n
, there exists a unique integer
k:=k(g)\geqslant 2
satisfying,

(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}.

This definition implies that
k=\Theta\big(\frac{\log\log n}{\log g}\big)
and that
k=\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
, since
g>1
.

Keeping in mind the previous inequality, we will be making the slightly stronger assumption for
g=\Omega(1)
(see
B.6
) that

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n))^{1/k}\leqslant g<\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\cdot(\log n)\right)^{1/(k-1)}.

(6.4)

For any
g
satisfying
\big(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\cdot(\log n)\big)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n))^{1/k}
, we will obtain the stated
\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\big)
bound by analyzing the
\widetilde{g}
-
Adv-Comp
setting for
\widetilde{g}=(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n))^{1/k}>g
, since

\frac{\widetilde{g}}{\log\widetilde{g}}\leqslant\frac{\widetilde{g}}{\log g}=\frac{\widetilde{g}}{g}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\leqslant\left(\frac{4\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{2}}\right)^{1/k}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{g}{\log g}\right).

We will now define the super-exponential potential functions
\Phi_{0},\ldots,\Phi_{k-1}
. The base potential function
\Phi_{0}
is just an exponential potential (i.e., has a constant smoothing parameter) defined as

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}^{s}:=\Phi_{0}^{s}(\alpha_{2},z_{0}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{0,i}^{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{0})^{+}\Big),

(6.5)

where
\alpha_{2}:=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{84}
and
z_{0}:=c_{5}\cdot g
for some sufficiently large constant integer
c_{5}>0
(to be defined in
Eq.

7.14
in
7.7
).
Further, we define for any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j}^{s}:=\Phi_{j}^{s}(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k},z_{j}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{j,i}^{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{j})^{+}\Big),

(6.6)

where the offsets are given by

\displaystyle z_{j}:=c_{5}\cdot g+\left\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\right\rceil\cdot j\cdot g.

(6.7)

Note that for most choices of
g
and
j
, the smoothing parameter is
\omega(1)
, motivating the term “super-exponential”. For
g=(\log n)^{1/k}
, the potential functions match in form those defined in
[
36
]
.

As concrete example, consider
g=(\log n)^{5/12}
, for which we have
k=3
and the potential functions are:

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{0})^{+}\Big),

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)^{2/12}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{1})^{+}\Big),

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{s}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)^{7/12}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{2})^{+}\Big).

When each of these potentials is
\mathcal{O}(n)
, this implies increasingly stronger bounds on the gap:
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
,
\mathcal{O}(2g+(\log n)^{10/12})
and finally
\mathcal{O}(3g+(\log n)^{5/12})=\mathcal{O}(g)
.

In general, we will employ this series of potential functions
(\Phi_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
to analyze the process over the time-interval
[m-n\log^{5}n,m]
, with the goal of eventually establishing an
\mathcal{O}(g\cdot k)
gap at time
m
. By definition of
k
, this will imply that
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\big)
.

6.2
The Layered Induction Argument

The next lemma (
9.3
) formalizes the inductive argument outlined in
Section

6.1
. It shows that if for all steps
s
within some suitable time-interval, the number of bins with load at least
z_{j}
is small, then the number of bins with load at least
z_{j+1}
is even smaller. This “even smaller” is captured by the (non-constant) base of
\Phi_{j}
, which increases in
j
; however, this comes at the cost of reducing the time-interval slightly by an additive
2n\log^{4}n
term.
Finally, for
j=k-1
, we conclude that at step
m
, there are no bins with load at least
z_{k}:=c_{5}\cdot g+\big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\big\rceil\cdot k\cdot g
, implying that
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
and establishing
9.2
.

Lemma 6.1

(
Induction step – Simplified version, page
9.3
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g=\Omega(1)
satisfying
(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}<g\leqslant(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
for some integer
k\geqslant 2
, for
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
Eq.

6.3
. Then, for any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and any step
m\geqslant 0
, if it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-2n(k-j+1)\cdot\log^{4}n,m]}\{\Phi_{j-1}^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8(j-1)}}{n^{4}},

then it also follows that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-2n(k-j)\cdot\log^{4}n,m]}\{\Phi_{j}^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8j}}{n^{4}}.

Base case.

The base case follows by strengthening the stabilization lemma for a variant of the
\Lambda
potential used in
Section

5
, so that the potential becomes
\mathcal{O}(n)
every
\mathcal{O}(ng)
steps (instead of
\Lambda
becoming
\mathcal{O}(n)
every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps which was proven in
5.12
). By making the constant
c_{5}>0
sufficiently large in the offset
c_{5}g
of the
\Phi_{0}
potential, we obtain concentration for
\Phi_{0}
and establish the base case for the layered induction.

Theorem
7.8

(
Base case – Simplified version, page
7.8
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\in[1,\mathcal{O}(\log n)]
. Then, for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Phi_{0}^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-4}.

6.3
Analysis of Super-Exponential Potential Functions

In order to prove the induction step (
9.3
), we will need to prove concentration for super-exponential potentials. We will now outline the proof of this and the challenges involved.

For super-exponential potentials, unlike the hyperbolic cosine potential, there exist load configurations, where a super-exponential potential may increase in expectation. We will show that in each step where the probability to allocate to a bin with load at least
z-1
is sufficiently small, the potential function
\Phi:=\Phi(\phi,z)
drops in expectation over one step. More specifically, we show that the following event is sufficient:

\mathcal{K}^{s}:=\mathcal{K}_{\phi,z}^{s}(q^{s}):=\left\{\forall i\in[n]\colon\ y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z-1\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ q_{i}^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}\right\},

where
q^{s}
is the probability allocation vector used by the process at step
s
.

We prove the following drop inequality in
Section

8
.

Lemma 6.1

(
Restated, page
8.2
)
.

Consider any allocation process and any super-exponential potential
\Phi:=\Phi(\phi,z)
with
\phi\in[4,n]
. For any step
s\geqslant 0
where
\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds, we have that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+2.

Using this drop inequality we are able to prove the following concentration lemma for super-exponential potentials.

Theorem
8.8

(
Restated, page
8.8
)
.

Consider any allocation process for which there exist super-exponential potential functions
\Phi_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\phi_{1},z)
and
\Phi_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\phi_{2},z)
with integer offset
z:=z(n)>0
and smoothing parameters
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in(0,(\log n)/6]
with
\phi_{2}\leqslant\frac{\phi_{1}}{84}
, such that they satisfy for any step
s\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{1}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{1}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

where
\mathcal{K}^{s}:=\mathcal{K}_{\phi_{1},z}^{s}
. Further, let
P\in[n^{-4},1]
. Then, for any steps
t\geqslant 0
and
\widetilde{t}\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
, which satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left\{\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n\right\}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\geqslant 1-P,

they must also satisfy

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

The statement of this theorem concerns steps in
[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
with
\widetilde{t}\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
. The interval
[t,\widetilde{t}]
is the
stabilization interval
, i.e., the interval where we want to show that
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n
for every
s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]
. The interval
[t-2n\log^{4}n,t]
is the
recovery
interval where we will show that w.h.p.
\Phi_{2}
becomes
\mathcal{O}(n)
at least
once
,
provided we start with a “good”
\mathcal{O}(\log^{2}n)
gap at step
t-2n\log^{4}n
. For both the recovery and stabilization intervals we will condition that the event
\mathcal{K}
holds at every step.

Using
8.8
to prove
9.3
.

In the concentration lemma (
8.8
), the potentials
(\Phi_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
will take the role of
\Phi_{2}
and the role of
\Phi_{1}
will be taken by the potentials
(\Psi_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1},
defined as
(\Phi_{j})_{j=0}^{k-1}
but with a smoothing parameter
\alpha_{1}
that is a constant factor larger, i.e.,
\alpha_{1}:=84\cdot\alpha_{2}
. More specifically,

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},z_{0}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{0,i}^{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{1}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{0})^{+}\Big),

(6.8)

and for
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{j}^{s}:=\Psi_{j}^{s}(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k},z_{j}):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{j,i}^{s}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\left(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{j})^{+}\right).

(6.9)

For the induction step (
9.3
), it trivially follows that when
\Phi_{j-1}^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
, then we also have that
\Gap(t)\leqslant\log^{2}n
and the following lemma establishes that the event
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{t}
(associated with the drop of
\Phi_{j}
and
\Psi_{j}
) also holds.

Lemma 6.2

(Simplified version, page
9.1
)
.

For any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and any step
s\geqslant 0
such that
\Phi_{j-1}^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
holds, then also
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{s}
holds.

Hence, combining this lemma (
9.1
) with the super-exponential potential drop (
8.2
), the preconditions of super-exponential concentration lemma (
8.8
) are satisfied and so layered induction step (
9.3
) follows.

Road map.

The proof of
9.2
is split into three parts. In
Section

7
, we strengthen the analysis of
Section

5
to establish the base case (
7.8
). In
Section

8
, we prove the general concentration for super-exponential potentials (
8.8
). Finally, in
Section

9
, we use this concentration theorem to prove the layered induction step (
9.3
) and complete the proof for the bound on the gap (
9.2
).

7
Upper Bound of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for
g
-
Adv-Comp
with
g\leqslant\log n
: Base Case

In this section, we will obtain for the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting a stronger guarantee for a variant of the
\Lambda
potential used in
Section

5
. The precise upper bound that we need on
g
is
g\leqslant c_{6}\log n
, where
c_{6}>0
is a sufficiently small constant defined as

\displaystyle c_{6}:=\frac{r}{9\cdot 20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}\leqslant 1,

(7.1)

where
r\in(0,1)
is as defined in
5.6
,
\widetilde{c}_{s}:=\widetilde{c}_{s}(\alpha_{1},c_{4},e^{2\alpha_{1}}c)\geqslant 1
as defined in

5.7
(for
c_{4}:=730
),
c>0
as defined in
5.9
and
\alpha_{1}:=\frac{1}{6\kappa}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{6}
, for
\kappa>0
the constant defined in
Eq.

5.10
and
\alpha:=\frac{1}{18}
used in
Section

5
.

In
Section

5
, we showed that w.h.p.
\Lambda^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
at least
once
every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps. Here, we will show that w.h.p. for any step
t
, we have for
all
steps
s\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
that
\Psi_{0}^{s}=\mathcal{O}(n)
. This will serve as the base case for the layered induction in
Section

9
.

We start by defining the potential function
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
which is a variant of the
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
potential function (defined in
Eq.

5.1
), with the same offset
c_{4}g=2Dg=730g
, but with a smaller smoothing parameter
\alpha_{1}\leqslant\frac{\alpha}{6}
,

\displaystyle V^{t}:=V^{t}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}+e^{\alpha_{1}(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\right].

(7.2)

In
Section

5
, we proved that w.h.p. every
\mathcal{O}(n\cdot(g+\log n))
steps the potential
\Lambda
satisfies
\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn
. In this section, we will strengthen this to show that every
\mathcal{O}(ng)
steps (for
g=\mathcal{O}(\log n)
) the potential
V
satisfies
V^{s}\leqslant e^{\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}g)}\cdot n
. By
7.3
, this implies the base case of the layered induction in
Section

9
, i.e., that for
all
steps
s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]
,
\Psi_{0}^{s}\leqslant Cn
for
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1
and recalling that
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
for some sufficiently large constant
c_{5}>0
(to be defined in
Eq.

7.14
in
7.7
) is given by

\Psi_{0}^{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{1}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z_{0})^{+}\Big)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\exp\Big(\alpha_{1}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-c_{5}g)^{+}\Big).

The proof follows along the lines of
5.14
in
Section

5
, but it further conditions on the gap being
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
at every step of the analysis. In particular, by conditioning on
\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)
, we obtain that
|\Delta V^{t+1}|=\mathcal{O}(n^{1/3})
(
7.4
), which allows us to apply Azuma’s inequality (
A.1
) to deduce that w.h.p.
V
remains small. This bounded difference condition is similar to the one used in
[
36
, Proof of Theorem 5.3]
.

7.1
A Modified Process

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the process in the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting (with arbitrary
1\leqslant g\leqslant c_{6}\log n
) that we want to analyze. We would like to condition on the event that
\mathcal{P}
satisfies
\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)
, for every step
t
in an interval of
2n\log^{5}n
steps, which holds w.h.p., as implied by
5.14
.

We implement this conditioning by defining a
modified process

\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}:=\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}(\mathcal{P})
for the same
g
and some arbitrary step
r_{0}
. Consider the stopping time
\sigma:=\inf\{s\geqslant r_{0}:\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{s}|>\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\}
, then the process
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
is defined so that

•

in steps
s\in[0,\sigma)
makes the same allocations as
\mathcal{P}
, and

•

in steps
s\in[\sigma,\infty)
allocates to the currently least loaded bin, i.e., it uses the probability allocation vector
r^{s}=(0,0,\ldots,0,1)
.

Let
y_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}
be the normalized load vector of
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
at step
s\geqslant 0
. By
5.14
, it follows that for any interval
[r_{0},m]
with
m-r_{0}\leqslant n^{2}
, with high probability the two processes agree

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[r_{0},m]}\left\{y_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}=y^{s}\right\}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[r_{0},m]}\left\{\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{s}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)\right\}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-9}\cdot n^{2}\geqslant 1-2n^{-7}.

(7.3)

The process
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
is defined in a way to satisfy the following property:

•

(
Property 1
) The
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process satisfies the drop inequalities for the potential functions
\Lambda_{\mathcal{Q}}
,
V_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{Q}}
(Lemmas
5.5
,
5.8

(ii)
and
5.9
) for any step
s\geqslant 0
. This holds because for any step
s<\sigma
, the process follows
\mathcal{P}
and so it is an instance of the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting. For any step
s\geqslant\sigma
, the process allocates to the currently least loaded bin and therefore minimizes the potential
\Lambda_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s+1}
given any
\mathfrak{F}^{s}
,
which means that
\Lambda_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s+1}\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,\right]
and so it trivially satisfies any drop inequality (and similarly for
V_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{Q}}
).

Further, we define the event that the maximum normalized load in absolute value is small at step
r_{0}
as,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}:=\left\{\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{\mathcal{Q},i}^{r_{0}}\right|\leqslant\min\left\{\kappa\cdot(g+\log n),c_{3}g\log(ng)\right\}\right\},

(7.4)

where
c_{3}\geqslant 2
is the constant defined in
Eq.

4.6
. We are primarily interested in the
\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)
bound on the gap and the second bound is only needed for very small values of
g=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
When the event
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}
holds, then the process
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
also satisfies the following property (which “implements” the conditioning that the gap is
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
):

•

(
Property 2
) For any step
s\geqslant r_{0}
, it follows that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{s}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)+1\leqslant 2\kappa\log n,

using that
g\leqslant c_{6}\log n
with
c_{6}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
by
Eq.

7.1
.
At any step
s\in[r_{0},\sigma)
, this holds by the definition of
\sigma
. For any step
s\geqslant\sigma
, a ball will never be allocated to a bin with
y_{i}^{s}>0
and in every
n
steps the at most
n
bins with load equal to the minimum load (at step
s
) will receive at least one ball each. Hence, over any
n
steps the maximum absolute normalized load does not increase and in the steps in between this can be larger by at most
1
.

7.2
Preliminaries

We now define the adjusted potential
\widetilde{V}
, analogously to
\widetilde{\Lambda}
in
Eq.

5.6
. Note that
5.9
with constants
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{12},c=12\cdot 18
also applies to the potential
V
, since
V
has the same form as
\Lambda
but a smaller smoothing parameter
\alpha_{1}\leqslant\alpha
. Next, we define the sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
as
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}:=V^{t_{0}}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
and, for any
s>t_{0}
,

\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}:=V^{s}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigg)\cdot\exp\bigg(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigg),

(7.5)

where
G_{t}^{s-1}
(and
B_{t}^{s-1}
) is the number of good (bad) steps in
[t_{0},s-1]
(as defined in
Section

5.5
) and

\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s}(V,c):=\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},s]}\left\{V^{t}>cn\right\}.

Similarly, to
\widetilde{\Lambda}
in
Section

5
, we have that
\widetilde{V}
is a super-martingale.

Lemma 7.1

(
cf.
5.10
)
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\geqslant 1
, any step
r_{0}\geqslant 0
, the sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
for any
t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
with
\alpha_{1}>0
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
\varepsilon,c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. For any step
s\geqslant t_{0}
, we have that,

\mathbf{E}[\,\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,]\leqslant\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
5.10
, by substituting
\Lambda
with
V
and
\widetilde{\Lambda}
with
\widetilde{V}
. The drop inequalities follow from
5.8
and
5.9
, since
V
has the same form as
\Lambda
and a smaller smoothing parameter
\alpha_{1}\leqslant\alpha
. The process
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
also satisfies the drop inequalities by Property 1 (see
•
‣
Section

7.1
).
∎

We defer the proofs of the next two lemmas to
B.7
. The first one is a simple smoothness argument for the potential
V
defined in
Eq.

7.2
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Consider the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha_{1}>0
, any
c_{4}>0
and any
g\geqslant 1
. Then,
(i)
for any step
t\geqslant 0
, we have that

e^{-\alpha_{1}}\cdot V^{t}\leqslant V^{t+1}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V^{t}.

Further,
(ii)
for any
\hat{c}>0
, for any integer
T>0
and any step
t\geqslant 0
, for which there exist steps
s_{0}\in[t-T,t]
and
s_{1}\in[t,t+T]
, such that
V^{s_{0}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
and
V^{s_{1}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
, we have that

V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot 2\hat{c}n.

The following lemma shows that by choosing a large enough offset
c_{5}>0
in the potential
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
(defined in
Eq.

6.8
), when
V^{t}=e^{\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}g)}\cdot cn
, then
\Psi_{0}^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Consider any
c,\hat{c}>0
and the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha_{1}>0
, any
c_{4}>0
and any
g\geqslant 1
. Further, consider the potential
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
with offset
c_{5}:=2\cdot\max\{c_{4},\hat{c}\}
and
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
with

V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\hat{c}\cdot g}\cdot 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
, it holds that

\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn.

In comparison to
Section

5
, where we proved stabilization over an interval of length
\Omega(n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\})
, here we will be using a shorter interval of length

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}:=\frac{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon r}\cdot ng,

(7.6)

where constants
\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{12},r:=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}>0
are as defined in
Section

5
and
\widetilde{c}_{s}:=\widetilde{c}_{s}(\alpha_{1},c_{4},e^{2\alpha_{1}}c)\geqslant 1
is defined in
5.7
.

We now prove the bounded difference condition for the
\widetilde{V}
potential. This follows from the second property of
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
that the maximum normalized load in absolute value is
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
for any step
s\geqslant r_{0}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
with
c_{6}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.1
, any step
r_{0}\geqslant 0
, and
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}
as defined in
Eq.

7.4
. Further, consider the sequence
(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
for any step
t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
with
\alpha_{1}>0
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
\varepsilon,c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
.
Then, for any step
s\geqslant t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
we have that
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}=0
or

\left(\left|\Delta\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}\right|\mid\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{s}\right)\leqslant 16\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{1/3}.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary step
s\geqslant r_{0}
and assume that the event
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}
holds. By Property 2 of the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process (see
Section

7.1
), we have that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{s}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)+1,

which also implies for
c_{4}:=730>0
, since
g\leqslant c_{6}\log n\leqslant\log n
and
\kappa>1
that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}\left\{(y_{i}^{s}-c_{4}g)^{+},(-y_{i}^{s}-c_{4}g)^{+}\right\}\leqslant\kappa\cdot(g+\log n)+1-c_{4}g\leqslant 2\kappa\log n.

(7.7)

We will now show that
|\Delta V^{s+1}|\leqslant 5n^{1/3}
and then use this to bound
|\Delta\widetilde{V}^{s+1}|
. By
Eq.

7.7
for any bin
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle V_{i}^{s}\leqslant 2\cdot e^{2\alpha_{1}\kappa\log n}=2\cdot e^{\frac{1}{3}\cdot\log n}=2n^{1/3},

using that
\alpha_{1}:=\frac{1}{6\kappa}
.
Hence, by aggregating over all bins,
V^{s}\leqslant 2n^{4/3}
. Furthermore, if the ball at step
s+1
is allocated to bin
j\in[n]
, then

\Delta V^{s+1}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}/n}\cdot V^{s}+e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V_{j}^{s}-V^{s}\leqslant\frac{2\alpha_{1}}{n}V^{s}+2\cdot 2n^{1/3}\leqslant\frac{2\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot(2n^{4/3})+4n^{1/3}\leqslant 5n^{1/3},

using that
e^{\alpha_{1}/n}\leqslant 1+2\cdot\frac{\alpha_{1}}{n}
and
e^{\alpha_{1}}\leqslant 2
, which both hold as
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1/4
. Similarly,

\Delta V^{s+1}\geqslant e^{-\alpha_{1}/n}\cdot V^{s}-e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V_{j}^{s}-V^{s}\geqslant-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{n}V^{s}-2\cdot 2n^{1/3}\geqslant-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot(2n^{4/3})-4n^{1/3}\geqslant-5n^{1/3},

using that
e^{-\alpha_{1}/n}\geqslant 1-\frac{\alpha_{1}}{n}
and
e^{\alpha_{1}}\leqslant 2
, as
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1/4
.

Now, we turn to upper bounding
|\Delta\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}|
by proving lower and upper bounds on
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}
. If
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}=0
, then the conclusion follows. Otherwise, since
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}>0
, we have that
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s}}=\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}=1
, so by definition of
\widetilde{V}
in
Eq.

7.5
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}=V^{s}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\leqslant 2\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{4/3},

(7.8)

using that
G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\leqslant s-t_{0}
and
V^{s}\leqslant 2n^{4/3}
.

Now, we upper bound
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}
, recalling that
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{s}}=1
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}

\displaystyle=V^{s+1}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant(V^{s}+5n^{1/3})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle\leqslant(V^{s}+5n^{1/3})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\Big)+5\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}\cdot\Big(1+\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\Big)+5\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot 2

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}+(2\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{4/3})\cdot\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}+10\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}+16\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{1/3},

(7.9)

using in
(a)
that
e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon/n}\leqslant 1+\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}
as
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1
and
\varepsilon=\frac{1}{12}
,

G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\leqslant s-t_{0}
,
and
e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon/n}\leqslant 2
and in
(b)
using
Eq.

7.8
.

Similarly, we lower bound
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}

\displaystyle=V^{s+1}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle\geqslant(V^{s}-5n^{1/3})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}(V^{s}-5n^{1/3})\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\Big)-5\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\Big)-5\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geqslant}}\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}-(2\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{4/3})\cdot\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}-5\cdot n^{1/3}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}

\displaystyle\geqslant\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}-16\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{1/3},

(7.10)

using in
(a)
that
V^{s}\geqslant 2n\geqslant 5n^{1/3}
holds deterministically, in
(b)
that
e^{-3\alpha_{1}/n}\geqslant 1-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}
and
G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\leqslant s-t_{0}
and in
(c)
using
Eq.

7.8
.

Hence, combining the two upper bounds in
Eq.

7.9
and
Eq.

7.10
, we conclude that
|\Delta\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}|\leqslant 16\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{s-t_{0}}{n}}\cdot n^{1/3}
.
∎

7.3
Strong Stabilization

We will now prove the following slightly stronger version of
5.12
, meaning that stabilization is over intervals of length
\Theta(ng)
instead of
\Theta(n\cdot(g+\log n))
.

Lemma 7.5

(
Strong Stabilization
)
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
for
c_{6}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.1
, any step
r_{0}\geqslant 0
and
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}
as defined in
Eq.

7.4
. Then, for the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
with
\alpha_{1}>0
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
,
c_{4},c>0
as defined in
5.9
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.6
, it holds that for any step
t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{s\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]}\left\{V^{s}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\,\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{t_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-11}.

Proof.

The proof of this lemma proceeds similarly to that of
5.12
, but we will apply Azuma’s inequality for
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}
instead of Markov’s inequality. However, we cannot directly apply concentration to
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}
because the bounded difference condition (
7.4
) holds only when
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}
is positive. So instead we apply it to a stopped random variable
X_{t_{0}}^{s}
to be defined in a way that ensures it is always positive.

Let
t_{1}:=t_{0}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
. We define the stopping time
\tau:=\inf\{s\geqslant t_{0}\colon V^{s}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn\}
and for any
s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
,

X_{t_{0}}^{s}:=\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s\wedge\tau}.

We will now verify that
X_{t_{0}}^{s}>0
for all
s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
. Firstly, consider any
s<\tau
. Since
V^{s}>e^{\alpha_{1}}cn
, by
7.2

(i)
, we have that
V^{s+1}\geqslant V^{s}\cdot e^{-\alpha_{1}}>cn
and hence
X_{t_{0}}^{s+1}=\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}>0
. Secondly, for any
s\geqslant\tau
, it trivially holds that
X_{t_{0}}^{s+1}=X_{t_{0}}^{s}>0
.

We proceed to verify the preconditions of Azuma’s inequality for super-martingales (
A.1
) for the sequence
(X_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]}
. Firstly, using
7.1
it forms a super-martingale, i.e., that
\mathbf{E}[\,X_{t_{0}}^{s}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\,]\leqslant X_{t_{0}}^{s-1}
. Secondly, by
7.4
, since
X_{t_{0}}^{s}>0
, for any filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}
where
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}
holds, we have that

\displaystyle\left(\bigl|X_{t_{0}}^{s}-X_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigr|\mid\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\bigl|\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s}-\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\bigr|\mid\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 16\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon\cdot\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}{n}}\cdot n^{1/3}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}(16\cdot e^{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot r^{-1}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})g})\cdot n^{1/3}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}16\cdot e^{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot r^{-1}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})\cdot c_{6}\log n}\cdot n^{1/3}

\displaystyle=16\cdot n^{4/9},

using in
(a)
that
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}:=\frac{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon r}\cdot ng
and in
(b)
that
g\leqslant c_{6}\log n
and
c_{6}:=\frac{r}{9\cdot 20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}
.

Hence, applying
A.1
for
\lambda:=n
,
N:=\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
and
a_{i}:=16\cdot n^{4/9}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.X_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\geqslant X_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}+n~\,\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{t_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{n^{2}}{2\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\cdot(16\cdot n^{4/9})^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)}.

(7.11)

As we condition on
\{V^{t_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\}
, we have that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{4}g+\frac{\log(e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn)}{\alpha_{1}}\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2},

for sufficiently large
n
, using that
\alpha_{1},c_{5},c>0
are constants. Further, by
5.7

(i)
(since
V
has the same form as
\Lambda
), we have that
\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant\widetilde{c}_{s}ng^{2}
, for some constant
\widetilde{c}_{s}:=\widetilde{c}_{s}(\alpha_{1},c_{4},e^{2\alpha_{1}}c)\geqslant 1
.

Applying
5.6
for
T:=\widetilde{c}_{s}ng^{2}
(since
T\in[ng^{2},o(n^{2}g^{3})]
) and
\hat{c}:=\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\cdot g}{T}\geqslant\frac{20}{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon r}\geqslant 1
(since
\alpha_{1},\varepsilon,r\leqslant 1
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\;\Big|\;\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{t_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\;\Big|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\geqslant 1-2\cdot n^{-12}.

(7.12)

Taking the union bound over
Section

7.3
and
Eq.

7.12
, we have

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left\{X_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}<X_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}+n\right\}\cap\left\{G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\right\}\;\Big|\;\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{t_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-11}.

Assume that
\{X_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}<X_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}+n\}
and
\{G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\}
hold. Then, we consider two cases based on whether the stopping was reached.

Case 1 [
\tau\leqslant t_{1}
]:
Here, clearly there is an
s\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
, namely
s=\tau
, such that
V^{s}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn
and the conclusion follows.

Case 2 [
\tau>t_{1}
]:
Here, using that
\{\tau>t_{1}\}
and
\{X_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}<X_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}+n\}
both hold, it follows that

\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}<\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}+n,

and so, by definition of
\widetilde{V}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
(
Eq.

7.5
),

V^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\bigg)\cdot\exp\bigg(+\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\bigg)<V^{t_{0}}+n.

By re-arranging and using that
\{G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\}
holds, we have that

\displaystyle V^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}

\displaystyle<(V^{t_{0}}+n)\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}-\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant(e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn+n)\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot(1-r)\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}-\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot cn\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}-\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot r\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot cn\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot cn\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\frac{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon r}\cdot ng\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot cn\cdot\exp\left(-10\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leqslant}}n/2,

where we used in
(a)
that
r=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}
implies
\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot(1-r)=\frac{3\alpha_{1}}{n}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{6+\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}
and
c\geqslant 1
, in
(b)
that
g\geqslant 1
,
c\geqslant 1
and
\widetilde{c}_{s}\geqslant 1
and in
(c)
that
c=18\cdot 12
. Since deterministically we have that
V^{t_{1}}\geqslant n
, it must be that
\mathbf{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}=0
, implying that there exists
s\in[t_{0},t_{1})
such that
V^{s}\leqslant cn\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn
.
∎

We will now show that the potential
V
becomes small every
\Theta(ng)
steps. The proof, given in
B.9
, proceeds similarly to the proof of
5.13
.

Lemma 7.6
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
for
c_{6}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.1
and any step
r_{0}\geqslant 0
. Then, for the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
with
\alpha_{1}
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
,
c_{4},c>0
as defined in
5.9
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.6
, it holds that for any step
t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
and
t_{1}
such that
t_{0}<t_{1}\leqslant t_{0}+2n\log^{5}n
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]}\bigcup_{s\in[t,t+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]}\left\{V^{s}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9},

7.4
Completing the Proof of Theorem
7.8

In this section we will complete the base case proof using the stronger stabilization for the
V
potential (see
7.5
) and upper bounding
\Psi_{0}
using
V
(see
7.3
). We will first prove the result for the modified process and then relate the results to the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting.

Lemma 7.7
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
and any step
r_{0}\geqslant-\Delta_{r}-n\log^{5}n
, where
c_{6}>0
is as defined in
Eq.

7.1
and
\Delta_{r}:=\Delta_{r}(g)>0
is as in
5.11
. Further, let
c>0
be as defined in
5.9
and
\alpha_{1}>0
as in
Eq.

6.2
, then for the constant
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1\geqslant 8
and the potential
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
with the constant integer
c_{5}>0
(to be defined in
Eq.

7.14
), we have that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-8}.

Proof.

This proof proceeds similarly to that of
5.14
having a recovery and a stabilization phase to show that the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
stabilizes at
\leqslant e^{\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{1}g)}\cdot cn
. By
7.3
, this implies that
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
stabilizes at
\leqslant Cn
for sufficiently large constants
c_{5},C>0
.

For the recovery phase, we will use the potential function
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
defined in
Eq.

5.1
. More specifically, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[r_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\left\{V^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[r_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[r_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{r_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geqslant}}1-3\cdot(ng)^{-12}\geqslant 1-n^{-11},

using in
(a)
that
V^{t}\leqslant\Lambda^{t}
for any step
t\geqslant 0
, as
V
and
\Lambda
have the same form, but
V
has a smoothing parameter
\alpha_{1}<\alpha
, in
(b)
recalling that
\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}:=\{\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{r_{0}}\right|\leqslant\min\{\kappa\cdot(g+\log n),c_{3}g\log(ng)\}\}
for
c_{3}\geqslant 2
the constant defined in
Eq.

4.6
and in
(c)
applying
5.11

(i)
.

Therefore, for the stopping time
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant r_{0}\colon V^{t}\leqslant cn\}
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant r_{0}+\Delta_{r}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-11}.

(7.13)

Consider any
t_{0}\in[r_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}]
(
t_{0}
will play the role of a concrete value of
\tau
). By
7.6
(for
t_{0}:=t_{0}
and
t_{1}:=r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n
, since
t_{1}-t_{0}\leqslant n\log^{5}n+\Delta_{r}\leqslant 2n\log^{5}n
), we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\bigcup_{s\in[t,t+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]}\left\{V^{s}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9}.

When the above event holds, then for every
t\in[t_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]
, there exists
s_{0}\in[t-\widetilde{\Delta}_{s},t]
and
s_{1}\in[t,t+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]
such that
V^{s_{0}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
and
V^{s_{1}}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
, using that for any
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]
we can set
s_{0}=t_{0}
since by the conditioning
V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
. So, by
7.2

(ii)
(for
\hat{c}:=e^{2\alpha_{1}}c
and
\Delta:=\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
), it follows that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}{n}}\cdot 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9}.

Next, adjusting the range of the big intersection using that
t_{0}\leqslant r_{0}+\Delta_{r}
, it follows that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}{n}}\cdot 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9}.

By
7.3

(for
\hat{c}:=\big\lceil\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}{ng}\big\rceil
), we conclude that for
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
with constant integer

\displaystyle c_{5}:=2\cdot\max\left\{c_{4},\left\lceil\frac{\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}}{ng}\right\rceil\right\}=2\cdot\max\left\{c_{4},\left\lceil\frac{20\cdot\widetilde{c}_{s}\cdot\log(2ce^{2\alpha_{1}})}{\alpha_{1}\varepsilon r}\right\rceil\right\},

(7.14)

and for the constant
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9}.

(7.15)

Finally,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\geqslant\sum_{t_{0}=r_{0}}^{r_{0}+\Delta_{r}}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},r_{0}+\Delta_{r}+n\log^{5}n]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}~\right|~\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\tau=t_{0}\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau=t_{0}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\geqslant\sum_{t_{0}=r_{0}}^{r_{0}+\Delta_{r}}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[r_{0}+\Delta_{r},m]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau=t_{0}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:g_adv_psi_0_concentration}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-n^{-9}\right)\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant r_{0}+\Delta_{r}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:g_adv_stopping_time_for_v_recovery}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-n^{-9}\right)\cdot\left(1-n^{-11}\right)\geqslant 1-n^{-8},

This concludes the claim for
r_{0}\geqslant 0
.

If
r_{0}<0
, then deterministically
\tau=0
, since
V^{\tau}=n\leqslant cn
. Hence, the rest of the proof follows for an interval of length at most
2n\log^{5}n
.
∎

Theorem 7.8

(
Base case
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
, where
c_{6}>0
is as defined in
Eq.

7.1
. For constant
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1\geqslant 8
with
\alpha_{1}>0
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
c>0
as in
5.9
, and the potential
\Phi_{0}:=\Phi_{0}(\alpha_{2},c_{5}g)
with
\alpha_{2}>0
as in
Eq.

6.3
, and constant integer
c_{5}>0
as in
Eq.

7.14
, we have that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Phi_{0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-4}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the original process in the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting. Consider the modified process
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
for
r_{0}:=m-n\log^{5}n-\Delta_{r}
where
\Delta_{r}:=\Theta(ng\cdot(\log(ng))^{2})
is as defined in
5.11
. Let
y_{\mathcal{Q}}
be its normalized load vector and
\Psi_{\mathcal{Q},0}
be its
\Psi_{0}
potential function. Recall from
Section

7.1
that the two processes
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
and
\mathcal{P}
agree at every step w.h.p., since
m-r_{0}\leqslant 2n\log^{5}n
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[r_{0},m]}\left\{y_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}=y^{s}\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-2n^{-7}.

(7.16)

Taking the union bound of the conclusions in
5.14
and
4.3

(iii)
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]=\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{r_{0}}\right|\leqslant\min\{\kappa\cdot(g+\log n),c_{3}g\log(ng)\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-3\cdot(ng)^{-9}.

(7.17)

By
7.7
we have that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Psi_{\mathcal{Q},0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{r_{0}},\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-8}.

By combining with
Eq.

7.17
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Psi_{\mathcal{Q},0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant\left(1-n^{-8}\right)\cdot\left(1-3\cdot(ng)^{-9}\right)\geqslant 1-n^{-7}.

Taking the union bound with
Eq.

7.16
, we get that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[r_{0},m]}\left\{y_{\mathcal{Q}}^{s}=y^{s}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Psi_{\mathcal{Q},0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-7}-2n^{-7}\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

When this event holds we have that
\Psi_{\mathcal{Q},0}^{s}=\Psi_{0}^{s}
for every step
s\in[r_{0},m]
and hence we can deduce for the original process
\mathcal{P}
that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[m-n\log^{5}n,m]}\left\{\Psi_{0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

Finally, since
\alpha_{2}\leqslant\alpha_{1}
, we have that
\Phi_{0}^{s}\leqslant\Psi_{0}^{s}
for any step
s\geqslant 0
and hence, the conclusion follows.
∎

8
Super-Exponential Potential Functions

In this section, we will analyze super-exponential potential functions, i.e., exponential potentials with
\Omega(1)
smoothing parameters. In
Section

8.1
, we recall the definition of the super-exponential potentials and give an overview of the theorems we prove. In
Section

8.2
, we give a sufficient condition for a super-exponential potential to satisfy a drop inequality over one step, and in
Section

8.3
, we prove the concentration theorem for super-exponential potentials.

8.1
Outline

Recall from
Eq.

6.1
that a super-exponential potential function is defined as

\Phi^{t}:=\Phi^{t}(\phi,z):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Phi_{i}^{t}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\phi\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-z)^{+}},

for smoothing parameter
\phi>0
and integer offset
z:=z(n)>0
.

Note that if
\Phi^{t}=\mathcal{O}(\operatorname{poly}(n))
at some step
t\geqslant 0
, then

\Gap(t)=\mathcal{O}\left(z+\frac{\log n}{\phi}\right).

There are two differences in the
form
compared to the hyperbolic cosine potential
\Gamma
that we used in
Section

4
:
(i)
there is no underloaded component, as w.h.p., its contribution would be
\omega(n)
for any process making a constant number of samples in each step
4
4

4

It follows by a coupon collector’s argument that the minimum load is w.h.p.
\frac{t}{n}-\Omega(\log n)
, for sufficiently large
t
.
and
(ii)
there is this
(\ldots)^{+}
operation which is not essential, but simplifies some of the derivations.

However, the
main
difference is that there exist load vectors where the potential
could increase
in expectation, even when sufficiently large.
5
5

5

An example of such a configuration for the
Two-Choice
process without noise is one where
n-1
bins have normalized loads
z+10/\phi
and one bin is underloaded.
Here, we will prove a sufficient condition for the potential
\Phi
to drop in expectation over one step; requiring that the probability to allocate to a bin with normalized load at least
z-1
is sufficiently small. More specifically, the event that we require is the following:

\mathcal{K}^{s}:=\mathcal{K}_{\phi,z}^{s}(q^{s}):=\left\{\forall i\in[n]\colon\ y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z-1\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ q_{i}^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}\right\},

where
q^{s}
is the probability allocation vector used by the process at step
s
.

Lemma 8.1

(Restated, page
8.2
)
.

Consider any allocation process and any super-exponential potential
\Phi:=\Phi(\phi,z)
with
\phi\in[4,n]
. For any step
s\geqslant 0
where
\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds, we have that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+2.

We will try to establish that this event
\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds for a sufficiently long interval and then show that in this interval the potential
\Phi
becomes small. In the analysis of
g
-Adv-Comp
, this event will arise from the concentration of the hyperbolic cosine potential or from that of a super-exponential potential with smaller smoothing parameter. Now we are ready to state the main theorem.

Theorem
8.8

(Restated, page
8.8
)
.

Consider any allocation process for which there exist super-exponential potential functions
\Phi_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\phi_{1},z)
and
\Phi_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\phi_{2},z)
with integer offset
z:=z(n)>0
and smoothing parameters
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in(0,(\log n)/6]
with
\phi_{2}\leqslant\frac{\phi_{1}}{84}
, such that they satisfy for any step
s\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{1}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{1}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

(8.1)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

(8.2)

where
\mathcal{K}^{s}:=\mathcal{K}_{\phi_{1},z}^{s}
. Further, let
P\in[n^{-4},1]
. Then, for any steps
t\geqslant 0
and
\widetilde{t}\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
, which satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left\{\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n\right\}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\geqslant 1-P,

(8.3)

they must also satisfy

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

The statement of this theorem concerns steps in
[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
with
\widetilde{t}\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
. The interval
[t,\widetilde{t}]
is the
stabilization interval
, i.e., the interval where we want to show that
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n
for every
s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]
. The interval
[t-2n\log^{4}n,t]
is the
recovery
interval where we will show that w.h.p.
\Phi_{2}
becomes
\mathcal{O}(n)
at least
once
,
provided we start with a “weak”
\leqslant\log^{2}n
gap at step
t-2n\log^{4}n
. For both the recovery and stabilization intervals we will condition on the event
\mathcal{K}
holding at every step.

We will write
\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}
as a shorthand for the event
\big\{\!\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n\big\}
.

Dealing with negative steps.

In the analysis, we deal with negative (integer) steps, by extending the definition of the process to allocate zero weight balls in steps
t<0
, i.e., we have that
x^{t}=0
. Hence, for any
t<0
it also follows deterministically that
\Phi_{1}^{t}=\Phi_{2}^{t}=n
and so the potentials trivially satisfy the drop inequalities
Eq.

8.1
and
Eq.

8.2
at these steps.

8.2
General Drop Inequality

We now show that the drop inequality is satisfied in every step
s
where
\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds.

Lemma 8.2

(General drop inequality)
.

Consider any allocation process and any super-exponential potential
\Phi:=\Phi(\phi,z)
with
\phi\in[4,n]
. For any step
s\geqslant 0
where
\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds, we have that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+2.

Proof.

We consider the following three cases for the contribution of a bin
i\in[n]
:

Case 1 [
y_{i}^{s}<z-1
]:
The contribution of
i
will remain
\Phi_{i}^{s+1}=\Phi_{i}^{s}=1
, even if a ball is allocated to bin
i
. Hence,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{i}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]=\Phi_{i}^{s}=\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+\frac{1}{n}.

Case 2 [
y_{i}^{s}\in[z-1,z]
]:
By the condition
\mathcal{K}^{s}
, the probability of allocating a ball to bin
i
with
y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z-1
is
q_{i}^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}
. Hence, the expected contribution of this bin is at most

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{i}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\phi}\cdot q_{i}^{s}+\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot(1-q_{i}^{s})

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\phi}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}+\Phi_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}+\Phi_{i}^{s}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+\frac{2}{n},

using in the last equation that
\Phi_{i}^{s}=1
.

Case 3 [
y_{i}^{s}>z
]:
Again, by the condition
\mathcal{K}^{s}
, the probability of allocating a ball to bin
i
with
y_{i}^{s}>z
is
q_{i}^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{i}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot e^{\phi\cdot(1-1/n)}\cdot q_{i}^{s}+\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot e^{-\phi/n}\cdot(1-q_{i}^{s})

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot e^{\phi\cdot(1-1/n)}\cdot q_{i}^{s}+\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot e^{-\phi/n}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot e^{-\phi/n}\cdot\Big(1+e^{\phi}\cdot q_{i}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\phi}{2n}\right)\cdot\Big(1+e^{\phi}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\phi}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leqslant}}\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{2}{n}\right)\cdot\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right),

using in
(a)
that
y_{i}^{s}>z
implies that
y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z+\frac{1}{n}
since
z
is an integer, in
(b)
that
e^{u}\leqslant 1+\frac{1}{2}u
(for any
-1.5\leqslant u<0
) and that
\phi\leqslant n
and in
(c)
that
\phi\geqslant 4
.

Aggregating over the three cases, we get the claim:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{i}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\Phi_{i}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+\frac{2}{n}\right)=\Phi^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)+2.\qed

8.3
Concentration

8.3.1
Proof Outline of Theorem
8.8

We will now give a summary of the main technical steps in the proof of
8.8
(an illustration of the key steps is shown in
Fig.

8.1
). The proof uses an interplay between two instances
\Phi_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\phi_{1},z)
and
\Phi_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\phi_{2},z)
of the super-exponential potential function with
\phi_{2}\leqslant\phi_{1}/84
, such that in steps
s\geqslant 0
when
\Phi_{1}^{s}
is small, then the change of
\Phi_{2}^{s}
is very small.

Recovery.

By the third precondition
Eq.

8.3
of
8.8
, we start with
\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n
, which implies that
\Phi_{1}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}
(
8.3
). Using the drop inequality for the potential
\Phi_{1}
(first precondition
Eq.

8.1
), it follows that
\mathbf{E}[\,\Phi_{1}^{s}\,]\leqslant 6n
, for any step
s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
(
8.6
).
By using Markov’s inequality and a union bound, we can deduce that w.h.p.
\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{12}
for all steps
s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
. By a simple interplay between two potentials, this implies
\Phi_{2}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant n^{7/6}
(
8.5

(i)
). Now using a drop inequality for the potential
\Phi_{2}
(second precondition
Eq.

8.2
), guarantees that w.h.p.
\Phi_{2}^{r_{0}}\leqslant 6n
for
some single
step
r_{0}\in[t-n\log^{4}n,t]
(
8.7
).

Stabilization.

To obtain the stronger statement which holds
for all
steps
s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]
, we will use a concentration inequality. The key point is that for any step
r
with
\Phi_{1}^{r}\leqslant 6n^{12}
the absolute difference
|\Phi_{2}^{r+1}-\Phi_{2}^{r}|
is at most
n^{1/3}
, because
\phi_{2}\leqslant\frac{\phi_{1}}{84}
(by preconditions
Eq.

8.1
and
Eq.

8.2
). This is crucial for applying the Azuma’s inequality for super-martingales (
A.1
) to
\Phi_{2}
which yields that
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n
for all steps
s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]
using a smoothing argument (
8.4
).

t-2n\log^{4}n
t-n\log^{4}n
t
\widetilde{t}\leqslant t+n\log^{5}n
\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}
and
\mathcal{K}^{s}
for all
s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
w.h.p. (
Third Precondition
)
\Phi_{1}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}
\mathbf{E}[\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,]\leqslant 6n
for all
s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{12}
w.h.p. for all
s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]
\Phi_{2}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant n^{7/6}
\exists r_{0}\in[t-n\log^{4}n,t]:\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n
w.h.p.
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n
for all
s\in[r_{0},\widetilde{t}]
w.h.p. (
Conclusion
)
8.3
8.6
First Precondition
Markov & U.-Bound
8.5

(i)
8.7
Starting Point

Bounded Difference (
8.5

(ii)
)

Drop inequality

(
Second Precondition
)

Completion of the proof of
8.8
(
Section

8.3.4
)

Figure 8.1
:
Outline for the proof of
8.8
. Results in blue are given in
Sections

8.3.2
and
8.3.3
, while results in green are used in the completion of the proof in
Section

8.3.4
.

8.3.2
Deterministic Relations between the Potential Functions

We collect several basic facts about the super-exponential potential functions
\Phi_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\phi_{1},z)
and
\Phi_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\phi_{2},z)
satisfying the preconditions of
8.8
.

We start with a simple upper bound on
\Phi_{1}^{s}
using a weak upper bound on the gap at step
s
.

Claim 8.3
.

For any step
s\geqslant 0
where
\Gap(s)\leqslant\log^{2}n
, we have that

\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}.

Proof.

Since
\phi_{1}\leqslant(\log n)/6
, we have that

\Phi_{1}^{s}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\phi_{1}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z)^{+}}\leqslant n\cdot e^{\phi_{1}\cdot\log^{2}n}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}.

∎

The next claim is a simple “smoothness” argument showing that the potential cannot decrease quickly within
\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
steps. The derivation is elementary and relies on the fact that the average load changes by at most
2/\log^{2}n
.

Claim 8.4
.

For any step
s\geqslant 0
and any step
r\in[s,s+\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil]
, we have that
\Phi_{2}^{r}\geqslant 0.99\cdot\Phi_{2}^{s}
.

Proof.

The normalized load after
r-s
steps can decrease by at most
\frac{r-s}{n}\leqslant\frac{2}{\log^{2}n}
. Hence, for any bin
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{2,i}^{r}

\displaystyle=e^{\phi_{2}\cdot(y_{i}^{r}-z)^{+}}\geqslant e^{\phi_{2}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-\frac{r-s}{n}-z)^{+}}\geqslant e^{\phi_{2}\cdot(y_{i}^{s}-z)^{+}-\phi_{2}\cdot\frac{2}{\log^{2}n}}=\Phi_{2,i}^{s}\cdot e^{-\frac{2\phi_{2}}{\log^{2}n}}\geqslant\Phi_{2,i}^{s}\cdot e^{-o(1)}

\displaystyle\geqslant 0.99\cdot\Phi_{2,i}^{s},

for sufficiently large
n
, using that
\phi_{2}\leqslant(\log n)/6
. By aggregating over all bins, we get the claim.
∎

The next claim is crucial for applying the concentration inequality, since the second statement bounds the maximum additive change of
\Phi_{2}^{s}
in any step
s
where
\Phi_{1}^{s}
is
\operatorname{poly}(n)
:

Lemma 8.5
.

For any step
s\geqslant 0
where
\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{12}
, we have that

\displaystyle(i)

\displaystyle\quad\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant n^{7/6},

\displaystyle(ii)

\displaystyle\quad|\Phi_{2}^{s+1}-\Phi_{2}^{s}|\leqslant n^{1/3}.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary step
s\geqslant 0
with
\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{12}
. We start by upper bounding the normalized load
y_{i}^{s}
of any bin
i\in[n]
,

y_{i}^{s}\leqslant z+\frac{\log\Phi_{1,i}^{s}}{\phi_{1}}\leqslant z+\frac{\log\Phi_{1}^{s}}{\phi_{1}}\leqslant z+\frac{\log(6n^{12})}{\phi_{1}}\leqslant z+\frac{14\log n}{\phi_{1}}.

First statement
. Now, we upper bound the contribution of any bin
i\in[n]
to
\Phi_{2}^{s}
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{2,i}^{s}=\exp\bigl(\phi_{2}\cdot\bigl(y_{i}^{s}-z\bigr)^{+}\bigr)\leqslant\exp\left(\frac{14\cdot\phi_{2}}{\phi_{1}}\cdot\log n\right)\leqslant n^{1/6},

(8.4)

using that
\phi_{2}\leqslant\frac{\phi_{1}}{84}
. Hence, by aggregating over all bins,

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant n\cdot n^{1/6}=n^{7/6}.

(8.5)

Second statement
. We will derive lower and upper bounds for
\Phi_{2}^{s+1}
. For the upper bound, let
i=i^{s+1}\in[n]
be the bin where the
(s+1)
-th ball is allocated, then

\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}+\Phi_{2,i}^{s}\cdot e^{\phi_{2}}\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}+n^{1/6}\cdot n^{1/6}=\Phi_{2}^{s}+n^{1/3},

using that
\phi_{2}\leqslant(\log n)/6
and
Eq.

8.4
. For the lower bound, we pessimistically assume that all bin loads decrease by
1/n
in step
s+1
, so

\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\geqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot e^{-\phi_{2}/n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\phi_{2}}{n}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\Phi_{2}^{s}-\frac{n\cdot n^{1/6}}{n}\cdot\log n\geqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}-n^{1/3},

using in
(a)
that
e^{u}\geqslant 1+u
(for any
u
) and in
(b)
that
\phi_{2}\leqslant\log n
and
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant n\cdot n^{1/6}
by
Eq.

8.5
. Combining the two bounds we get the second statement.
∎

8.3.3
Recovery Phase

In this section, we will show for an auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
(to be defined below) that the potential function
\Phi_{2}
satisfies
\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 6n
in
at least one step

s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,t]
w.h.p.

First, we show that for the original process
\mathcal{P}
in the statement of
8.8
, the potential

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}:=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}(t):=\Phi_{1}^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\cap_{r\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,s]}\mathcal{K}^{r}\cap\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}}

(8.6)

is small in expectation for
all
steps
s\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n
. Note that there is a “recovery time” until the expectation becomes small, of at most
n\log^{4}n
steps after the “weak” bound
\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n
which follows from the third precondition
Eq.

8.3
in
8.8
.

Lemma 8.6
.

Consider the potential
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}:=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}(t)
for any step
t\geqslant 0
. Then, for any step
s\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant 6n.

Proof.

By the precondition
Eq.

8.1
of
8.8
, for any step
s\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{1}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{1}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2.

Next note that whenever
\neg\mathcal{K}^{s}
holds, it follows deterministically that
\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s+1}=0\}
, and hence

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2.

(8.7)

We will now upper bound
\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t-2n\log^{4}n},\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\,\right]
for any step
s\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n
. When
\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}
holds, by
8.3
, it also follows that
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\leqslant\Phi_{1}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}
. Hence applying
A.11

(i)
(for
a=1-\frac{1}{n}
and
b=2
) using
Eq.

8.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t-2n\log^{4}n},\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t-2n\log^{4}n},\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{s-(t-2n\log^{4}n)}+2n

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)^{n\log^{4}n}+2n

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\cdot\log^{4}n}\cdot e^{-\log^{4}n}+2n\leqslant 1+2n\leqslant 6n,

using in
(a)
that
s\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n
and in
(b)
that
e^{u}\geqslant 1+u
(for any
u
). Hence, the claim follows,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\,\,\right|\,\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\,\right]+0\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\neg\mathcal{H}^{t-2n\log^{4}n}\,\right]\leqslant 6n.\qed

The auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be the process in the statement of
8.8
, define
t_{1}:=t-n\log^{4}n
and the stopping time
\sigma:=\inf\{s\geqslant t_{1}:\{\Phi_{1}^{s}>6n^{11}\}\cup\neg\mathcal{K}^{s}\}
. Now we define the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
such that

•

in steps
s\in[0,\sigma)
it follows the allocations of
\mathcal{P}
, and

•

in steps
s\in[\sigma,\infty)
it allocates to the (currently) least loaded bin, which is a bin with normalized load
\leqslant 1\leqslant z
.

This way the process trivially satisfies
\mathcal{K}^{s}
and therefore also the drop inequalities (
Eq.

8.1
and
Eq.

8.2
) for any step
s\geqslant t_{1}
. Furthermore, starting with
\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}
, for any
s\geqslant t_{1}
it deterministically satisfies
\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{12}
, since
\Phi^{\sigma}\leqslant 6n^{11}\cdot e^{\phi_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{12}
(as
\phi_{1}\leqslant(\log n)/6
) and for
s\geqslant\sigma
the potential does not increase.

For this starting condition, we will also show that the other potential function
\Phi_{2}
w.h.p. becomes linear in
at least one
step in
[t-n\log^{4}n,t]
, by using
8.6
.

Lemma 8.7

(Recovery)
.

For any step
t\geqslant 0
and the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t-n\log^{4}n}
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{s\in[t-n\log^{4}n,t]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 6n\right\}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t-n\log^{4}n},\Phi_{1}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

Proof.

Fix any step
t\geqslant 0
and let
t_{1}:=t-n\log^{4}n
be the starting point of the analysis. Further, assume that
