\{\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}\}
holds, which also implies that the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
satisfies
\mathcal{K}^{s}
for any step
s\geqslant t_{1}
. Further, for any
s\geqslant t_{1}
, we define the potential function

\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{s}:=\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\cap_{r\in[t_{1},s]}\{\Phi_{2}^{r}>6n\}}.

We will show that
\widehat{\Phi}_{2}
drops in expectation by a multiplicative factor in every step. We start by showing for
\Phi_{2}
that when
\Phi_{2}^{s}>6n
, then it drops in expectation by a multiplicative factor. By the second precondition
Eq.

8.2
of
8.8
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11},\mathfrak{F}^{s},\Phi_{2}^{s}>6n\,\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11},\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s},\Phi_{2}^{s}>6n\,\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{2n}\Big)-6n\cdot\frac{1}{2n}+2\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{2n}\Big).

Whenever the event
\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 6n\}
holds, it follows deterministically that
\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{s}=\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{s+1}=0
. So, for the potential
\widehat{\Phi}_{2}
we obtain the drop inequality (with one fewer condition),

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11},\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{2n}\Big).

(8.8)

By inductively applying
Eq.

8.8
for
n\log^{4}n
steps starting at
t_{1}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t_{1}}\leqslant n^{7/6}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t_{1}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{2n}\Big)^{n\log^{4}n}\leqslant n^{7/6}\cdot e^{-\frac{1}{2}\log^{4}n}\leqslant n^{-6},

for sufficiently large
n
, using that
e^{u}\geqslant 1+u
(for any
u
). Hence, by Markov’s inequality,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t}\leqslant 1\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

Since it deterministically holds that
\{\Phi_{2}^{t}\geqslant n\}
for any step
t\geqslant 0
, it follows that if
\{\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t}\leqslant 1\}
holds, then also
\{\widehat{\Phi}_{2}^{t}=0\}
. So, we conclude that
\mathbf{1}_{\cap_{r\in[t_{1},t]}\{\Phi_{2}^{r}>6n\}}=0
holds, i.e.,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{r\in[t_{1},t]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n\right\}~\right|~\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.\qed

8.3.4
Completing the Proof of Theorem
8.8

For some values of
g
, the number of steps
k
in the layered induction may be
\omega(1)
and so we may need to apply
8.8
up to
\omega(1)
times, which means that we cannot afford to lose a polynomial factor in the error probability. To overcome this, we will partition the time-interval into consecutive intervals of length
\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
. Then, we will prove that at the end of each such interval the potential is small w.h.p., and finally use a simple smoothness argument (
8.4
) to show that the potential is small w.h.p. in
all
steps.

Theorem 8.8
.

Consider any allocation process for which there exist super-exponential potential functions
\Phi_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\phi_{1},z)
and
\Phi_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\phi_{2},z)
with integer offset
z:=z(n)>0
and smoothing parameters
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in(0,(\log n)/6]
with
\phi_{2}\leqslant\frac{\phi_{1}}{84}
, such that they satisfy for any step
s\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{1}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{1}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

where
\mathcal{K}^{s}:=\mathcal{K}_{\phi_{1},z}^{s}
. Further, let
P\in[n^{-4},1]
. Then, for any steps
t\geqslant 0
and
\widetilde{t}\in[t,t+n\log^{5}n]
, which satisfy

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left\{\Gap(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant\log^{2}n\right\}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]\geqslant 1-P,

they must also satisfy

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

We will start by proving the following lemma for the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t-n\log^{4}n}
.

Lemma 8.9
.

For any step
t\geqslant 0
and the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t-n\log^{4}n}
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\Phi_{1}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{4}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

Proof.

Our goal is to apply Azuma’s inequality to
\Phi_{2}
. However, there are two challenges:
(i)
we cannot afford to take the union bound over all
\operatorname{poly}(n)
steps and
(ii)

\Phi_{2}
is a super-martingale only when it is sufficiently large. To deal with
(i)
we will apply Azuma’s inequality to sub-intervals of length at most
\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
and then use a smoothness argument (
8.4
) to deduce that
\Phi_{2}
is small in the steps in between. For
(ii)
we will define
X^{s}:=X^{s}(\Phi_{2}^{s})
in a way to ensure that it is super-martingale at every step and also satisfies the bounded difference inequality.

More specifically, consider an arbitrary step
r\in[t-n\log^{4}n,t]
and partition the interval
(r,\widetilde{t}]
into

\mathcal{I}_{1}:=(r,r+\Delta],~\mathcal{I}_{2}=(r+\Delta,r+2\Delta],~\ldots~,~\mathcal{I}_{q}:=(r+(q-1)\Delta,\widetilde{t}],

where
\Delta:=\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
and
q:=\big\lceil\frac{\widetilde{t}-r}{\Delta}\big\rceil\leqslant\big\lceil\frac{t+n\log^{5}n-r}{\Delta}\big\rceil\leqslant 2\log^{7}n
. In order to prove that
\Phi_{2}
is at most
8n
in every step in
(r,\widetilde{t}]
, we will use our auxiliary lemmas (
Sections

8.3.2
and
8.3.3
) and Azuma’s super-martingale concentration inequality (
A.1
)
to establish that
\Phi_{2}
is at most
7n
at each of the steps
r+\Delta,r+2\Delta,\ldots,r+(q-1)\Delta,\widetilde{t}
. Finally, by using a smoothness argument (
8.4
), we will establish that
\Phi_{2}
is at most
8n
at all steps in
(r,\widetilde{t}]
, which is the conclusion of the theorem.

For each interval
i\in[q]
, we define
X_{i}^{r+(i-1)\Delta}:=\max\{\Phi_{2}^{r+(i-1)\Delta},5n+n^{1/3}\}
and for any
s\in(r+(i-1)\Delta,r+i\Delta]
,

X_{i}^{s}:=\begin{cases}\Phi_{2}^{s}&\text{if there exists }u\in[r+(i-1)\Delta,s)\text{ such that }\Phi_{2}^{u}\geqslant 5n,\\
5n+n^{1/3}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Note that whenever the first condition in the definition of
X_{i}^{s}
is satisfied, it remains satisfied until the end of the interval, i.e., until step
r+i\cdot\Delta
. Our next aim is to establish the preconditions of Azuma’s inequality (
A.1
) for
X_{i}^{s}
.

For convenience, we define
t_{1}:=t-n\log^{4}n
and the event

\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}:=\{\Phi_{1}^{t_{1}}\leqslant 6n^{11}\}.

Lemma 8.10
.

Fix any interval
\mathcal{I}_{i}
for
i\in[q]
. Then for the random variables
X_{i}^{s}
, for any step
s\in(r+(i-1)\Delta,r+i\Delta]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,X_{i}^{s}\,\left|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\right.\,\right]\leqslant X_{i}^{s-1},

and

\left(\left.\left|X_{i}^{s}-X_{i}^{s-1}\,\right|\,~\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\right)\leqslant 2n^{1/3}.

Case 2(a)
Case 2(c)
Case 2(b)
5n
5n+n^{1/3}
s
t_{0}+(i-1)\cdot\Delta
\phantom{1}\sigma\phantom{1}
\sigma+1
t_{0}+i\cdot\Delta
X_{i}^{s}
\Phi_{2}^{s}

Figure 8.2
:
Visualization of three cases in the proof of
8.10
for interval
i\in[q]
.

Proof of
8.10
.

Recall that by conditioning on
\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}
, the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
satisfies
\{\Phi_{1}^{s-1}\leqslant 6n^{12}\}
and
\mathcal{K}^{s-1}
, for any
s\in(r+(i-1)\Delta,r+i\Delta]
, since
r+(i-1)\Delta\geqslant t_{1}
.

By precondition
Eq.

8.2
, when
\{\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 4n\}
also holds, we have that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Phi_{2}^{s}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1},\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 4n\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Phi_{2}^{s}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1},\mathcal{K}^{s-1},\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 4n\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-1}-4n\cdot\frac{1}{n}+2\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-1}.

(8.9)

Further, by
8.5

(ii)
, we also have that

\displaystyle\left(\left.\left|\Phi_{2}^{s}-\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\right|~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\right)\leqslant n^{1/3}.

(8.10)

Case 1 [
\Phi_{2}^{r+(i-1)\Delta}\geqslant 5n+n^{1/3}
]:
In this case
X_{i}^{s-1}=\Phi_{2}^{s-1}
for all
s\in(r+(i-1)\Delta,r+i\Delta]
. By
8.4
, we also have
\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 0.99\cdot(5n+n^{1/3})\geqslant 4n
(as
\Delta\leqslant\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
) and the two statements follow by
Eq.

8.9
and
Eq.

8.10
.

Case 2 [
\Phi_{2}^{r+(i-1)\Delta}<5n+n^{1/3}
]:
Let
\sigma:=\inf\{u\geqslant r+(i-1)\Delta\colon\Phi_{2}^{u}\geqslant 5n\}
. We consider the following three cases (see
Fig.

8.2
):

•

Case 2(a) [
s-1<\sigma
]:
Here
X_{i}^{s}=X_{i}^{s-1}=5n+n^{1/3}
, so the two statements hold trivially.

•

Case 2(b) [
s-1=\sigma
]:

We will first establish that

\displaystyle 5n\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\leqslant 5n+n^{1/3}.

(8.11)

The lower bound
\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 5n
follows by definition of
\sigma
. For the upper bound, we consider the following two cases. If
s-1=r+(i-1)\Delta
, then this follows by the assumption for Case 2. Otherwise, we have that
\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 5n
and
\Phi_{2}^{s-2}<5n
. By
Eq.

8.10
, we obtain that
\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-2}+n^{1/3}<5n+n^{1/3}
.

Next, by definition,
X_{i}^{s-1}=5n+n^{1/3}
and
X_{i}^{s}=\Phi_{2}^{s}
, so by
Eq.

8.9
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.X_{i}^{s}\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\,\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{2}^{s}\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{2}^{s-1}<X_{i}^{s-1},

which establishes the first statement. For the second statement, by
Eq.

8.10
and
Eq.

8.11
we have

\left|X_{i}^{s}-X_{i}^{s-1}\right|=\left|\Phi_{2}^{s}-5n-n^{1/3}\right|\leqslant\left|\Phi_{2}^{s-1}-5n-n^{1/3}\right|+\left|\Phi_{2}^{s}-\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\right|\leqslant 2n^{1/3}.

using in the last inequality that
Eq.

8.10
and
Eq.

8.11
.

•

Case 2(c) [
s-1>\sigma
]:
Here,
X_{i}^{s-1}=\Phi_{2}^{s-1}
and
X_{i}^{s}=\Phi_{2}^{s}
. Since
\Phi_{2}^{\sigma}\geqslant 5n
, by
8.4
(as
s-\sigma\leqslant\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
), we also have that

\Phi_{2}^{s-1}\geqslant 0.99\cdot\Phi_{2}^{\sigma}\geqslant 0.99\cdot 5n\geqslant 4n,

and thus by
Eq.

8.9
, the first statement follows. The second statement follows by
Eq.

8.10
.∎

Now we return to the proof of
8.9
. By
8.10
, we have verified that
X_{i}^{s}
satisfies the preconditions of Azuma’s inequality for any filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{s-1}
where
\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}
holds. So, applying
A.1
for
\lambda=\frac{n}{2\log^{7}n}
,
N\leqslant\Delta
and
D=2n^{1/3}
, we get for any
i\in[q]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.X_{i}^{r+i\Delta}\geqslant X_{i}^{r+(i-1)\Delta}+\lambda\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{r}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{n^{2}/(4\log^{14}n)}{2\cdot\Delta\cdot(4n^{2/3})}\right)+2P\leqslant 3P,

since
\Delta\leqslant\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
and
P\geqslant n^{-4}
. Taking the union bound over the at most
2\log^{7}n
intervals
i\in[q]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{i\in[q]}\left\{X_{i}^{r+i\Delta}\geqslant X_{1}^{r}+i\cdot\frac{n}{2\log^{7}n}\right\}\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{r}\,\right]\leqslant(2\log^{7}n)\cdot 3P\leqslant\frac{1}{8}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

Next, conditional on
(\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{r},\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n)
, we have the following chain of inclusions:

\displaystyle\bigcap_{i\in[q]}\left\{X_{i}^{r+i\Delta}\leqslant X_{1}^{r}+i\cdot\frac{n}{2\log^{7}n}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\bigcap_{i\in[q]}\left\{X_{i}^{r+i\Delta}\leqslant 6n+n\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\bigcap_{i\in[q]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{r+i\Delta}\leqslant 7n\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\subseteq}}\bigcap_{s\in[r,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant\frac{7}{0.99}\cdot n\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\subseteq}}\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\},

where
(a)
holds since
i\leqslant q\leqslant 2\log^{7}n
and
X_{1}^{r}\leqslant\max\{\Phi_{2}^{r},5n+n^{1/3}\}
,
(b)
holds since
\Phi_{2}^{r+i\Delta}\leqslant X_{i}^{r+i\Delta}
,
(c)
holds by applying the smoothness argument of
8.4
to each interval
i\in[q]
, as the length of each interval is at most
\lceil n/\log^{2}n\rceil
and
(d)
holds since
8\geqslant\frac{7}{0.99}
and
r\leqslant t
. This implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{r},\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{8}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

(8.12)

Next define
\tau:=\inf\{r\geqslant t-n\log^{4}n\colon\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n\}
. By
8.7
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant t\,\left|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}\right.\,\right]\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant t\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

(8.13)

We get the conclusion for the
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
process, by combining this with
Eq.

8.12
and
Eq.

8.13
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{r=t-n\log^{4}n}^{t}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}},\mathfrak{F}^{r},\Phi_{2}^{r}\leqslant 6n\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\tau=r\,\right|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:phi_t0_good_whp_conclusion}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-\frac{1}{8}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P\right)\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\tau\leqslant t\,\left|\,\mathcal{Z}^{t_{1}}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:concentration_stopping_time_recovery}})}}{{\geqslant}}\left(1-\frac{1}{8}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P\right)\cdot\left(1-n^{-6}\right)\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{4}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.\qed

We now return to the proof of
8.8
for the original process
\mathcal{P}
.

Proof of
8.8
.

Let
t_{1}:=t-n\log^{4}n
.
We start by showing that the processes
\mathcal{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
agree with high probability in the interval
[t_{1},\widetilde{t}]
(and so at every step
s\in[t_{1},\widetilde{t}]
we have
\Phi_{2}^{s}=\Phi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}},2}^{s}
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t_{1},\widetilde{t}]}\left\{y^{s}=y_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}}^{s}\right\}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{11}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}^{s}\leqslant 6n^{11}\right\}\cap\mathcal{H}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\cap\bigcap_{s\in[t-2n\log^{4}n,\widetilde{t}]}\mathcal{K}^{s}\,\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}1-n^{2}\cdot n^{-11}-P\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geqslant}}1-2P,

(8.14)

using in
(a)
the definition of
\widetilde{\Phi}_{1}
in
Eq.

8.6
, in
(b)

8.6
, Markov’s inequality and union bound over
\widetilde{t}-(t-2n\log^{4}n)\leqslant n^{2}
steps and precondition
Eq.

8.3
and in
(c)
that
P\geqslant n^{-4}
. Note that this also implies that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Phi_{1}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-2P.

(8.15)

By
8.9
, for the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}},2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\,\right|\,\,\Phi_{1}^{t-n\log^{4}n}\leqslant 6n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{4}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

By combining with
Eq.

8.15
(since by definition
\mathcal{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
agree in steps
s\leqslant t_{1}
) we get,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}},2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\right]\geqslant\left(1-\frac{1}{4}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P\right)\cdot\left(1-2P\right)\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.

Finally, using
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,A\cap B\,\right]\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,A\,\right]-\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\neg B\,\right]
with
Eq.

8.14
, we conclude that
\mathcal{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{1}}
agree in all steps of the interval
[t_{1},\widetilde{t}]
and so

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t,\widetilde{t}]}\left\{\Phi_{2}^{s}\leqslant 8n\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{2}\cdot(\log^{8}n)\cdot P-2P\geqslant 1-(\log^{8}n)\cdot P.\qed

9
Upper Bound of
\mathcal{O}(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
for
g
-
Adv-Comp
with
g\leqslant\log n
: Layered Induction

In this section, we will complete the proof of the
\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\big)
upper bound for any
g\leqslant\log n
. We do this by first proving the key lemma for the drop of the super-exponential potentials (
9.1
) and then complete the layered induction in
9.2
.

We begin by recalling from
Section

6.1
the definition for the number of steps in the layered induction
k
. Recall that
\alpha_{1}:=\frac{1}{6\kappa}
, for
\kappa>0
the constant in
5.13
and
k:=k(g)\geqslant 2
is the unique integer satisfying,

(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}.

Note that the integer
k
always satisfies
k=\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
, since
g>1
.

Further, recall that for any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
we are using the super-exponential potentials
\Phi_{j}:=\Phi_{j}(\phi_{j},z_{j})
with smoothing parameters
\phi_{j}:=\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}
with
\alpha_{2}:=\frac{1}{84}\cdot\alpha_{1}
and offsets
z_{j}:=c_{5}\cdot g+\big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\big\rceil\cdot j\cdot g
(for the constant
c_{5}>0
defined in
Eq.

7.14
), as well as the potentials
\Psi_{j}:=\Psi_{j}(\psi_{j},z_{j})
with smoothing parameters
\psi_{j}:=\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}
.

We are now ready to prove the key lemma for the layered induction.

Lemma 9.1
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\in[\log(2C),\alpha_{1}\log n]
, for the constant
C>0
defined in
7.8
and
\alpha_{1}>0
defined in
Eq.

6.2
. Further, let
k:=k(g)\geqslant 2
be the unique integer such that
(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
. Then, for any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and any step
s\geqslant 0
,
\Phi_{j-1}^{s}\leqslant Cn
implies
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{s}
.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary step
s
with
\Phi_{j-1}^{s}\leqslant Cn
. Recall the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{s}
,

\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{s}(q^{s}):=\left\{\forall i\in[n]\colon\ y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1\ \ \Rightarrow\ \ q_{i}^{s}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\psi_{j}}\right\}.

Thus, we want to bound the probability to allocate to a bin
i\in[n]
with load
y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1
. In order to do this, we will bound the number of bins
\ell\in[n]
for which the adversary can reverse the comparison of
i
and
\ell
, by bounding the ones with load
y_{\ell}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1-g
. Recall that
z_{j}:=c_{5}\cdot g+\big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\big\rceil\cdot j\cdot g
. In the analysis below we make use of the following simple bound,

\displaystyle z_{j}-1-g-z_{j-1}=\left\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\right\rceil\cdot g-1-g\geqslant\frac{3}{\alpha_{2}}\cdot g,

(9.1)

using that
\alpha_{2}\leqslant 1/2
. We consider the cases
j=1
and
j>1
separately as
\Phi_{0}
has a slightly different form than
\Phi_{j-1}
for
j>1
.

Case 1 [
j=1
]:
The contribution of any bin
\ell\in[n]
with load
y_{\ell}^{s}\geqslant z_{1}-1-g
to
\Phi_{0}^{s}
is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{0,\ell}^{s}=e^{\alpha_{2}\cdot(y_{\ell}^{s}-z_{0})^{+}}\geqslant e^{\alpha_{2}\cdot(z_{1}-1-g-z_{0})^{+}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:z_j_diff_bound}})}}{{\geqslant}}e^{3g}.

Hence, when
\{\Phi_{0}^{s}\leqslant Cn\}
holds, the number of such bins is at most

Cn\cdot e^{-3g}=Cn\cdot e^{-g}\cdot e^{-2g}\leqslant\frac{n}{2}\cdot e^{-2g},

using that
g\geqslant\log(2C)
. Hence, the probability of allocating a ball to a bin
i\in[n]
with
y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z_{1}-1
is at most that of sampling
i
and a bin
\ell\in[n]
with
y_{\ell}^{s}\geqslant z_{1}-1-g
, i.e., at most

q_{i}^{s}\leqslant 2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot e^{-2g}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\psi_{1}},

using that
\psi_{1}:=\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{1-k}\leqslant g
, as
g\geqslant(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}
.

Case 2 [
j>1
]:
The contribution of any bin
\ell\in[n]
with load
y_{\ell}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1-g
to
\Phi_{j-1}^{s}
is,

\displaystyle\Phi_{j-1,\ell}^{s}=e^{\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-1-k}\cdot(y_{\ell}^{s}-z_{j-1})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:z_j_diff_bound}})}}{{\geqslant}}e^{\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-1-k}\cdot(\frac{3}{\alpha_{2}}\cdot g)}=e^{3\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}}.

Hence, when
\{\Phi_{j-1}^{s}\leqslant Cn\}
holds, the number of such bins is at most

Cn\cdot e^{-3\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}}\leqslant\frac{n}{2}\cdot e^{-2\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}},

using that
e^{-(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}}\leqslant e^{-(\log n)\cdot g^{2-k}}\leqslant e^{-(\log n)\cdot g\cdot\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}\log n}}\leqslant e^{-\log(2C)}=\frac{1}{2C}
, since
j>1
,
g\leqslant(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
,
g\geqslant\log(2C)
and
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1
.

Hence, the probability of allocating a ball to a bin
i\in[n]
with
y_{i}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1
is at most that of sampling
i
and a bin
\ell\in[n]
with
y_{\ell}^{s}\geqslant z_{j}-1-g
, i.e., at most

q_{i}^{s}\leqslant 2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot e^{-2\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}}=\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-2\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}}\leqslant\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{-\psi_{j}},

recalling that
\psi_{j}:=\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}
for
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1
.

Combining the two cases, we conclude that the event
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{s}
holds at step
s
.
∎

Theorem 9.2
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\in(1,\log n]
. Then, there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

Proof.

Let
g_{\min}:=\max\big\{\log(2C),\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}\big\}
. We consider three cases depending on the value of
1\leqslant g\leqslant\log n
:

Case 1 [
\min\big\{\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4},c_{6}\big\}\cdot\log n\leqslant g\leqslant\log n
]:
(for
c_{6}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.1
) In this case, the
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
upper bound follows by the
\mathcal{O}(g+\log n)
upper bound of
5.14
.

Case 2 [
g<g_{\min}
]:
For
1\leqslant g\leqslant g_{\min}
,
g
is constant and the
\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
upper bound on the gap will follow by considering the
\widetilde{g}
-
Adv-Comp
setting with
\widetilde{g}=\lceil g_{\min}\rceil
. This setting encompasses
g
-Adv-Comp
as
\widetilde{g}\geqslant g
and is analyzed in Case 3.

Case 3 [
g_{\min}\leqslant g<\min\big\{\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4},c_{6}\big\}\cdot\log n
]:
Recall that for any
g<\alpha_{1}\log n
, we defined the unique integer
k:=k(g)\geqslant 2
satisfying,

\displaystyle(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)},

and as explained in
Eq.

6.4
, since
g<\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n
, we may assume that the following stronger condition holds

(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n\right)^{1/(k-1)},

where the inequalities are valid using
B.6
and that
g\geqslant\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}
.

Let
t_{j}:=m-2n(k-j)\log^{4}n
for any integer
0\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
. We will proceed by induction on the potential functions
\Phi_{j}
. The base case follows by applying
7.8
(using
g\leqslant c_{6}\log n
and
t_{0}\geqslant m-n\log^{5}n
),

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},m]}\left\{\Phi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-4}.

(9.2)

We will now prove the induction step.

Lemma 9.3

(
Induction step
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant\max\big\{\log(2C),\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}\big\}
satisfying
(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}\leqslant g<(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
for some integer
k\geqslant 2
, where
C>0
is the constant defined in
7.8
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
are defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
Eq.

6.3
. Then, for any integer
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and any step
m\geqslant 0
, if it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{j-1},m]}\{\Phi_{j-1}^{t}\leqslant Cn\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8(j-1)}}{n^{4}},

then it also follows that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{j},m]}\{\Phi_{j}^{t}\leqslant Cn\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8j}}{n^{4}}.

Proof of
9.3
.

Consider an arbitrary integer
j
with
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
and assume that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{j-1},m]}\{\Phi_{j-1}^{t}\leqslant Cn\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8(j-1)}}{n^{4}}.

By
9.1
, we have that
\{\Phi_{j-1}^{t}\leqslant Cn\}
implies
\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{t}
. Furthermore,
\{\Phi_{j-1}^{t_{j-1}}\leqslant Cn\}
also implies
\{\Gap(t_{j-1})\leqslant\log^{2}n\}
. Hence, it also holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left\{\Gap(t_{j-1})\leqslant\log^{2}n\right\}\cap\bigcap_{t\in[t_{j-1},m]}\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{t}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8(j-1)}}{n^{4}}.

(9.3)

Applying
8.2
for the potentials
\Phi_{j}
and
\Psi_{j}
, since
\psi_{j}\geqslant\phi_{j}=\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{j-k}\geqslant\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{1-k}\geqslant 4
(as
g<(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
), for any step
t\geqslant 0
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Phi_{j}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Phi_{j}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2,

(9.4)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Psi_{j}^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\mathcal{K}_{\psi_{j},z_{j}}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Psi_{j}^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)+2.

(9.5)

Hence, by
Eq.

9.4
,
Eq.

9.5
and
Eq.

9.3
, the preconditions of
8.8
are satisfied for starting step
t_{j}:=m-2n(k-j)\cdot\log^{4}n
,
P:=(\log n)^{8(j-1)}/n^{4}
and terminating step at
\widetilde{t}:=m
, and so we conclude (since
C\geqslant 8
) that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{j},m]}\left\{\Phi_{j}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8j}}{n^{4}}.\qed

Returning to the proof of
9.2
, inductively applying
9.3
for
k-1
times and using
Eq.

9.2
as a base case, we get that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{t\in[t_{k-1},m]}\left\{\Phi_{k-1}^{t}\leqslant Cn\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\frac{(\log n)^{8(k-1)}}{n^{4}}\geqslant 1-n^{-3},

using in the last step that
k=\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
. When
\{\Phi_{k-1}^{m}\leqslant Cn\}
occurs, the gap at step
m
cannot be more than
z_{k}:=c_{5}g+\big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\big\rceil\cdot k\cdot g
, since otherwise we would get a contradiction

\displaystyle Cn\geqslant\Phi_{k-1}^{m}

\displaystyle\geqslant\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{(k-1)-k}\cdot(z_{k}-z_{k-1})\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\alpha_{2}\cdot(\log n)\cdot g^{-1}\cdot\Big(\Big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\Big\rceil\cdot g\Big)\Big)\geqslant\exp(4\cdot\log n)=n^{4}.

Hence,
\Gap(m)\leqslant z_{k}=c_{5}g+\big\lceil\frac{4}{\alpha_{2}}\big\rceil\cdot k\cdot g
.

By the assumption on
g
, we have

g<\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n\right)^{1/(k-1)}~\Rightarrow~\log g<\frac{1}{k-1}\cdot\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n\right)~\Rightarrow~k<1+\frac{\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n\right)}{\log g},

using that
g>1
. Since
\alpha_{2}>0
and
c_{5}>0
are constants, we conclude that there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.\qed

In the above, we actually proved the following slightly stronger corollary, which we will use in
Section

10.2
.
This is based on the insight that, in order to prove the above gap bound at step
m
, the only assumption on the steps
[0,t_{0})
, with
t_{0}:=m-n\log^{5}n-\Delta_{r}
, is that the coarse gap bound of
\mathcal{O}(g\log(ng))
must hold at step
t_{0}
(see, e.g.,
5.11
and
7.8
). While during the interval
[t_{0},m]
the process is required to be an instance of
g
-Adv-Comp
, in the interval
[0,t_{0})
the process can be arbitrary as long as the coarse gap bound holds at step
t_{0}
.

Corollary 9.4
.

Consider any
g\in(1,\log n]
,
m\geqslant 0
,
t_{0}:=m-n\log^{5}n-\Delta_{r}
for
\Delta_{r}:=\Delta_{r}(g)>0
as defined in
5.11
and
c_{3}>0
the constant in
4.3
. Further, consider a process which, in steps
[t_{0},m]
, is an instance of
g
-Adv-Comp
setting. Then, there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
, such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\Gap(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g\log(ng)\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

10
Upper Bounds for Probabilistic Noise and Delay Settings

In this section, we present upper bounds for the probabilistic noise settings and the delay settings. These upper bounds follow from the results (and analysis) for
g
-Adv-Comp
in Sections
4
-
9
.

10.1
An Upper Bound for the Probabilistic Noise Setting

Proposition 10.1
.

Consider the
\rho
-Noisy-Comp
setting with
\rho(\delta)
being any non-decreasing function in
\delta
with
\lim_{\delta\rightarrow\infty}\rho(\delta)=1
. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define
\delta^{*}:=\delta^{*}(n)=\min\{\delta\geqslant 1\colon\rho(\delta)\geqslant 1-n^{-4}\}
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{m}\right|\leqslant\kappa\cdot\delta^{*}\log(n\delta^{*})\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

Note that for the
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
process where
\rho(\delta)
has
Gaussian tails (see
Eq.

2.1
), we have
\delta^{*}=\mathcal{O}(\sigma\cdot\sqrt{\log n})
.
The choice of
\delta^{*}
in
10.1
ensures that in most steps, all possible comparisons among bins with load difference greater than
\delta^{*}
will be correct, implying that the process satisfies the condition of
g
-Adv-Comp
with
g=\delta^{*}
.

Proof.

We will analyze the hyperbolic cosine potential
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
as defined in
Eq.

4.1
, with
\gamma:=-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})/\delta^{*}
.
We first state a trivial upper bound on

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

in terms of
\Gamma^{t}
, which holds deterministically for all steps
t\geqslant 0
(cf.
7.2

(i)
),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Gamma_{i}^{t+1}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\gamma}\cdot\Gamma_{i}^{t}=e^{\gamma}\cdot\Gamma^{t}\leqslant(1+2\gamma)\cdot\Gamma^{t},

using that
e^{\gamma}\leqslant 1+2\gamma
for
0<\gamma\leqslant 1
.

We will now provide a better upper bound, exploiting that with high probability all possible comparisons between bins that differ by at least
\delta^{*}
will be correct. Again, consider any step
t\geqslant 0
. Let us assume that in step
t
, we first determine the outcome of the load comparisons among all
n^{2}
possible bin pairs. Only then we sample two bins, and allocate the ball following the pre-determined outcome of the load comparison. For any two bins
i_{1},i_{2}\in[n]
with
|x_{i_{1}}^{t}-x_{i_{2}}^{t}|\geqslant\delta^{*}
, we have

\rho(|x_{i_{1}}^{t}-x_{i_{2}}^{t}|)\geqslant\rho(\delta^{*})\geqslant 1-n^{-4}.

Hence by the union bound over all
n^{2}
pairs, we can conclude that with probability at least
1-n^{-2}
, all comparisons among bin pairs with load difference at least
\delta^{*}
are correct. Let us denote this event by
\mathcal{G}^{t}
, so
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}
for every step
t\geqslant 0
. Conditional on
\mathcal{G}^{t}
, the process in step
t
is an instance of
g
-Adv-Comp
with
g=\delta^{*}
. Therefore, by
4.3

(i)
, there exists a constant
c_{1}\geqslant 1
, so that the hyperbolic cosine potential satisfies

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Gamma^{t}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\right)+c_{1}.

Now combining our two upper bounds on
\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]
, we conclude

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]+\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\cdot(1-\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right])

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t},\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]\cdot(1-n^{-2})+\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\cdot n^{-2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\Gamma^{t}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\right)+c_{1}\right)\cdot(1-n^{-2})+(1+2\gamma)\cdot\Gamma^{t}\cdot n^{-2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Gamma^{t}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\right)+c_{1}-\Gamma^{t}\cdot n^{-2}+\Gamma^{t}\cdot\frac{\gamma}{96n}\cdot n^{-2}+\Gamma^{t}\cdot n^{-2}+\Gamma^{t}\cdot 2\gamma\cdot n^{-2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Gamma^{t}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{100n}\right)+c_{1},

using in
(a)
that
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{G}^{t}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}
. So, using
A.11

(ii)
(with
a=1-\frac{\gamma}{100n}
and
b=c_{1}
) since
\Gamma^{0}=2n\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n
, we get for any
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n.

Using Markov’s inequality yields,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gamma^{t}>\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n^{4}\,\right]\leqslant n^{-3}.

Now the claim follows, since the event
\left\{\Gamma^{t}\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n^{4}\right\}
for
\gamma=\Theta\big(\frac{1}{\delta^{*}}\big)
, implies that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma}\log\left(\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n^{4}\right)=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{*}\cdot\log(n\delta^{*})).\qed

10.2
Upper Bounds for Delay Settings

In this section, we will prove tight upper bounds for the
Two-Choice
process in the
\tau
-Delay
setting and the
b
-Batch
process for a range of values for the delay parameter
\tau
and batch size
b
. In particular for
\tau=n
, we show:

Theorem 10.2
.

Consider the
\tau
-Delay
setting with
\tau=n
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa>0
such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

This implies the same upper bound for the
b
-Batch
process for
b=n
, since it is an instance of the
\tau
-Delay
setting with
\tau=n
. This bound improves the
\mathcal{O}(\log n)
bound in
[
15
, Theorem 1]
and can be easily seen to be asymptotically tight due to the
\Omega\big({\scriptstyle\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}}\big)
lower bound for
One-Choice
for with
n
balls (
11.6
).

We will analyze
\tau
-Delay
using a more general approach which also works for other choices of the delay parameter
\tau\leqslant n\log n
. First note that
\tau
-Delay
is an instance of
g_{1}
-
Adv-Comp
with
g_{1}:=\tau-1
. This follows since for all steps, a bin could sampled (and be allocated to) at most
\tau-1
times during the last
\tau-1
steps. However, for a typical step we expect each bin to be incremented much less frequently during the last
\tau-1
steps, and thus the process is with high probability an instance of
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
for some
g_{2}\ll g_{1}
.

To make this more specific, consider an arbitrary instance of
g_{1}
-
Adv-Comp
and define
D^{t}
as the set of pairs of bins (of unequal load) whose comparison is reversed with non-zero probability by the adversary in step
t+1
,

D^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t}):=\left\{(i,j)\in[n]\times[n]:y_{i}^{t}>y_{j}^{t}\wedge\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,A^{t+1}(\mathfrak{F}^{t},i,j)=i\,\right]>0\right\},

and then define the largest load difference that could be reversed by the process at step
t+1
,

g^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t}):=\max_{(i,j)\in D^{t}(\mathfrak{F}^{t})}\left|y_{i}^{t}-y_{j}^{t}\right|.

This can be seen as the “effective
g
-bound” of the process in step
t+1
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Consider the
g_{1}
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g_{1}\in[1,n\log n]
, and consider any
g_{2}\in[1,\log^{2}n]
. If for every step
t\geqslant 0
, we have

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,g^{t}\leqslant g_{2}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6},

then there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
such that for any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

We will now use
10.3
to prove
10.2
.

Proof of
10.2
.

Any bin can be allocated at most
n-1
times during an interval of
\tau-1=n-1
steps, so an adversary with
g_{1}=n-1
who also remembers the entire history of the process, can simulate
\tau
-Delay
at any step
t
, by using the allocation information in steps
[t-n,t]
.

To obtain the bound for
g_{2}
, note that in
Two-Choice
, at each step two bins are sampled for each ball. So in
n-1
steps of
Two-Choice
, there are
2(n-1)
bins sampled using
One-Choice
. By the properties of
One-Choice
(
A.6
), we have that for any consecutive
n-1
allocations, with probability at least
1-n^{-6}
we sample (and allocate to) no bin more than
11\log n/\log\log n
times. In such a sequence of bin samples, we can simulate
\tau
-Delay
using
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
with
g_{2}=11\log n/\log\log n
. Hence, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,g^{t}\leqslant g_{2}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

Since the precondition of
10.3
holds for
g_{1}=n-1
and
g_{2}=11\log n/\log\log n
, we get that there exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.\qed

The same argument also applies for any
\tau\in[n\cdot e^{-\log^{c}n},n\log n]
. For
One-Choice
with
2\tau
balls the gap is w.h.p.
\operatorname{polylog}(n)
(e.g., see
A.5
) and so we can apply
10.3
with
g_{2}=\operatorname{polylog}(n)
, to obtain the gap bound of

\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n=\Theta(g_{2}).

Corollary 10.4
.

There exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that the
\tau
-Delay
setting with any
\tau\in[n\cdot e^{-\log^{c}n},n\log n]
, where
c>0
is any constant, for any step
m\geqslant 0
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\leqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\left(\frac{4n}{\tau}\cdot\log n\right)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

Remark 10.5
.

A matching lower bound holds for the
b
-Batch
process for any batch size
b\in[n\cdot e^{-\log^{c}n},n\log n]
. This follows by the lower bound for
One-Choice
with
b
balls (e.g., see
A.10
) which matches the gap of
b
-Batch
in the first batch (
11.6
).

Therefore,
10.4
and
10.5
establish that w.h.p.
\Gap(m)=\Theta\big({\scriptstyle\frac{\log n}{\log((4n/b)\cdot\log n)}}\big)
for the
b
-Batch
process for any
b\in[n\cdot e^{-\log^{c}n},n\log n]
. However, the following remark (which also applies to the
\tau
-Delay
setting), establishes that there are regions where the
b
-Batch
process has an asymptotically worse gap than
One-Choice
with
b
balls (see also some empirical results in
Fig.

12.2
in
Section

12
):

Remark 10.6
.

For any
\tau
(or
b
) being
n^{1-\varepsilon}
for any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, the
One-Choice
process has
\Gap(b)=\mathcal{O}(1)
w.h.p. (see
A.7
). Hence, by
10.3
with
g_{2}=\mathcal{O}(1)
,
\tau
-Delay
has for any step
m\geqslant 0
,
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(\log\log n)
w.h.p., which is asymptotically tight by
11.1
for
m=n
.

We now return to proving
10.3
.

Proof of
10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a process satisfying the preconditions in the statement and we will define the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
for some step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
(to be specified below). Consider the stopping time
\sigma:=\inf\{s\geqslant t_{0}:g^{s}>g_{2}\}
. Then, the auxiliary process
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
is defined so that

•

in steps
s\in[0,\sigma)
, it makes the same allocations as
\mathcal{P}
, and

•

in steps
s\in[\sigma,\infty)
, it makes the same allocations as the
g_{2}
-
Bounded
process.

This way
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
is a
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
process for all steps
s\geqslant t_{0}
. Let
\widetilde{y}
be the normalized load vector for
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
, then it follows by the precondition that w.h.p. the two processes agree for any interval
[t_{0},m]
with
m-t_{0}\leqslant n^{3}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t_{0},m]}\left\{y^{s}=\widetilde{y}^{s}\right\}\,\right]\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcap_{s\in[t_{0},m]}\left\{g^{s}\leqslant g_{2}\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}\cdot n^{3}=1-n^{-3}.

(10.1)

For
m\leqslant n^{3}
, the upper bound follows directly by
9.2
for
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
and taking the union bound with
Eq.

10.1
, i.e., that
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{0}
agrees with
\mathcal{P}
.

For
m>n^{3}
, the analysis is slightly more challenging. We need to show that the process recovers from the weak upper bound obtained from the
g_{1}
-
Adv-Comp
setting. Let
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
be as defined in
Eq.

4.1
with
\gamma:=-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})/g_{2}
for the
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
process (i.e., the
\widetilde{y}
load vector). Also, let
t_{0}:=m-n^{3}
and
t_{1}:=m-n\log^{5}n-\Delta_{r}
where
\Delta_{r}:=\Delta_{r}(g_{2})=\Theta(ng_{2}(\log(ng_{2}))^{2})
is the recovery time defined in
5.11
.
In this analysis, we consider the following three phases (see
Fig.

10.1
):

•

[0,t_{0}]
: The process
\mathcal{P}
is an instance of the
g_{1}
-
Adv-Comp
setting with
g_{1}=n\log n
. Hence, by
4.3
, it follows that at step
t_{0}
w.h.p.
\Gap(t_{0})=\mathcal{O}(n\log^{2}n)
.

•

(t_{0},t_{1}]
: The process
\mathcal{P}
w.h.p. agrees with
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
which is a
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
process. We will use an analysis similar to that in
Section

4
to prove that w.h.p.
\Gap(t_{1})=\mathcal{O}(g_{2}\log(ng_{2}))
.

•

(t_{1},m]
: The process
\mathcal{P}
w.h.p. continues to agree with
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
which is a
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
process and so by
9.4
this implies that w.h.p.
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}\big(\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n\big)
.

g_{1}
-
Adv-Comp
Instance
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
Instance
0
t_{0}
t_{1}
m
\Gap(t)
Phase 1
Phase 2
Phase 3
n\log^{2}n
g_{2}\log(ng_{2})
\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n
t
\Gamma
drops in expectation
9.4
\mathbf{\ldots}

Figure 10.1
:
The three phases in the proof of
10.3
.

Phase 1
[0,t_{0}]
:
Using
4.3

(iii)
(for
g:=n\log n
) and for
c_{3}>0
being the constant defined in
Eq.

4.6
, we have that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-14},

(10.2)

using
c_{3}g\log(ng)\leqslant c_{3}n\cdot\log n\cdot\log(n^{2}\cdot\log n)\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n
.

Phase 2
(t_{0},t_{1}]
:
Let us now turn our attention to the interval
(t_{0},t_{1}]
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
is a
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
process. So, by
4.3

(i)
(for
g:=g_{2}
), there exists a constant
c_{1}\geqslant 1
, such that for any step
t\geqslant t_{0}

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\Gamma^{t}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\Big)+c_{1}.

At step
t_{0}
, when
\{\max_{i\in[n]}|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{0}}|\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n\}
holds, we also have that
\Gamma^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{3\gamma c_{3}n\log^{2}n}
. Hence, applying
A.11

(i)
(with
a=1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}
and
b=c_{1}
), for step
t_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t_{1}}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t_{1}}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Gamma^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{3\gamma c_{3}n\log^{2}n}\right.\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant\Gamma^{t_{0}}\cdot\Big(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\Big)^{t_{1}-t_{0}}+\frac{96c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}2n\cdot e^{3\gamma c_{3}n\log^{2}n}\cdot e^{-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\cdot\frac{1}{2}n^{3}}+\frac{96c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n.

using in
(a)
that
e^{u}\geqslant 1+u
and
t_{1}-t_{0}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}n^{3}
. By Markov’s inequality, we have that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t_{1}}\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n^{4}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

When the event
\left\{\Gamma^{t_{1}}\leqslant\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\cdot n^{4}\right\}
holds, it implies that

\displaystyle\Gap(t_{1})

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{100c_{1}}{\gamma}\right)+4\log n\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{c_{3}g_{2}}{16}\cdot\left(\mathcal{O}(1)+\log\left(\frac{c_{3}g_{2}}{16}\right)+4\log n\right)\leqslant c_{3}g_{2}\log(ng_{2}),

using in (a) that
c_{3}:=\frac{16}{\gamma g_{2}}

(defined in
Eq.

4.6
). Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g_{2}\log(ng_{2})\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant 3c_{3}n\log^{2}n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

(10.3)

Phase 3
(t_{1},m]
:
Now, we turn our attention to the steps in
(t_{1},m]
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
is again a
g_{2}
-
Adv-Comp
process.
Therefore, applying
9.4
(for
t_{0}:=t_{1}=m-n\log^{5}n-\Delta_{r}
and
g:=g_{2})
, there exists a constant
\widetilde{\kappa}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}}(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n\,\,\left|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{1}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widetilde{y}_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant c_{3}g_{2}\log(ng_{2})\right.\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-3}.

(10.4)

By combining
Eq.

10.2
,
Eq.

10.3
and
Eq.

10.4
, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}}(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant\big(1-n^{-14}\big)\cdot\big(1-n^{-3}\big)\cdot\big(1-n^{-3}\big)\geqslant 1-3n^{-3}.

Finally, by
Eq.

10.1
we have that w.h.p.
\mathcal{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{t_{0}}
agree in every step in
[t_{0},m]
, so by taking the union bound we conclude

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap_{\mathcal{P}}(m)\leqslant\widetilde{\kappa}\cdot\frac{g_{2}}{\log g_{2}}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-3n^{-3}-n^{-3}\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.\qed

11
Lower Bounds

In this section, we will state and prove lower bounds on the gap of various processes and settings. Recall that the
g
-Bounded
and
g
-Myopic-Comp
processes are specific instances of the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting. Our main result is for
g
-Myopic-Comp
, where we will prove a lower bound of
\Omega(g+\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n)
, which matches the upper bounds of Sections
5
-
9
for all
g\geqslant 0
(
11.4
).
Table

11.1
gives a summary of the results.

g
-Adv-Comp

Prop

g
-Myopic-Comp

Obs
11.6

Table 11.1
:
Overview of the lower bounds for different noise settings. All of these hold for a particular value of
m
with high probability.

We first start with a simple lower bound which follows immediately by majorization with the
Two-Choice
process without noise. This lower bound holds regardless of which strategy the adversary uses.

Observation 11.1
.

There is a constant
\kappa>0
such that for any
g\geqslant 0
and any instance of the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(n)\geqslant\log_{2}\log n-\kappa\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.

Proof.

For the
Two-Choice
process without noise, it was shown in
[
8
, Theorem 3.3]
that there is a constant
\kappa>0
such that
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(n)\geqslant\log_{2}\log n-\kappa\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}
.

At any step
t\geqslant 0
, probability allocation vector
p
of
Two-Choice
without noise is majorized the probability allocation vector
q^{t}
of any instance of
Two-Choice
in the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting, as
q^{t}
is formed by
p
and possibly reallocating some probability mass from light to heavy bins. Hence,
the lower bound follows by majorization (see
A.13
).
∎

We proceed by analyzing the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process by coupling its allocations with that of a
One-Choice
process.

Proposition 11.2
.

Consider the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process. Then,
(i)
for any
g\in[2,6\log n]
and for
m=\frac{1}{2}\cdot ng
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{35}\cdot g\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

Further,
(ii)
for any
g\geqslant 6\log n
and for
m=ng^{2}/(32\log n)
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{60}\cdot g\,\right]\geqslant 1-2n^{-2}.

Recall that for
g=\Omega(\operatorname{polylog}(n))
, our upper bound on the gap is

\mathcal{O}\left(g+\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\right)=\mathcal{O}(g).

This means that the lower bound in
11.2
is matching for those
g
. For smaller values of
g
, a stronger lower bound will be presented in
11.3
.

Proof.

First statement.
Consider
g
-Myopic-Comp
with
m=\frac{1}{2}\cdot ng
balls and define the stopping time
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant 0\colon\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{t}\geqslant g\}
.
Note that
\tau\leqslant m
implies there is a bin
j
with
x_{j}^{\tau}\geqslant g
, and hence

\Gap(m)\geqslant x_{j}^{m}-\frac{m}{n}\geqslant x_{j}^{\tau}-\frac{1}{2}g\geqslant g-\frac{1}{2}g>\frac{1}{35}g.

Let us now assume
\tau>m
. In that case, all bins have an absolute load in
[0,g]
and are therefore indistinguishable. Hence during steps
1,2,\ldots,m
, the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process behaves exactly like
One-Choice
.

By
A.9
(for
c:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{g}{\log n}\geqslant\frac{1}{\log n}
since
g\geqslant 2
) it holds for
One-Choice
that,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{10\sqrt{2}}\cdot\sqrt{g\cdot\log n}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2},

which, as
g\cdot\log n\geqslant\frac{1}{6}g^{2}
, implies that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{35}\cdot g\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

Second statement.
We will first prove for the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process that w.h.p. for any step
1\leqslant t\leqslant m
and any bin
i\in[n]
, it holds that
|y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant\frac{g}{2}
. To this end, fix any bin
i\in[n]
and define for any
1\leqslant t\leqslant m
,
Z^{t}=Z^{t}(i):=\sum_{j=1}^{t}(Y_{j}-\frac{1}{n})
, where the
Y_{j}
’s are independent Bernoulli variables with parameter
1/n
each. Clearly,
Z^{t}
forms a martingale, and
\mathbf{E}\left[\,Z^{t}\,\right]=0
for all
1\leqslant t\leqslant m
. Further,
let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant 1\colon|Z^{t}|>\frac{g}{2}\}
. Then
Z^{t\wedge\tau}
is also martingale. Further,
we have

\mathbf{Var}\left[\,Z^{t+1}\,\mid\,Z^{t}\,\right]=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\leqslant\frac{1}{n}=:\sigma^{2}.

Also
|Z^{t+1}-Z^{t}|\leqslant 1=:M
. Hence by a martingale inequality
A.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left|Z^{m\wedge\tau}\right|\geqslant\lambda\,\right]\leqslant 2\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma^{2}+M\lambda/3)}\right),

with
\lambda=\frac{g}{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,|Z^{m\wedge\tau}|\geqslant\frac{g}{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant 2\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{8(m\cdot\frac{1}{n}+\frac{g}{6})}\right)=2\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{g^{2}}{8\cdot(\frac{g^{2}}{32\log n}+\frac{g}{6})}\right)\leqslant 2n^{-3},

where the last inequality holds since

8\cdot\left(\frac{g^{2}}{32\log n}+\frac{g}{6}\right)\leqslant 8\cdot\left(\frac{g^{2}}{32\log n}+\frac{g^{2}}{32\log n}\right)=\frac{g^{2}}{2\log n},

using that
g\geqslant 6\log n
.

If the event
|Z^{m\wedge\tau}|\leqslant\frac{g}{2}
(or equivalently
\tau>m
) occurs, then this implies that bin
i
deviates from the average load by at most
\frac{g}{2}
in all steps
1,2,\ldots,m
. By the union bound, this holds with probability
1-2n^{-2}
for all
n
bins. Consequently, the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process behaves exactly like
One-Choice
until time
m
with probability
1-2n^{-2}
.

By
A.9
(for
c:=\frac{g^{2}}{32\log^{2}n}
) it holds for
One-Choice
that,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{g}{10\cdot\sqrt{32}}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

The claim follows by taking the union bound and using that
\frac{g}{10\cdot\sqrt{32}}\geqslant\frac{g}{60}
.
∎

Theorem 11.3
.

Consider the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process for any
g\in[10,\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}]
. Then, there exists
\ell:=\ell(g,n)
(defined in
Eq.

11.1
), such that for
m=n\cdot\ell
, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-\omega(1)}.

The proof of this lower bound is similar to the method used by Azar, Broder, Karlin and Upfal
[
8
]
to prove the
\Omega(\log\log n)
lower bound for
Two-Choice
without noise, in the sense that it follows a layered induction approach, but some additional care is needed. For example, the induction step
size in the load is
g
and not
1
, and the outcome of a load comparison depends on the load difference.

Proof.

In the proof we will divide the allocation of
m=n\cdot\ell
balls into
\ell
consecutive phases, each of which lasts for
n
steps, where

\displaystyle\ell:=\left\lfloor\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log g)}{\log g}\right\rfloor.

(11.1)

First, we verify that
\ell\geqslant 1
,

\displaystyle\ell=\left\lfloor\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log g)}{\log g}\right\rfloor\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\left\lfloor\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log g)}{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log n)}\right\rfloor\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\left\lfloor\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log n)}{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log n)}\right\rfloor=1,

where
(a)
used that
g\leqslant\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log\log n
and
(b)
that
g\leqslant\log n
.

Next we define for any
k=1,2,\ldots,\ell
the following event:

\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\biggl\{\Bigl|\bigl\{i\in[n]\colon x_{i}^{k\cdot n}\geqslant k\cdot g\bigr\}\Bigr|\geqslant n\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\biggr\}.

The main goal of this proof is to show that
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
occurs with high probability. Assuming for the moment that
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
indeed occurs, let us verify that the lower bound on the gap follows.
First, note that
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
implies the existence of a bin
i\in[n]
with
x_{i}^{m}\geqslant\ell\cdot g
and thus
\Gap(m)\geqslant x_{i}^{m}-\frac{m}{n}\geqslant\ell\cdot g-\ell=\ell\cdot(g-1)
, since by the choice of
\ell=\left\lfloor\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log g)}{\log g}\right\rfloor
,

\displaystyle g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}g^{-4g^{\ell}}\geqslant g^{-\frac{1}{2}\log n/\log g}=n^{-1/2},

(11.2)

where in
(a)
we used that
for any
g\geqslant 2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}\leqslant 2\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}g^{j}=2\cdot\frac{g^{\ell+1}-1}{g-1}\leqslant 2\cdot\frac{g^{\ell+1}}{\frac{1}{2}g}=4g^{\ell}.

(11.3)

Thus
n\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\geqslant 1
. Secondly, we verify that
\ell\cdot(g-1)=\Omega\big(\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\big)
,

\displaystyle\ell\cdot(g-1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\log(\frac{1}{8}\log n/\log g)}{\log g}\cdot g

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\log(1/8)+\log\log n-\log\log g}{\log g}\cdot g

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{\log\log n}{\log g}\cdot g,

(11.4)

where
(a)
holds since
\lfloor u\rfloor\geqslant u/2
for
u\geqslant 1
(as
\ell\geqslant 1
) and

g-1\geqslant g/2
, since
g\geqslant 2
, and
(b)
holds since for sufficiently large
n
,
(1/4)\cdot\log\log n\geqslant-\log(1/8)
and
(1/4)\cdot\log\log n\geqslant\log\log g
as
g\leqslant\log n
.

In order to establish that
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
occurs w.h.p., we will proceed by induction and prove that for any
k\geqslant 1
, with
\varepsilon_{k}:=n^{-\omega(1)}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\Omega
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}_{k}\,\mid\,\mathcal{E}_{k-1}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\varepsilon_{k}.

Induction Base (
k=1
).
Here we consider the allocation of the first
n
balls into
n
bins. We are interested in the number of bins which reach load level
g
during that phase. Note that as long as the loads of both sampled bins are smaller than
g
, the allocation follows that of
One-Choice
; if one of the bins has a load which is already larger than
g
, then the load difference may force the process to place a ball in the lighter of the two bins. In the following, we will (pessimistically) assume that the allocation of
all

n
balls follows
One-Choice
.

Instead of the original
One-Choice
process which produces a load vector
(x_{i}^{n})_{i\in[n]}
,
we consider the Poisson Approximation (
A.3
) and analyze the load vector
(\widetilde{x}_{i}^{n})_{i\in[n]}
, where the
\widetilde{x}_{i}^{n}
,
i\in[n]
are independent Poisson random variables with mean
n/n=1
. Clearly, for any
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i}^{n}\geqslant g\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i}^{n}=g\,\right]=e^{-1}\cdot\frac{1^{g}}{g!}\geqslant 2\cdot g^{-g},

using in the last inequality Stirling’s approximation (e.g.
[
43
, Lemma 5.8]
) and
g\geqslant 5
,

g!\leqslant e\cdot g\cdot\left(\frac{g}{e}\right)^{g}=\frac{e^{-1}}{2}\cdot g^{g}\cdot\left(\frac{2e^{2}g}{e^{g}}\right)\leqslant\frac{e^{-1}}{2}\cdot g^{g}.

Let
Y:=\left|\left\{i\in[n]\colon\widetilde{x}_{i}^{n}\geqslant g\right\}\right|
. Then
\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\geqslant 2n\cdot g^{-g}
. By a standard Chernoff Bound,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Y\leqslant n\cdot g^{-g}\,\right]\leqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Y\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\,\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)},

where we have used
g^{-g}\geqslant g^{-4g^{\ell}}\geqslant n^{-1/2}
, due to
Eq.

11.2
. Hence by the Poisson Approximation (
A.3
),
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}_{1}\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot n^{-\omega(1)}=1-n^{-\omega(1)}
.

Induction Step (
k-1\longrightarrow k
,
k\geqslant 2
).
For the induction step, we analyze phase
k=2,\ldots,\ell
and we will lower bound
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}_{k}\,\mid\,\mathcal{E}_{k-1}\,\right]
. Assuming
\mathcal{E}_{k-1}
occurs, there are at least
n\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}}
bins whose load is at least
(k-1)\cdot g
at the beginning of phase
k
. Let us call such a set of bins
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
, which can be assumed to satisfy with equality:

\displaystyle|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|=n\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}}.

(11.5)

Next note that whenever we sample two bins
i_{1},i_{2}
from
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
, we allocate the ball to a random bin among
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
if both bins have not reached load level
k\cdot g
.
Therefore, in order to lower bound the number of bins in
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
which reach load level
k\cdot g
by the end of phase
k
, we may pessimistically assume that if two bins in
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
are sampled, the ball will be
always
placed in a randomly sampled bin. Note that the probability that a ball will be allocated into the set
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
is lower bounded by

\bigl(\frac{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}{n}\bigr)^{2}.

Let
Z
denote the number of balls allocated to
\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
in phase
k
. Then
\mathbf{E}\left[\,Z\,\right]\geqslant n\cdot\left(\frac{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}{n}\right)^{2}
. Using a Chernoff Bound for
\widetilde{m}:=\frac{2}{3}\cdot n\cdot\left(\frac{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}{n}\right)^{2}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z\leqslant\widetilde{m}\,\right]\leqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z\leqslant\frac{2}{3}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Z\,\right]\,\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{1}{18}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Z\,\right]\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)},

where we used that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,Z\,\right]\geqslant n\cdot g^{-2\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}n\cdot g^{-2g^{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}n\cdot n^{-1/2},

using in
(a)
that for any integer
g\geqslant 2
,

\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}\leqslant\frac{g^{k}-1}{g-1}\leqslant g^{k}
, and in

(b)
that
k\leqslant\ell
and the property of
\ell
in
Eq.

11.2
.

Conditioning on this event, in phase
k
we have a
One-Choice
process with
\widetilde{m}
balls into
\widetilde{n}:=\left|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}\right|
bins, which w.l.o.g. will be labeled
1,2,\ldots,\widetilde{n}
. Again, we apply the Poisson approximation and define
(\widetilde{x}^{\widetilde{m}})_{i\in[\widetilde{n}]}
as
\widetilde{n}
independent Poisson random variables with mean
\lambda
given by

\lambda:=\frac{\widetilde{m}}{\widetilde{n}}=\frac{\frac{2}{3}\cdot n\cdot\left(\frac{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}{n}\right)^{2}}{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}=\frac{2}{3}\cdot\frac{|\mathcal{B}_{k-1}|}{n}=\frac{2}{3}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}},

using
Eq.

11.5
.
With that, it follows for any bin
i\in\mathcal{B}_{k-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i}\geqslant g\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{x}_{i}=g\,\right]=e^{-\lambda}\cdot\frac{\lambda^{g}}{g!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}e^{-1}\cdot\frac{\bigl(\frac{2}{3}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}}\bigr)^{g}}{g!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}2\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}},

using in
(a)
that
\lambda\leqslant 1
and in
(b)
Stirling’s approximation (e.g.
[
43
, Lemma 5.8]
) and that
g\geqslant 10
,

g!\leqslant e\cdot g\cdot\left(\frac{g}{e}\right)^{g}=\frac{e^{-1}}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{g}\cdot g^{g}\cdot\left(2e^{2}g\cdot\left(\frac{3}{2e}\right)^{g}\right)\leqslant\frac{e^{-1}}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{g}\cdot g^{g}.

Let
Y:=\left|\left\{i\in[\widetilde{n}]\colon\widetilde{x}_{i}\geqslant g\right\}\right|
.
Then
\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\geqslant 2\widetilde{n}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}
.
Thus by a Chernoff Bound,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Y\leqslant\widetilde{n}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\,\right]\leqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Y\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\,\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{1}{8}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\right)\leqslant n^{-\omega(1)},

where we used that

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,Y\,\right]\geqslant 2\widetilde{n}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}

\displaystyle=2n\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}g^{j}}\cdot g^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\geqslant 2n\cdot g^{-2\sum_{j=1}^{k}g^{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:geo_series}})}}{{\geqslant}}2n\cdot g^{-4g^{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}2n^{1/2},

where in
(a)
we used
k\leqslant\ell
and
Eq.

11.2
. Thus by the union bound and Poisson Approximation,
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}_{k}\,\mid\,\mathcal{E}_{k-1}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-\omega(1)}-2\cdot n^{-\omega(1)}=1-n^{-\omega(1)}
, which completes the induction.

Finally, by a simple union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}_{\ell}\,\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant 1-\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\neg\mathcal{E}_{k}\,\mid\,\mathcal{E}_{k-1}\,\right]\geqslant 1-\ell\cdot n^{-\omega(1)}\geqslant 1-n^{-\omega(1)}.

As verified in
Eq.

11.4
,
\mathcal{E}_{\ell}
implies
\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{1}{8}\cdot\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n
, and therefore the proof is complete.
∎

Combining
11.1
,
11.2
and
11.3
, we get:

Corollary 11.4
.

Consider the
g
-Myopic-Comp
process for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, there exists an
m:=m(g)\geqslant 0
, such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)=\Omega\left(g+\frac{g}{\log g}\cdot\log\log n\right)\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.

We proceed with two lower bounds for the
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
process.

Proposition 11.5
.

Consider the
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
process with
\rho(\delta):=1-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\exp\bigl(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2}\bigr)
for some (not necessarily constant)
\sigma>0
.
Then,
(i)
for any
\sigma\geqslant 2\cdot(\log n)^{-1/3}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(n)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{1}{8}\cdot(\log n)^{1/3},\frac{1}{2}\sigma\cdot(\log n)^{1/3}\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-2n^{-1}.

Further,
(ii)
for any
\sigma\geqslant 32
, it holds for
m=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5}\cdot n
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5},\frac{1}{30}\sigma^{2/5}\cdot\sqrt{\log n}\right\}\,\right]\geqslant 1-2n^{-2}.

Proof.

First statement.
Let
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant 1\colon\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{t}\geqslant\sigma\cdot(\log n)^{1/3}\}
. If
\tau\leqslant n
, then

\Gap(n)\geqslant x_{i}^{n}-\frac{n}{n}\geqslant x_{i}^{\tau}-1\geqslant\sigma\cdot(\log n)^{1/3}-1\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sigma\cdot(\log n)^{1/3}.

Consider now the case
\tau>n
. For any step
t\leqslant\tau
, we will perform a “sub-sampling” of the correct comparison in (possibly) two stages as follows. Let
i_{1}=i_{1}^{t}
and
i_{2}=i_{2}^{t}
be the two sampled bins, and
\delta
be their load difference. Let
Z_{1}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1-\exp(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2}))
and
Z_{2}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1/2)
be two independent random variables, which can be thought as the outcome of two biased coin flips that will be used to determine whether the load comparison is correct. If
Z_{1}=1
, then the comparison is correct (regardless of what
Z_{2}
is). However, if
Z_{1}=0
, then the comparison is correct if and only if
Z_{2}=1
. Overall, the probability of a correct comparison (if
\delta>0
) is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z_{1}=1\,\right]+\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z_{1}=0\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,Z_{2}=1\,\mid\,Z_{1}=0\,\right]

\displaystyle=1-\exp\left(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2}\right)+\exp\left(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2}\right)\cdot\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{2}\exp\left(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2}\right).

Further, conditional on
Z_{1}=0
, the ball will be placed in a random bin among
\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
.
Hence as long as
t\leqslant\tau
, we can couple the allocation of each ball by
g
-Myopic-Comp
to an allocation by
One-Choice
with probability at least
\exp(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2})\geqslant\exp(-(\log n)^{2/3})
. Using a Chernoff bound, with probability
1-n^{-\omega(1)}
, we we can couple the allocation of at least
n/2\cdot\exp(-(\log n)^{2/3})
balls out of the first
n
balls with that of
One-Choice
. Consequently, using
A.8

the maximum load (and gap) is at least
\frac{1}{8}(\log n)^{1/3}
with probability at least
1-n^{-1}
. Combining the two cases we get the claim.

Second Statement.

Consider any
\sigma\geqslant 32
and define the stopping time
\tau:=\inf\{t\geqslant 1\colon\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{t}\geqslant\sigma^{4/5}\}
.
Let
m:=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5}\cdot n
.
If
\tau\leqslant m
, then there is a bin
i\in[n]
with
x_{i}^{\tau}\geqslant\sigma^{4/5}
, and

\Gap(m)\geqslant x_{i}^{m}-\frac{m}{n}\geqslant x_{i}^{\tau}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5}.

Otherwise, in each step until
m
, the load difference between any two sampled bins is at most
\sigma^{4/5}
, and therefore each ball is coupled with the allocation of a
One-Choice
process with probability at least
\exp\big(-(\sigma^{4/5}/\sigma)^{2}\big)\geqslant 1-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}.

Let
X
be the number of
One-Choice
allocations in the first
m
steps. Using the following standard Chernoff bound,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,X\geqslant(1-\delta)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,X\,\right]\,\right]\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}[\,X\,]},

with
\delta=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\leqslant 1
(since
\sigma\geqslant 1
) and
\mathbf{E}\left[\,X\,\right]\geqslant\left(1-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot m
, we get that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,X\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\left(1-2\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}\cdot n\,\right]\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{1}{2}\delta^{2}\cdot\mathbf{E}[\,X\,]}\geqslant 1-n^{-\omega(1)}.

Therefore, using
A.9
(for
c:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(1-2\cdot\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}\cdot\frac{1}{\log n}\geqslant\frac{1}{\log n}
), we get that

\displaystyle\Gap(m)

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{10}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}\cdot\log n}+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}-\frac{m}{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{10}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}\cdot\log n}+\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{4/5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{10}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2/5}}\right)\cdot\sigma^{4/5}\cdot\log n}-\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2/5}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\frac{1}{20}\sigma^{2/5}\cdot\sqrt{\log n}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\sigma^{2/5}

\displaystyle\geqslant\frac{1}{30}\sigma^{2/5}\cdot\sqrt{\log n},

using in
(a)
that
\sigma\geqslant 32
. So, combining the two cases we get the claim.
∎

Finally, we will prove a simple (but tight) lower bound for
b
-Batch
, which already holds in the first batch of
b
balls, by a
One-Choice
argument.

Observation 11.6
.

There exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that for the
b
-Batch
process for any
b\in[n\cdot e^{\log^{c}n},n\log n]
with
c>0
any constant, it holds that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(b)\geqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\left(\frac{4n}{b}\cdot\log n\right)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.

Proof.

During the allocation of the first batch consisting of
b
balls, the load information of all bins is never updated, i.e., their load “estimate” equals zero. Hence in these first
n
steps, as ties are broken randomly,
b
-Batch
behaves exactly like
One-Choice
. Hence, by
A.10
there exists a constant
\kappa>0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(b)\geqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\left(\frac{4n}{b}\cdot\log n\right)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.\qed

12
Experimental Results

In this section, we empirically analyze the gap for the
g
-Bounded
,
g
-Myopic-Comp
and
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
processes for various values of
g
,
\sigma
,
n
and
m
(
Fig.

12.1
and
Table

12.3
). For sufficiently large
g
, the gap plots for
g
-Bounded
and
g
-Myopic-Comp
are almost linear, as suggested by our upper bound of
\Theta(g)
for any
g=\log^{\Omega(1)}n
. We also present the empirical gap for
b
-Batch
for various values of batch size
b
around
n
and compare with the
One-Choice
gap for the case of
b
balls (
Fig.

12.2
and
Table

12.4
). As our analysis in
Section

10.2
suggests, these gaps are close when
b
is close to
n
and for smaller
b
, e.g., when
b=n^{1-\Omega(1)}
, the gaps of the two processes diverge (
10.6
).

:

g
-Bounded
and
g
-Myopic-Comp
with
g\in\{1,\ldots,20\}
and
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
with
\sigma\in\{1,\ldots,20\}
, for
n\in\{10^{4},5\cdot 10^{4},10^{5}\}
and
m=1000\cdot n
, over
100
runs.

, for

and

and for

for
b
balls, over

runs.
