13
Conclusions

In this work, we proposed a number of different noise settings, and analyzed the gap of the
Two-Choice
process. As our main result, we established for all
g\geqslant 1
the asymptotic value of
\Gap(\cdot)
in the adversarial setting
g
-Adv-Comp
. This shows that
\Gap(\cdot)
undergoes a delicate phase transition at the point
g=\log^{\Theta(1)}n
, around which the impact of
g
on the gap switches from superlinear to linear. Further, we presented tight bounds for the delay and batched settings for a range of batch sizes
b
and delay parameters
\tau
, where load information of the bins may be stale.
Finally, we also proposed a smoothed probabilistic setting where each load comparison between two bins
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
is correct with some probability
\rho(\delta)
, where
\delta=|x_{i_{1}}^{t}-x_{i_{2}}^{t}|
is their load difference.
Assuming that the load estimates of bins are independent Gaussian perturbations leads to
\rho(\delta)=1-\frac{1}{2}\exp(-(\delta/\sigma)^{2})
. For this choice of
\rho
, we proved that the gap is some function that is polynomial in
\sigma
and logarithmic in
n
.

There are several directions for future work. One interesting avenue is establishing tight(er) bounds for
\sigma
-Noisy-Load
or other choices of
\rho
, as well as determining tight(er) bounds for the remaining values of
b
and
\tau
in the delay settings. Also, it would be interesting to investigate the noisy setting for other balanced allocations processes, such as the
Mean-Thinning
or the
(1+\beta)
process.

Appendix A
Analysis Tools

A.1
Concentration Inequalities

We state the following well-known concentration inequalities.

Lemma A.1

(
Azuma’s Inequality for Super-Martingales
[
25
, Problem 6.5]
)
.

Consider a super-martingale
X^{0},\ldots,X^{N}
satisfying
|X^{i}-X^{i-1}|\leqslant a_{i}
for any
i\in[N]
, then for any
\lambda>0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,X^{N}\geqslant X^{0}+\lambda\,\right]\leqslant\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}^{2}}\right).

Lemma A.2

(
[
19
, Theorems 6.1 & 6.5]
)
.

Consider a martingale
X^{0},\ldots,X^{N}
with filtration
\mathfrak{F}^{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{F}^{N}
satisfying
|X^{i}-X^{i-1}|\leqslant M
and
\mathbf{Var}\left[\,X^{i}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{i-1}\,\right]\leqslant\sigma_{i}^{2}
for any
i\in[N]
, then for any
\lambda>0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left|X^{N}-\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{N}\,\right]\right|\geqslant\lambda\,\right]\leqslant 2\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2\cdot(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma_{i}^{2}+M\lambda/3)}\right).

A.2
Facts about
One-Choice

In this part, we collect several (mostly folklore) results for the maximum load and the gap of
One-Choice
. In contrast to e.g.,
[
47
]
, we are aiming to obtain bounds on the gap with probability at least
1-n^{-6}
and not just with probability at least
1-o(1)
, but which are not necessarily tight up to lower order terms. Furthermore, the bounds in
[
47
]
hold for any
m=\Omega\big(\frac{n}{\operatorname{polylog}(n)}\big)
, but we wish to also cover
m=n\cdot\exp\big(-\log^{\mathcal{O}(1)}n\big)
. We use these bounds to analyze the
b
-Batch
and
\tau
-Delay
settings for various values of
b
and
\tau
in
Section

10
.

We first restate the so-called Poisson Approximation method.

Lemma A.3

(
[
43
, Corollary 5.11]
)
.

Let
(x^{T})_{i\in[n]}
be the load vector after
T
steps of
One-Choice
. Further, let
(\widetilde{x}^{T})_{i\in[n]}
be
n
independent Poisson random variables with parameter
\lambda=T/n
each. Further, let
\mathcal{E}
be any event which is determined by
x^{T}
, and further assume that
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}\,\right]
is either monotonically increasing in
T
or monotonically decreasing in
T
. Further, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
be the corresponding event determined by
\widetilde{x}^{T}
. Then,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\mathcal{E}\,\right]\leqslant 2\cdot\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\,\right].

Lemma A.4
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process, for any
\alpha>0
, any bin
i\in[n]
, and any step
m\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m}}\,]\leqslant e^{\frac{m}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}.

Proof.

We will proceed inductively to show that
\mathbf{E}[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m}}\,]\leqslant e^{\frac{m}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}
. The base case follows since
\mathbf{E}[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{0}}\,]=1\leqslant 1
. For
m\geqslant 1
, let
Z_{i}^{m}
indicate whether the
m
-th ball was allocated to bin
i\in[n]
and assume that
\mathbf{E}[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m-1}}\,]\leqslant e^{\frac{m-1}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}
holds, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m}}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha(Z_{i}^{m}+x_{i}^{m-1})}\,\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha Z_{i}^{m}}\,\right]\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m-1}}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{1}{n}\cdot e^{\alpha}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot e^{0}\Big)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m-1}}\,\right]=\Big(1+\frac{1}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)\Big)\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m-1}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{\frac{1}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m-1}}\,\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{\frac{1}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}\cdot e^{\frac{m-1}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}=e^{\frac{m}{n}\cdot(e^{\alpha}-1)}.

using in
(a)
that
1+u\leqslant e^{u}
(for any
u
).
∎

We now proceed to obtain an upper bound for the maximum load of
One-Choice
for any
m\leqslant 2n\log n
. As we will show in
A.8
, this bound is asymptotically tight.

Lemma A.5

(
cf.
[
2
, Lemma 14]
)
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process for any
m\leqslant 2n\log n
. Then,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{m}\leqslant 11\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

Proof.

Using
A.4
, for the given
m
and
\alpha=\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)>0
, we have that for any bin
i\in[n]
,

\mathbf{E}[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m}}\,]\leqslant e^{\frac{m}{n}\cdot e^{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}}=n^{4}.

Hence, by Markov’s inequality,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,e^{\alpha x_{i}^{m}}\leqslant n^{11}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-7}.

When this event holds, we have

x_{i}^{m}\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\log(n^{11})\leqslant 11\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}.

By taking the union bound over all bins
i\in[n]
, we get the claim.
∎

For
m=\mathcal{O}(n)
, this recovers the well-known
\mathcal{O}(\frac{\log n}{\log\log n})
bound.

Corollary A.6

(
cf.
[
43
, Chapter 5]
)
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process for
m=2n
. Then,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{m}\leqslant 11\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log\log n}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

For
m=\mathcal{O}(n^{1-\varepsilon})
for some constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, this shows that
\Gap(m)=\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Corollary A.7
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process with
m=2n^{1-\varepsilon}
for any constant
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. Then,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{m}\leqslant\frac{11}{\varepsilon}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-6}.

In the following lemma, we prove that the
One-Choice
bound obtained in
A.5
is asymptotically tight.

Lemma A.8

(
cf. Lemma 14 in
[
2
]
)
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process with
m\leqslant n\log n
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\max_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{m}\geqslant\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.

Proof.

We will bound the probability of event
\mathcal{E}
, that the maximum load is less than
M=\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}
. Clearly, the probability of the event
\mathcal{E}
is monotonically increasing
in the number of balls (while keeping
M
fixed).

Following
A.3
, it suffices to bound the probability of the event
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}
which is that the maximum value of
n
independent Poisson random variables with parameter
\lambda=\frac{m}{n}
is less than
M
.
We want to show that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}\,\right]\leqslant\left(1-\frac{e^{-\frac{m}{n}}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{M}}{M!}\right)^{n}\leqslant\exp{\left(-n\cdot\frac{e^{-m/n}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{M}}{M!}\right)}<n^{-1}.

This is equivalent to showing that

\displaystyle-n\cdot\frac{e^{-m/n}\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)^{M}}{M!}<-\log{n}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\log{n}-\frac{m}{n}+M\cdot\log\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)-\log{M!}>\log\log n

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\log{n}+M\cdot\log\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)>\frac{m}{n}+\log{M!}+\log\log n.

Using
\log M!\leqslant M\cdot(\log M-1)+\log M
(e.g., in
[
28
, Equation 9.1]
), we deduce that

\displaystyle\frac{m}{n}+M\cdot\left(\log M-1-\log\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\right)+\log M+\log\log n

\displaystyle\qquad<\frac{3}{4}\cdot\log n+\frac{\log n}{4\cdot\log(\frac{4n\log n}{m})}\cdot\left(\log\log n-\log\left(4\cdot\log\left(\frac{4n\log n}{m}\right)\right)-\log\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{3}{4}\cdot\log n+\frac{\log n}{4\cdot\log(\frac{4n\log n}{m})}\cdot\left(\log\left(\frac{4n\log n}{m}\right)-\log\left(16\cdot\log\left(\frac{4n\log n}{m}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\leqslant\frac{3}{4}\cdot\log n+\frac{\log n}{4\cdot\log(\frac{4n\log n}{m})}\cdot\log\left(\frac{4n\log n}{m}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{3}{4}\cdot\log n+\frac{1}{4}\cdot\log n=\log n,

for sufficiently large
n
and using that
\log\big(\frac{4n\log n}{m}\big)>0
as
m\leqslant n\log n
. Hence, we get the desired lower bound.
∎

The next standard result was also used in
[
46
, Section 4]
and is based on
[
47
]
.

Lemma A.9

(
cf.
[
37
, Lemma A.2]
)
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process for
m=cn\log n
where
c\geqslant 1/\log n
.
Then,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\frac{\sqrt{c}}{10}\cdot\log n\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-2}.

Combining the previous two lemmas, we also get the asymptotically tight bound on the gap

Lemma A.10

(
cf.
[
2
, Lemma 14]
)
.

Consider the
One-Choice
process with
m\leqslant n\log n
. Then, there exists a constant
\kappa>0
, such that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Gap(m)\geqslant\kappa\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-1}.

Proof.

For sufficiently small constant
C\in(0,1)
, for any
m\leqslant Cn\log n
we have that

\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\log n}{\log(\frac{4n}{m}\cdot\log n)}\geqslant 2\cdot\frac{m}{n},

and hence the conclusion follows by
A.8
for
\kappa=\frac{1}{8}
. For
m>Cn\log n
, the stated bound follows from
A.9
.
∎

A.3
Auxiliary Probabilistic Claims

For convenience, we state and prove the following well-known result.

Lemma A.11
.

Consider any sequence of random variables
(X^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
for which there exist
0<a<1
and
b>0
, such that every
i\geqslant 1
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\mid X^{i-1}\,\right]\leqslant X^{i-1}\cdot a+b.

Then,
(i)
for every
i\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\mid X^{0}\,\right]\leqslant X^{0}\cdot a^{i}+\frac{b}{1-a}.

Further,
(ii)
if
X^{0}\leqslant\frac{b}{1-a}
holds, then for every
i\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\,\right]\leqslant\frac{b}{1-a}.

Proof.

First statement.
We will prove by induction that for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\mid X^{0}\,\right]\leqslant X^{0}\cdot a^{i}+b\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a^{j}.

For
i=0
,
\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{0}\mid X^{0}\,\right]\leqslant X^{0}
. Assuming the induction hypothesis holds for some
i\geqslant 0
, then since
a>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i+1}\mid X^{0}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i+1}\mid X^{i}\,\right]\mid X^{0}\,\right]\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\mid X^{0}\,\right]\cdot a+b

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(X^{0}\cdot a^{i}+b\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}a^{j}\right)\cdot a+b

\displaystyle=X^{0}\cdot a^{i+1}+b\cdot\sum_{j=0}^{i}a^{j}.

The claims follows using that
\sum_{j=0}^{i}a^{j}\leqslant\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a^{j}=\frac{1}{1-a}
, for any
a\in(0,1)
.

Second statement.
We will prove this claim by induction. Then, assuming that
\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\,\right]\leqslant\frac{b}{1-a}
holds for
i\geqslant 0
, we have for
i+1

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i+1}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}\left[\,\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i+1}\mid X^{i}\,\right]\,\right]\leqslant\mathbf{E}\left[\,X^{i}\,\right]\cdot a+b\leqslant\frac{b}{1-a}\cdot a+b=\frac{b}{1-a}.\qed

A.4
Majorization

We begin with the following well-known majorization inequality which appears in Theorem 1
[
39
, Chapter XII]
. For a proof see e.g.,
[
38
, Lemma A.2]
.

Lemma A.12

(
cf.
[
38
, Lemma A.2]
)
.

Let
(a_{k})_{k=1}^{n},(b_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
be non-negative and
(c_{k})_{k=1}^{n}
be non-negative and non-increasing. If
\sum_{k=1}^{i}a_{k}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{i}b_{k}
holds for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
then,

\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_{k}\cdot c_{k}\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{k}\cdot c_{k}.

Next we state an auxiliary result, which is implicit in
[
46
]
. Theorem 3.1 in
[
46
]
proves the required majorization for time-independent probability allocation vectors, but as mentioned in the proof of Theorem 3.2 in
[
46
]
, the same result generalizes to time-dependent probability allocation vectors.

Lemma A.13

(
cf.
[
46
, Section 3]
)
.

Consider two allocation processes
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{P}
. The allocation process
\mathcal{Q}
uses at each step a fixed probability allocation vector
q
. The allocation process
\mathcal{P}
uses a time-dependent probability allocation vector
p^{t}
, which may depend on
\mathfrak{F}^{t}
but majorizes
q
at each step
t\geqslant 0
. Let
y^{t}(\mathcal{Q})
and
y^{t}(\mathcal{P})
be the two normalized load vectors, sorted non-increasingly. Then there is a coupling such that for all steps
t\geqslant 0
,
y^{t}(\mathcal{P})
majorizes
y^{t}(\mathcal{Q})
, so in particular,
y_{1}^{t}(\mathcal{P})\geqslant y_{1}^{t}(\mathcal{Q})
.

Appendix B
Omitted Proofs and Auxiliary Claims

B.1
Omitted Proofs from Section
4

Corollary B.1

(
Restated, page
4.5
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
. Then, for any steps
t_{0}\geqslant 0
and
t_{1}\geqslant t_{0}
, we have that

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant 2g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{14}.

Proof.

We will be using the hyperbolic cosine potential
\Gamma:=\Gamma(\gamma)
with smoothing parameter
\gamma:=-\log(1-\frac{1}{8\cdot 48})/g
as we did in the proof of
4.3
.

Consider an arbitrary step
t_{0}
with
\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}
. Then, it follows that

\Gamma^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}.

By
4.3

(i)
, there exists a constant
c_{1}\geqslant 1
such that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t}\,\right]\leqslant\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\right)+c_{1},

and using
A.11

(i)
(for
a:=1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}
and
b:=c_{1}
) at step
t_{1}\geqslant t_{0}
, we have that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\Gamma^{t_{1}}\,\left|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Gamma^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\right.\,\right]\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{96n}\right)^{t_{1}-t_{0}}+\frac{96c_{1}n}{\gamma}\leqslant 4n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}},

recalling that
\gamma=\Theta\big(\frac{1}{g}\big)
. Hence, by Markov’s inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t_{1}}\leqslant 4n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\cdot(ng)^{14}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\Gamma^{t_{1}}\leqslant 4n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\cdot(ng)^{14}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Gamma^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-14}.

When the event
\big\{\Gamma^{t_{1}}\leqslant 4n\cdot e^{\gamma g(\log(ng))^{2}}\cdot(ng)^{14}\big\}
holds, then it also follows that

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{1}}\right|\leqslant\frac{\log\Gamma^{t_{1}}}{\gamma}\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}+\frac{1}{\gamma}\cdot\left(\log(4n)+14\log(ng)\right)\leqslant 2g(\log(ng))^{2},

for sufficiently large
n
and using that
\gamma=\Theta\big(\frac{1}{g}\big)
. Hence, we get the conclusion.
∎

B.2
Omitted Proofs from Section
5

Lemma B.2

(
Restated, page
5.3
)
.

Consider any allocation process with probability allocation vector
r^{t}
at step
t\geqslant 0
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
,
(i)
it holds that

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1,

and
(ii)
it holds that

\left|\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\right|\leqslant 4\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t}\right|+2.

Proof.

First statement.
For any bin
i\in[n]
, its expected contribution to
\Upsilon^{t+1}
is given by,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Upsilon_{i}^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\Big(y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\cdot r_{i}^{t}+\Big(y_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\cdot(1-r_{i}^{t})

\displaystyle=(y_{i}^{t})^{2}+2\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}\cdot r_{i}^{t}-2\cdot\frac{1}{n}\cdot y_{i}^{t}\cdot(1-r_{i}^{t})+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\cdot r_{i}^{t}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot(1-r_{i}^{t})

\displaystyle=(y_{i}^{t})^{2}+2\cdot\Big(r_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\cdot r_{i}^{t}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot(1-r_{i}^{t}).

Hence, by aggregating over all bins we get,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\Upsilon^{t+1}\,\right|\,y^{t}\,\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[(y_{i}^{t})^{2}+2\cdot\Big(r_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\cdot r_{i}^{t}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot(1-r_{i}^{t})\right]

\displaystyle=\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot\Big(r_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n^{2}}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot\Big(r_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle=\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{2}{n}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Upsilon^{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}2\cdot r_{i}^{t}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+1,

using in
(a)
that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i}^{t}=0
.
Therefore, by subtracting
\Upsilon^{t}
, statement
(i)
follows.

Second statement.
Let
M:=\max_{i\in[n]}|y_{i}^{t}|
.
We will upper bound the change
\Delta\Upsilon_{i}^{t+1}
for an arbitrary bin
i\in[n]
, by considering the following two cases:

Case 1:
Ball at step
t+1
is allocated to bin
i
. So,

\left|\Delta\Upsilon_{i}^{t+1}\right|=\Big|\Big(y_{i}^{t}+1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}-(y_{i}^{t})^{2}\Big|=\Big|2\cdot\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\Big(1-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}\Big|\leqslant 2M+1.

Case 2:
Ball at step
t+1
is not allocated to bin
i
. So,

\left|\Delta\Upsilon_{i}^{t+1}\right|=\left|\Big(y_{i}^{t}-\frac{1}{n}\Big)^{2}-(y_{i}^{t})^{2}\right|=\left|-\frac{2}{n}\cdot y_{i}^{t}+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\right|\leqslant\frac{2M}{n}+\frac{1}{n}.

Aggregating over all bins
i\in[n]
yields

\displaystyle\left|\Delta\Upsilon^{t+1}\right|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\Delta\Upsilon_{i}^{t+1}\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant 2M+1+(n-1)\cdot\left(\frac{2M}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\right)\leqslant 4M+2.\qed

Lemma B.3

(
Restated, page
5.10
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the sequence
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}:=(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}(\alpha,c_{4}g,\varepsilon)
for any starting step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
, any
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{18}]
and
\varepsilon,c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. Then, for any step
s\geqslant t_{0}
,

\mathbf{E}\left[\,\left.\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}\,\right|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,\right]\leqslant\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s}.

Proof.

Recalling the definition of
\widetilde{\Lambda}
in
Eq.

5.6
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s+1}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,]\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,]\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{s}}-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\neg\mathcal{G}^{s}}\right)\cdot\exp\Big(-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big)\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{s-1}\Big).

Thus, to prove the statement, it suffices to show that

\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s}\,]\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{s}}-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\neg\mathcal{G}^{s}}\right)\leqslant\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}.

(B.1)

To show
Eq.

B.1
, we consider two cases based on whether
\mathcal{G}^{s}
holds.

Case 1 [
\mathcal{G}^{s}
holds]:

Recall that when
\mathcal{G}^{s}
holds, then
\Delta^{s}\leqslant Dng
. Further, when
\Lambda^{s}\leqslant cn
holds (for
c>0
the constant in
5.9
), then
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}=0
. Thus, using
5.9

(ii)
,

\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{G}^{s}\,]\leqslant\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\right)\leqslant\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\right).

Hence, since in this case
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{s}}=1
, the left hand side of
Eq.

B.1
is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\mathcal{G}^{s}\,]\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\right)\right)\cdot\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\right)=\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}.

Case 2 [
\mathcal{G}^{s}
does not hold]:
By
5.8

(ii)
, we get

\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\neg\mathcal{G}^{s}\,]\leqslant\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\left(1+\frac{3\alpha}{n}\right)\leqslant\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\right).

Hence, since in this case
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{s}}=0
, the left hand side of
Eq.

B.1
is equal to

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}[\,\Lambda^{s+1}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s}}\,\big|\,\mathfrak{F}^{s},\neg\mathcal{G}^{s}\,]\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\right)\right)\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{3\alpha}{n}\right)=\Lambda^{s}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{s-1}}.

Since
Eq.

B.1
holds in either case, we deduce that
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{s})_{s\geqslant t_{0}}
forms a super-martingale.∎

Lemma B.4

(
Restated, page
5.7
)
.

Consider the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
for any constant
\alpha\in(0,1)
, any
g\geqslant 1
and any constant
c_{4}>0
. Then
(i)
for any constant
\hat{c}>0
, there exists a constant
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},\hat{c})\geqslant 1
, such that for any step
t\geqslant 0
with
\Lambda^{t}\leqslant\hat{c}\cdot n
,

\Upsilon^{t}\leqslant c_{s}\cdot ng^{2}.

Furthermore,
(ii)
there exists a constant
c_{r}:=c_{r}(\alpha,c_{4})\geqslant 1
, such that for any step
t\geqslant 0
,

\Upsilon^{t}\leqslant c_{r}\cdot n\cdot\Big(g^{2}+(\log\Lambda^{t})^{2}\Big).

Proof.

First statement.
We begin by proving some basic inequalities between exponential, quadratic and linear terms. Let
\hat{u}:=(4/\alpha)\cdot\log(4/\alpha)
. Note that
e^{u}\geqslant u
(for any
u\geqslant 0
) and hence for any
u\geqslant\hat{u}
,

\displaystyle e^{\alpha u/2}=e^{\alpha u/4}\cdot e^{\alpha u/4}\geqslant\frac{\alpha u}{4}\cdot e^{\alpha\hat{u}/4}=\frac{\alpha u}{4}\cdot\frac{4}{\alpha}=u,

and
e^{\alpha u}=e^{\alpha u/2}\cdot e^{\alpha u/2}\geqslant u\cdot u=u^{2}
. Therefore, for every
u\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle u^{2}\leqslant\max\left\{\hat{u}^{2},e^{\alpha u}\right\}.

(B.2)

Recall that for any bin
i\in[n]
,
\Lambda_{i}^{t}:=e^{\alpha\cdot(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}+e^{\alpha\cdot(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\left((y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}\right)^{2}+\left((-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}\right)^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}\max\left\{2\hat{u}^{2},\Lambda_{i}^{t}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}\max\left\{2\hat{u}^{2}\cdot\Lambda_{i}^{t},\Lambda_{i}^{t}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}2\hat{u}^{2}\cdot\Lambda_{i}^{t},

(B.3)

where in
(a)
we used
Eq.

B.2
first with
u=(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}
and then with
u=(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}
, in
(b)
that
\Lambda_{i}^{t}\geqslant 1
for any
i\in[n]
and in
(c)
that
\hat{u}\geqslant 1
, since
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

We now proceed to upper bound the quadratic potential,

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{t}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big[\left((y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}+c_{4}g\right)^{2}+\left((-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}+c_{4}g\right)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}2\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big[\left((y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}\right)^{2}+\left((-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}\right)^{2}+2\cdot(c_{4}g)^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\!\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\text{\ref{eq:quadratic_potential_term_bound}})}}{{\leqslant}}4\hat{u}^{2}\cdot\Lambda^{t}+4c_{4}^{2}\cdot ng^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}4\hat{c}\hat{u}^{2}\cdot n+4c_{4}^{2}\cdot ng^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant(4\hat{c}\hat{u}^{2}+4c_{4}^{2})\cdot ng^{2},

using in
(a)
that
(a+b)^{2}\leqslant 2\cdot(a^{2}+b^{2})
(for any
a,b
) and in
(b)
that
\Lambda^{t}\leqslant\hat{c}\cdot n
. Therefore, for the constant
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},\hat{c}):=4\hat{c}\hat{u}^{2}+4c_{4}^{2}
, we get the first statement.

Second statement.
For any bin
i\in[n]
we have,

|y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant c_{4}g+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\Lambda^{t}.

Hence, using that
(a+b)^{2}\leqslant 2\cdot(a^{2}+b^{2})
,

(y_{i}^{t})^{2}\leqslant\Big(c_{4}g+\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\Lambda^{t}\Big)^{2}\leqslant 2\cdot\Big(c_{4}^{2}g^{2}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\cdot(\log\Lambda^{t})^{2}\Big)\leqslant c_{r}\cdot\Big(g^{2}+(\log\Lambda^{t})^{2}\Big),

for some constant
c_{r}:=c_{r}(\alpha,c_{4})=\max\left\{2c_{4}^{2},\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\right\}\geqslant 1
. By aggregating the contributions over all bins, we get the second statement.
∎

Lemma B.5

(
Restated, page
5.12
)
.

Consider the
g
-Adv-Comp
setting for any
g\geqslant 1
and the potential
\Lambda:=\Lambda(\alpha,c_{4}g)
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{18}
, and
c_{4}>0
as defined in
5.9
. Further, let the constants
c,\varepsilon>0
be as defined in
5.9
,
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},2c)\geqslant 1
as in
5.7

(i)
and
r\in(0,1)
as in
5.6
. Then, for
\Delta_{s}:=\Delta_{s}(g):=\frac{60c_{s}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}
, we have that for any step
t_{0}\geqslant 0
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{s}]}\left\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\right\}~\right|~\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.

Proof.

By
5.7

(i)
,
\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn
implies that deterministically
\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant c_{s}ng^{2}
for constant
c_{s}:=c_{s}(\alpha,c_{4},2c)\geqslant 1
and

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant c_{4}g+\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\log(2cn)\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2},

for sufficiently large
n
using that
c_{4},\alpha,c>0
are constants. Let
t_{1}:=t_{0}+\Delta_{s}
. By
5.6
with
T:=c_{s}ng\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}\geqslant c_{s}ng^{2}
(and
o(n^{2}g^{3})
) and
\hat{c}:=\frac{\Delta_{s}\cdot g}{T}=\frac{60}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\geqslant 1
as
\alpha,\varepsilon,r\leqslant 1
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{s}\;\Big|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{s}\;\Big|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Upsilon^{t_{0}}\leqslant T,\max_{i\in[n]}\left|y_{i}^{t_{0}}\right|\leqslant g(\log(ng))^{2}\,\right]

\displaystyle\quad\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}.

(B.4)

By
5.10
,
(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t})_{t\geqslant t_{0}}
is a super-martingale, so
\mathbf{E}[\,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,]\leqslant\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{0}}=\Lambda^{t_{0}}
. Hence, using Markov’s inequality we get
\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}>\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\mid\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]\leqslant(ng)^{-12}
. Thus, by the definition of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
in
Eq.

5.6
, we have

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\Lambda^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\right)\,\,\Bigg|\,\,\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-12}.

(B.5)

Further, if in addition to the two events
\{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\}
and
\{\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\}
, also the event
\{G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{s}\}
holds, then

\displaystyle\Lambda^{t_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot B_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2cn\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot(1-r)\cdot\Delta_{s}-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot r\cdot\Delta_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}2cn\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\Delta_{s}\bigg)

\displaystyle=2cn\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\bigg(-\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}\cdot\frac{60c_{s}}{\alpha\varepsilon r}\cdot n\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}\bigg)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leqslant}}2cn\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(-30\cdot\max\{\log n,g\}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 2cn\cdot(ng)^{12}\cdot\exp\left(-15\cdot\log(ng)\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant 1,

where we used in
(a)
that
r=\frac{6}{6+\varepsilon}
implies
\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot(1-r)=\frac{3\alpha}{n}\cdot\frac{\varepsilon}{6+\varepsilon}=\frac{\alpha\varepsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{r}{2}
and in
(b)
that
c_{s}\geqslant 1
and
\alpha\leqslant 1/2
.
Also
\Lambda^{t_{1}}\geqslant 2n
holds deterministically, so we can deduce from the above inequality that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}}=0
, that is,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\neg\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\;\Bigg|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\;\;\;\widetilde{\Lambda}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\leqslant\Lambda^{t_{0}}\cdot(ng)^{12},\;\;\;\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn,\;\;\;G_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}\geqslant r\cdot\Delta_{s}\,\right]=1.

Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}-1}:=\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}-1]}\{\Lambda^{t}>cn\}
, and taking the union bound over
Eq.

B.4
and
Eq.

B.5
yields

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\bigcup_{t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\Delta_{s}]}\{\Lambda^{t}\leqslant cn\}\;\bigg|\;\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},\Lambda^{t_{0}}\leqslant 2cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-2\cdot(ng)^{-12}-(ng)^{-12}\geqslant 1-(ng)^{-11}.\qed

B.3
Omitted Proofs from Section
6

Claim B.6
.

Consider
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}>0
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
and
Eq.

6.3
respectively. Then, for any
g\geqslant\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}
and
k:=k(g)\geqslant 2
being the unique integer such that
(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/k}<g\leqslant(\alpha_{1}\log n)^{1/(k-1)}
, it holds that

(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n))^{1/k}\leqslant\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\cdot(\log n)\right)^{1/(k-1)}.

Proof.

Let
R:=\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}=\frac{\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}{4\cdot 84}\leqslant 1
(using that
\alpha_{2}=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{84}
and
\alpha_{1}\leqslant 1
). By rearranging the target inequality,

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\cdot(\log n)\right)^{1/(k-1)}\cdot(\alpha_{1}\cdot(\log n))^{-1/k}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{k-1}\cdot\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4}\log n\right)-\frac{1}{k}\cdot\log(\alpha_{1}\log n)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geqslant}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{k-1}\cdot\left(\log R+\frac{1}{k}\cdot\log\log n\right)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geqslant}}\exp\left(\frac{1}{k-1}\cdot\left(\log R-\log R\right)\right)=1.

using in
(a)
that
-\frac{1}{k}\log\alpha_{1}\geqslant-\frac{1}{2(k-1)}\log\alpha_{1}
and in
(b)
that
k\leqslant\frac{1}{-\log R}\cdot\log\log n
since
g\geqslant R^{-1}
.
∎

B.4
Omitted Proofs from Section
7

Next, we proceed with a simple smoothness argument for the potential
V
defined in
Eq.

7.2
.

Lemma B.7

(
Restated, page
7.2
)
.

Consider the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha_{1}>0
, any
c_{4}>0
and any
g\geqslant 1
. Then,
(i)
for any step
t\geqslant 0
, we have that

e^{-\alpha_{1}}\cdot V^{t}\leqslant V^{t+1}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V^{t}.

Further,
(ii)
for any
\hat{c}>0
, for any integer
T>0
and any step
t\geqslant 0
, for which there exist steps
s_{0}\in[t-T,t]
and
s_{1}\in[t,t+T]
, such that
V^{s_{0}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
and
V^{s_{1}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
, we have that

V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot 2\hat{c}n.

Proof.

First statement.
In each step the normalized load of any bin can change by at most
1
, i.e.,
|y_{i}^{t+1}-y_{i}^{t}|\leqslant 1
and so
e^{-\alpha_{1}}\cdot V_{i}^{t}\leqslant V_{i}^{t+1}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V_{i}^{t}
. By aggregating over all bins, we get the claim.

Second statement.
For any bin
i\in[n]
, in
T
steps the normalized load can decrease by at most
T/n
, i.e.,
y_{i}^{s_{1}}\geqslant y_{i}^{t}-\frac{T}{n}
. So, the overload term is bounded by

e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{s_{1}}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot V_{i}^{s_{1}}.

Similarly,
y_{i}^{t}\geqslant y_{i}^{s_{0}}-\frac{T}{n}
, and so the underload term is bounded by

e^{\alpha_{1}(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot e^{\alpha_{1}(-y_{i}^{s_{0}}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot V_{i}^{s_{0}}.

Hence, by aggregating over all bins and using the preconditions
V^{s_{0}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
and
V^{s_{1}}\leqslant\hat{c}n
,

V^{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}+e^{\alpha_{1}(-y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}\right]\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(V_{i}^{s_{1}}+V_{i}^{s_{0}}\right)=e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot\left(V^{s_{1}}+V^{s_{0}}\right)\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\frac{T}{n}}\cdot 2\hat{c}n.\qed

The following lemma shows that by choosing a large enough offset
c_{5}>0
in the potential
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
(defined in
Eq.

6.8
), when
V^{t}=e^{\mathcal{O}(g)}\cdot cn
, then
\Psi_{0}^{t}=\mathcal{O}(n)
.

Lemma B.8

(
Restated, page
7.3
)
.

Consider any
c,\hat{c}>0
and the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
for any
\alpha_{1}>0
, any
c_{4}>0
and any
g\geqslant 1
. Further, consider the potential
\Psi_{0}:=\Psi_{0}(\alpha_{1},c_{5}g)
with offset
c_{5}:=2\cdot\max\{c_{4},\hat{c}\}
and
C:=2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot c+1
. Then, for any step
t\geqslant 0
with

V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\hat{c}\cdot g}\cdot 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
, it holds that

\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant Cn.

Proof.

We start by upper bounding
\Psi_{0}^{t}
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{5}g)^{+}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}\geqslant c_{5}g}e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{5}g)}+\sum_{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}<c_{5}g}e^{0}

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-\alpha_{1}(c_{5}/2)g}\sum_{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}\geqslant c_{5}g}e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-(c_{5}/2)g)}+n

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leqslant}}e^{-\alpha_{1}(c_{5}/2)g}\sum_{i\in[n]\colon y_{i}^{t}\geqslant c_{4}g}e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)}+n

\displaystyle\leqslant e^{-\alpha_{1}(c_{5}/2)g}\sum_{i=1}^{n}e^{\alpha_{1}(y_{i}^{t}-c_{4}g)^{+}}+n

\displaystyle=e^{-\alpha_{1}(c_{5}/2)g}\cdot V^{t}+n,

where in
(a)
we used that
c_{5}/2\geqslant c_{4}
.
Now, using that
V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\hat{c}\cdot g}\cdot 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
and
c_{5}\geqslant 2\cdot\hat{c}
, we conclude

\Psi_{0}^{t}\leqslant e^{-\alpha_{1}(c_{5}/2)g}\cdot V^{t}+n\leqslant 2e^{2\alpha_{1}}\cdot cn+n=Cn.\qed

Lemma B.9

(
Restated, page
7.6
)
.

Consider the
\mathcal{Q}_{g,r_{0}}
process for any
g\in[1,c_{6}\log n]
for
c_{6}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.1
and any step
r_{0}\geqslant 0
. Then, for the potential
V:=V(\alpha_{1},c_{4}g)
with
\alpha_{1}
as defined in
Eq.

6.2
,
c_{4},c>0
as defined in
5.9
and
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}>0
as defined in
Eq.

7.6
, it holds that for any step
t_{0}\geqslant r_{0}
and
t_{1}
such that
t_{0}<t_{1}\leqslant t_{0}+2n\log^{5}n
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcap_{t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]}\bigcup_{s\in[t,t+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]}\left\{V^{s}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-9},

Proof.

Analogously to the proof of
5.13
, we begin by defining the event

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}=\left\{\text{for all }t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\text{ there exists }s\in[t,t+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]\text{ such that }V^{s}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn\right\},

that is, if
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds then we have that
V^{s}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
at least once every
\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}
steps and so the claim follows.

Note that if for some step
j_{1}
we have that
V^{j_{1}}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn
and for some
j_{2}\geqslant j_{1}
that
V^{j_{2}}>e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn
, then there must exist
j\in(j_{1},j_{2})
such that
V^{j}\in(e^{\alpha_{1}}cn,e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn]
, since for every
t\geqslant 0
it holds that
V^{t+1}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}\cdot V^{t}
(
7.2

(i)
).
Let
t_{0}<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots
and
t_{0}=:s_{0}<s_{1}<\cdots
be two interlaced sequences defined recursively for
i\geqslant 1
by

\tau_{i}:=\inf\{\tau>s_{i-1}:V^{\tau}\in(e^{\alpha_{1}}cn,e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn]\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{i}:=\inf\{s>\tau_{i}:V^{s}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn\}.

Thus we have

t_{0}=s_{0}<\tau_{1}<s_{1}<\tau_{2}<s_{2}<\cdots,

and since
\tau_{i}>\tau_{i-1}
we have
\tau_{t_{1}-t_{0}}\geqslant t_{1}-t_{0}
. Therefore, if the event
\cap_{i=1}^{t_{1}-t_{0}}\{s_{i}-\tau_{i}\leqslant\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\}
holds, then also
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}
holds.

Recall that by the strong stabilization (
7.5
) we have for any
i=1,2,\ldots,t_{1}-t_{0}
and any
\tau=t_{0}+1,\ldots,t_{1}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\bigcup_{t\in[\tau_{i},\tau_{i}+\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}]}\left\{V^{t}\leqslant e^{\alpha_{1}}cn\right\}~\right|~\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{\tau},\;e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{\tau}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn,\tau_{i}=\tau\,\right]\geqslant 1-n^{-11},

and by negating and the definition of
s_{i}
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,s_{i}-\tau_{i}>\widetilde{\Delta}_{s}\,\,\left|\,\,\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{\tau},e^{\alpha_{1}}cn<V^{\tau}\leqslant e^{2\alpha_{1}}cn,\tau_{i}=\tau\right.\,\right]\leqslant n^{-11}.

Since the above bound holds for any
i\geqslant 1
and
\mathfrak{F}^{\tau}
, with
\tau_{i}=\tau
, it follows by the union bound over all
i=1,2,\ldots,t_{1}-t_{0}
, as
t_{1}-t_{0}\leqslant 2n\log^{5}n
,

\mathbf{Pr}\left[\,\left.\neg\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\,\,\right|\,\,\mathcal{Z}^{r_{0}},\mathfrak{F}^{t_{0}},V^{t_{0}}\leqslant cn\,\right]\leqslant(t_{1}-t_{0})\cdot n^{-11}\leqslant n^{-9}.\qed
